LETTER OF TRANSMITTAL THE SENATE SACRAMENTO, CALIFORNIA, March, 1953 HON. GOODWIN J. KNIGHT, President, and Members of the Senate: 

GENTLEMEN: Your Senate Interim Judiciary Committee, created by 

Senate Resolution No. 200 (Senate Journal, June 23, 1951, page 3967), presents herewith a second progress report of its activities and results of its investigations, together with its recommendations. 

Respectfully submitted,

BURT W. BUSCH GERALD J. O'GARA EARL D. DESMOND JACK B. TENNEY A. W. WAY 

(7)

SENATE RESOLUTION No. 200 RESOLVED BY THE SENATE OF THE STATE OF CALIFORNIA, As follows: 

1. The Senate Interim Judiciary Committee is hereby created and authorized and directed to ascertain, study and analyze all facts relating to the judicial system of the State, and the administration of justice therein, including but not limited to: (a) The composition, structure, jurisdiction and administration of the several courts; (b) The method of selection, compensation and retirement of judicial officers and attaches; (c) The procedure, process, and rules of the courts, statutory and otherwise; (d) All provisions of the law relating to civil actions and remedies with particular attention to the doctrine of comparative negligence; (e) All laws relating to publication of notices and service of process; (f) The plan to reorganize the inferior courts of this State, which was approved by the people of the State at the last general election ; (g) All laws relating to divorce, alimony, and domestic relations; (h) The study of the recommendations of the Senate Interim Committee on Judiciary in its 1951 Report to the Legislature; including but not limited to the operation, effect, administration, enforcement and needed revision of any and all laws in any way bearing upon or relating to the subject of this resolution, and to report thereon to the Senate, including in the reports its recommendations for appropriate legislation. 

2. The committee shall consist of five Members of the Senate appointed by the Committee on Rules thereof. Vacancies occurring in the membership of the committee shall be filled by the appointing power. 

3. The committee is authorized to act during this session of the Legislature, including any recess, and after final adjournment until the commencement of the 1953 Regular Session, with authority to file its final report not later than the fifteenth day after the end of the constitutional recess. The time for filing the final report shall not be extended. 

4. The committee and its members shall have and exercise all of the rights, duties and powers conferred upon investigating committees and their members by the provisions of the Joint Rules of the Senate and Assembly and of the Standing Rules of the Senate as they are adopted and amended from time to time at this session, which provisions are incorporated herein and made applicable to this committee and its members. 

5. The committee has the following additional powers and duties: (a) To select a chairman and vice chairman from its membership. (b) To contract with such other agencies, public or private, as it deems necessary for the rendition and affording of such services, (8)

facilities, studies and reports to the committee as will best assist it to carry out the purposes for which it is created. (c) To cooperate with and secure the cooperation of county, city, city and county, and other local law enforcement agencies in investigating any matter within the scope of this resolution and to direct the sheriff of any county to serve subpenas, orders and other process issued by the committee. (d) To report its findings and recommendations to the Legislature and to the people from time to time and at any time, not later than herein provided. (e) To do any and all other things necessary or convenient to enable it fully and adequately to exercise its powers, perform its duties, and accomplish the objects and purposes of this resolution. 

6. The sum of eight thousand five hundred dollars ($8,500) or so much thereof as may be necessary is hereby made available from the Contingent Fund of the Senate for the expenses of the committee and its members and for any charges, expenses or claims it may incur under this resolution, to be paid from the said contingent fund and disbursed, after certification by the chairman of the committee, upon warrants drawn by the State Controller upon the State Treasurer.

INTRODUCTION AND ACKNOWLEDGMENT 

As has been indicated in previous reports, the Senate Interim Judiciary Committee is particularly concerned with reviewing, "screening" and analyzing legislation of the type normally referred to the Senate Standing Judiciary Committee. This type of legislation being extremely technical in character and vitally affecting the welfare of every citizen in the State, it was deemed advisable to have as much preliminary investigation as possible where there was indication that amendments or additions were to be presented. However, in addition to the work set forth above, the committee engaged in several special investigations on matters of major importance, and the results of such investigations are set forth in this report.

The committee held many hearings on the various matters with which it was concerned and wishes at this time to gratefully acknowledge the assistance given to it by many distinguished members of the bench, bar and California Law School faculties, as well as other civic-minded citizens from all walks of life.

Among the distinguished persons whose views were sought or who appeared as witnesses before the committee at its various hearings are the following:

BAR EXAMINATIONS OF OCTOBER, 1951, TO OCTOBER, 1952 I. OCTOBER, 1951, EXAMINATION 

In its progress report submitted to the Legislature in March, 1952, this committee discussed the results of the October, 1951 bar examination. 

The report included a resolution adopted by this committee on February 2, 1952, requesting the State Supreme Court to review the examination, and requesting the court and the Committee of Bar Examiners to establish certain rules for future examinations and to give consideration and study to a number of suggestions which had been made to the committee.

Subsequently, eight students who had been unsuccessful in the October, 1951, bar examination filed petitions for writs of review, asking the Supreme Court to review the decision of the Committee of Bar Examiners. That decision had refused to recommend the admission of those students to practice law. 

The State Supreme Court, by a 5-2 majority made an order denying the writs of review. The order was not amplified by a majority opinion. 

However, two members of the court, Justice Jesse W. Carter and 

Justice B. Rey Schauer, filed a dissent and set forth their reasons for dissenting in the following opinion: "I dissent from the orders denying writs of review in the above entitled proceedings and desire to state my views in relation thereto. 

"The above named petitioners, having taken the Bar examination given by the Committee of Bar Examiners of the State Bar of California in October, 1951, seek to have this Court review the decision of said Committee. The decision which petitioners seek to have reviewed refused to recommend to this Court their admission to practice law in this state. As grounds for such review, petitioners urge that the Committee of Bar Examiners acted arbitrarily, capriciously and unreasonably in conducting said bar examination and in the grading, and re-grading, of the examination papers of each of said petitioners. "From the record before us in these proceedings and published statements by members of the Committee of Bar Examiners in the January-February, 1952, Journal of the State Bar of California, it appears that 1041 applicants took the October, 1951, bar examination given by the Committee of Bar Examiners of the State Bar of California. Of this number, on the first reading of the examination papers, 15.4 per cent received an average grade of 70 per cent or better. This means that only 160 applicants cut of the total number of 1041 taking the examination passed on the first reading. Thereafter, in accordance with past practice, papers which received grades ranging from 65 per cent to 69.9 per cent were reappraised. On ( 13 )

November 23, the Committee of Bar Examiners directed the reappraisement of papers with scores below 65 per cent and papers with grades ranging downward to 63.75 per cent were reviewed. No paper with a score below 64.2 per cent was found to be of passing quality. 

Three hundred and twenty-six papers in all were reappraised by three members of The Board of Reappraisers, Miller, Jonas and Aldwell. This resulted in 231 more applicants passing the examination. In other words, all but 94 of the papers reappraised were given passing grades. In the group at or above 65 per cent, 47 were refused passing grades while 13 of the group below 65 per cent (which are not normally in the reappraisal group) were passed. Every applicant who received an original grade above 67 per cent, with the exception of one who received a grade of 67.1 per cent, was passed on reappraisal. These figures show that before reappraisal, the passing percentage was 15.4 per cent; on reappraisal, 22.2 per cent more passed, making a total percentage passing the examination of 37.6 per cent. (Note that almost half again as many passed on reappraisal as on the first reading.) "After the result of the examination was announced, an Interim Committee of the State Senate conducted an investigation into the examination, and on February 2, 1952, said Committee adopted the following resolution which was submitted to this Court: 

RESOLUTION (Adopted by the Senate Interim Judiciary Committee, February 2, 1952.) 

"WHEREAS, 1041 law students took the California bar examination in October, 1951, and only 391 passed. This is 37.5%, the lowest since October, 1946 which was 37.1%. It is 13% below the 50.9% average for fall examinations in the five-year period, 1946-50. ""WHEREAS, This Committee has carefully investigated the reason for this low percentage and has found the following facts and reached the following conclusions: "1. There is no suggestion of dishonest, favoritism, intentional severity or carelessness in the conduct of the examination. ""2. The fact that only 160 or 15% of those taking the examination attained the passing grade of 70% on first reading was due to two major factors: '(a) Unusual difficulty in at least three of the questions. (b) Strictness of grading. 66 6 66 6 66 3. After the original grading, three reappraisers reviewed 326 papers graded between 63.75 and 69.9%. They passed 231 students, 59% of all the successful candidates. This is a radical departure from the original purpose of reappraisement which was to remedy inequities in a relatively few borderline cases. "'4. The process of reappraisement in this examination was critized for these reasons. 66 6 '(a) Reappraisement is not authorized, prescribed, or governed by the Business and Professions Code or the rules governing the State Bar. It is a procedure for which the Committee of Bar Examiners makes its own rules and standards from year to year. “(b) The standard used on reappraisement is not fixed, definite or certain. 

It is subject to the broad discretion of each reappraiser in individual cases. 66 "(c) Each of the three reappraisers was competent and conscientious. However, each had his own temperament, viewpoint and subjective standards. One was relatively strict, one less strict, and one liberal in passing papers. Since in most cases the reappraisers acted independently, this lack of uniformity may have worked substantially to the advantage of certain students and the disadvantage of others. In addition, it appears that certain students whose original grades were between 1560 and 1610, or 65% and 67.1%, and whose papers were reviewed and failed on or before November 25, 1951, may have been reappraised more

strictly than students whose papers were reviewed and passed on and after 

November 26, 1951, the date when a lower minimum review grade of 63.75% was established. 66 "(d) Many of the persons adversely affected by the decision of the reappraisers are veterans of World War II who sacrificed several years of their normal scholastic life in the service of their country. ""Now THEREFORE BE IT RESOLVED that this Committee requests the Supreme 

Court of California to : "(a) Determine, after investigation and hearing, whether the 47 students whose original marks were between 65 and 67.1% and who were failed by the 

Board of Reappraisers should not be admitted to the bar without further examination. "(b) Review the papers of students receiving original marks between 63.75 and 65% to determine if the procedures which were followed with regard to this group resulted in substantial injustice to any of the 48 applicants who were failed. 66 6 (c) Make such further inquiry concerning the papers between 60 and 63.75% as will satisfy the Court that no students in that category should have been reappraised and passed. And be it ""FURTHER RESOLVED that the Court and the Committee of Bar Examiners are requested to establish the following rules for future student bar examinations: "(a) Require submission of the authors' analyses of all questions used, after the bar examination but prior to the grading of the examination, to all of the law schools in California for evaluation and criticism. "(b) Require the giving of a reasonable number of alternative questions in each examination. And be it ""FURTHER RESOLVED that the Court and the Committee of Bar Examiners are requested to give consideration and study to the following suggestions which have been made to this committee: 66 6 (a) Credit each applicant who has failed to obtain a passing average with each subject in which he has received a passing grade. Limit re-examinations to subjects in which an unsuccessful applicant has failed. “(b) Appoint a full-time Committee of Bar Examiners to serve under the direction of the Supreme Court of California, and to be responsible only to such Court. (c) Establish a base, perhaps at a level 5% below the average percentage of success in the bar examinations of the preceding 5 years, above which there must be no failures. "'(d) Whenever an average grade of all applicants who have answered a particular question is 10% or more below the average of the grades for all of the other questions, the percentage of difference between the two shall be credited to each answer to the question; i.e., if the average grade of all other questions is 

50% and the average for a particular question is 40%, then 10% shall be added to each grade previously awarded for the particular question. 66 "(e) Establish a policy, implemented by rules, which will make standards of questions, grading, and reappraisal more uniform from year to year. "(f) Encourage examinations which most of the qualified applicants can pass without selective reappraisement. "(g) Establish more uniform standards and better coordinated procedures on reappraisement. And be it ""FURTHER RESOLVED that a copy of this resolution be sent to the Supreme Court of California, the State Bar of California, and the Committee of Bar Examiners.' "At the hearings before the Senate Interim Judiciary Committee, the following matters were brought to light: That of the 24 questions submitted by the Committee of Bar Examiners, 16.6 per cent of the total number of examinees received passing grades (70 per cent) on question No. 5; 21.8 per cent received passing grades on question No. 10; 18.1 per cent received passing grades on question No. 12; 20.2 per cent received passing grades on question No. 

20; 20.4 per cent received passing grades on question No. 24. In the entire examination, no applicant received a higher grade than 80.8

per cent. The average grades of all the answers to five of twentyfour questions were as low as 45.3 per cent on question No. 10; 46.5 per cent on question No. 24; 52.3 per cent on question No. 12; 54.8 per cent on question No. 20 and 55.2 per cent on question No. 5. "In the January-February, 1952, issue of the Journal of The State Bar of California (page 47), it appears that 488 graduates of American Bar Association approved California law schools took the 

October, 1951, bar examination for the first time. Of the 488, 288 passed the examination. There were 64 graduates of out-of-state 

American Bar Association approved law schools who took the same examination and of these, 21 passed. This makes a total of 552 

American Bar Association approved law school graduates who took the examination with 56 per cent, or 309, receiving a passing grade.

So far as individual law schools are concerned, Harvard passed 50 per cent, Michigan passed 50 per cent, Hastings College of the Law passed 44.1 per cent, Yale passed 40 per cent, and the University of San Francisco passed 38 per cent. "C 'Noteworthy, by way of contrast, is the fact that six states in 

1950 had passing percentages of 90 or better: Kansas, 99 per cent; 

Nebraska, 99 per cent; Iowa, 96 per cent; Oklahoma, 93 per cent; 

Wyoming, 92 per cent; Indiana, 90 per cent. Each of these states, however, has adopted the American Bar Association standards, or their substantial equivalent, which means that a more highly select group of applicants is being examined than in California which has had practically no selective educational requirements. Thus, Kansas, which requires a four-year college degree plus graduation from an accredited law school, would be expected to show, and does show, a very high percentage of success, namely 99 per cent.' (See 

'The California Bar Examination: Its Comparative Standing' by Sheldon D. Elliott, Dean of the School of Law, University of South ern California, in the January-February, 1952, issue of The Journal of the State Bar of California.) The figures just quoted by me belie the above quoted statement because Harvard, Michigan, Hastings, Yale and the University of San Francisco are all American Bar Association approved law schools. It is also a complete refutation of the argument that there is a drop in the quality of the applicants who took the October, 1951 bar examination (see 'The October 

1951 California Bar Examination' by Graham L. Sterling, Jr., of the Los Angeles Bar, Member of the Committee of Bar Examiners; 

Journal of the State Bar of California, January-February, 1952, issue). "Petitioners here contend that the reappraisement by a single reappraiser is a deviation from the customary practice when the reappraisers acted as a board; that one reappraiser, Miller, who graded approximately half the papers, had a more severe standard than either Aldwell or Jonas; that the reappraisers applied their own individual standards in grading the papers which resulted in discrimination against those whose papers were reappraised by 

Miller and Jonas. The transcript of the Senate Interim Judiciary 

Committee shows that the reappraising was done on a personal basis; that the reappraisers themselves admitted it would have been 'better' had two or more reappraisers read each paper.

"The Recorder (a newspaper published in San Francisco and accepted as the official organ of the courts) for Friday, May 2, 1952, quoted Goscoe Farley, Secretary of the Committee of State Bar 

Examiners, as follows: "The difficulty with the October 1951 bar examination arose from the fact that inadvertently three or four questions out of the twenty-four contained problems that had not been adequately covered at most law schools. The low grades on these three or four questions caused many otherwise fairly good. papers to fall within the reappraisal group.' (Emphasis added.) 

It seems obvious that if the examination contained four questions which had not been covered at 'most' law schools, and which, consequently, the applicants could not answer, it would be necessary for them to receive not less than 85% on every other question. 

When one takes into consideration that in this examination no applicant received a higher grade than 80.8%, the examination was manifestly unfair. It also appears to me that when the future of more than a thousand applicants who have spent three years of time, money, and labor in the study of law is at stake, that there should be no room for such ‘inadvertance' on the part of the Committee of Bar Examiners. 66 Quoting again from Mr. Farley's statement as reported in The 

Recorder: 'In order to help avoid such situations in the future, the 

Committee of Bar Examiners has reinstated the former practice of giving optional questions. At all but one of the six sessions of the 

April 1952 bar examination, for example, candidates were permitted to answer any four questions out of five. This use of optional questions will make is less likely that a student will perforce have to answer a question with unfamiliar subject content. "Another important improvement in this aspect of the examination will take place as (a) result of a survey just completed by a committee of the State Bar. Each California law school plus three of the larger eastern schools was requested to furnish the name of the case and text book used for the course in each bar examination subject. A total of 129 books were reported used. The school was then further requested to state precisely what portions of the books used by it were not covered. A report was then prepared showing precisely which topics in the subject were covered and which were not covered at each school. This report will serve as a guide to the 

Committee of Bar Examiners in the future in deciding what topics should not be included in the examination. * * * 666 'Another recent change which will be put into effect with this April bar examination will be to have each paper in the reappraisal group reviewed by two appraisers. The second reappraiser will not know whether the first reappraiser passed or failed the paper. 

If the two reappraisers do not agree on the result, the paper will go to a third reappraiser who will cast the deciding vote. This will stop the complaint that the three reappraisers do not each apply the same standards to their review of the papers.' (Emphasis added.) "It would appear from Mr. Farley's statement to the press that he has, by implication at least, admitted all of the charges raised by petitioners here. He has admitted the examination was unfair in that 'three or four' questions unfamiliar to the applicants were

'inadvertently' used; that there is a need to ascertain what subjects are being taught in California law schools and three of the larger 

Eastern schools; and that a 'new' system of reappraisement of borderline papers is necessary. "This court, although it has not in the past chosen to exercise it, has plenary authority with respect to admissions to practice law in this state. Because of the constitutional provision providing for a tripartite form of government and the separation of those three departments into the judiciary, legislative and executive branches, the Legislature may regulate and control the subject of admissions to practice law only so far as this court, by a vote of a majority of its members, chooses to permit it to do so. There can not be the slightest doubt that the State Bar of California is an arm of the judiciary, and that this court, if it chose to do so, as I believe it should, could take over the entire field of admissions to practice law and prescribe the rules and regulations therefor. (Preston v. 

State Bar, 28 Cal. 2d 643; Brydonjack v. State Bar, 208 Cal. 439; 

Vaughan v. State Bar, 208 Cal. 740; Fish v. State Bar, 214 Cal. 215;

In re Lacey, 11 Cal. 2d 699.) In addition to the inherent power of this court over admissions to practice law, section 6066 of the Business and Professions Code provides: Any person refused certification to the Supreme Court for admission to practice may have the action of the board, or of any committee authorized by the board to make a determination on its behalf, pursuant to the provisions of this chapter, reviewed by the Supreme Court, in accordance with the procedure prescribed by the court." "In view of the rules of law above stated and the foregoing facts, I am convinced that petitioners herein have made out a prima facie case for a review by this court of the October, 1951 bar examination and that the petitions should be granted and a complete record of all proceedings before the State Bar relative to said examination certified to this court for such determination as may be warranted. 

While the granting of these petitions may place a heavy burden on this court because of the effort required for a full review of the proceedings involved in said examination, I am convinced that the matter is of such great importance to the public, the applicants and the law schools in this state and elsewhere as to justify the undertaking. "In refusing to take this action, I feel that this court is being remiss in the exercise of its power over admissions to practice law in this state and fails to respond to an opportunity to render an outstanding public service.

I CONCUR: 

SCHAUER, J."

CARTER, J. 

A somewhat more favorable view of the October, 1951, bar examination was taken by the Subcommittee on Audit of the October, 1951, bar examination. That subcommittee consisted of Honorable Roger J. Traynor, Justice of the Supreme Court of the State of California, Chairman; and A. Russell Berti, Assistant Dean of the University of San Francisco

Law School. The substance of that report, without the charts referred to in it, follows:

FINAL REPORT OF SUBCOMMITTEE ON AUDIT OF OCTOBER, 1951, BAR EXAMINATION To: The Committee for Cooperation Between the 

Law Schools in California and the State Bar This is the second and final report on this subject. A preliminary report was filed with you on June 27, 1952. At that time no grades had been received on 3 of the 24 questions, and grades on 2 other questions were lacking also as to 62 papers. The grading is now complete. One hundred books on each of the 24 questions were read and graded by professors on the faculties of the following California law schools: University of California (Berkeley), University of California (Los Angeles), Golden Gate, Hastings, Loyola, McGeorge, Santa Clara, San 

Francisco Law School, Southwestern, Stanford, University of San Francisco and 

University of Southern California.

The completed statistical data, in the form of three charts, are transmitted with this report. Some observations on these data will be of interest. 

1. The auditors on an over-all average raised the bar examiners' readers' grades 2 percent; the highest average grades were raised 2.1 percent (77.7 percent to 79.8 percent), and the lowest average grades were raised 2.5 percent (45.8 percent to 48.3 percent). (Chart III.) 

2. The bar examiners' readers (without reappraisal) passed 18 of the 100 papers and upon reappraisal 38 out of the 100 papers were passed. By comparison, the auditors passed only 4 more candidates than the bar examiners' readers, and 16 fewer candidates than the bar examiners' reappraisers. (Chart III.) 

3. There was only one instance where the auditors passed a man whom the bar examiners had failed. (Chart III, applicant No. 17.) Three candidates whose grades the auditors increased above the 70 percent passing mark had been passed by the bar examiners on reappraisal. (Chart III, applicants Nos. 61, 66, 93.) There were four instances of candidates passed on reappraisal by the 

Committee of Bar Examiners who were given lower grades by the auditors than by the bar examiners' readers. (Chart III, applicants Nos. 19, 94, 97, 98.) 

4. Substantial increases were made by the auditors in the gradings of Questions 3, 4, 7, 9, 12 and 22 over the grades given by the bar examiners' readers, but the auditors lowered substantially the grades on Questions 6, 11, 13 and 17 below those given by the bar examiners' readers. (Chart II.) 

From the statistical data, it does not appear that the over-all results of the October, 1951, bar examination would have been changed materially had the law school professors conducted the grading of the examination questions rather than the bar examiners' readers and the reappraisers. The percentage increase in average grades made by the professors was 2 percent. Moreover, there was but 

1 applicant out of 100 who would have been passed by the professors, whom the bar examiners failed.

The members of the subcommittee wish to express their sincere appreciation to the auditing professors for the invaluable assistance which they have given in this audit. 

Respectfully submitted,

Dated: November 21, 1952 A. RUSSELL BERTI ROGER J. TRAYNOR, Chairman II. PROCEDURE CHANGES IN 1952 

Following the October, 1951, bar examination, a new Committee of

Bar Examiners, including several members of the previous committee, took office.

That committee made two major changes in the bar examinations of April and October, 1952, as compared to October, 1951. These changes were: 

1. Inclusion of optional questions. In the October, 1951, examination, there were no optional questions.

2. Reappraisal of all papers subject to reappraisal by two reappraisers. 

In previous examinations, including the October, 1951, examination, as a rule only one reappraiser reappraised a paper. Under the new procedure, if the two reappraisers agreed, their decision was final. If they disagreed, a third reappraiser read the paper and made the final decision. 

In addition there has been one other improvement in bar examination procedures which should be noted. It will be recalled that there were several questions in the October, 1951, bar examination that gave many of the applicants difficulty because of unfamiliar subject content. The 

Committee for Cooperation Between the Law Schools in California and the State Bar undertook a thorough study of the course content of bar examination subjects as taught at the various law schools throughout the Nation. (A detailed report of this study is one file at the Office of the 

Committee of Bar Examiners of the State of California.) It was therefrom discovered that the coverage varies greatly from one school to another. By having accurate information of what is taught in the various schools, the Committee of Bar Examiners is now able to avoid questions involving principles of law not taught at a substantial number of schools. 

This committee is gratified that the Committee of Bar Examiners adopted its recommendation with regard to alternative questions and that it has acted upon the committee's suggestion that more uniform standards and better coordinated procedures should be established for reappraisal.

III. 1952 EXAMINATIONS CONTRASTED WITH OCTOBER, 1951 

The changes made by the Committee of Bar Examiners resulted in a sharp increase in the percentage of successful applicants for admission to the bar. As compared to 37.5 percent passing in October, 1951, 61.2 percent passed in April, 1952 and 53.2 percent in October, 1952. More important than the number of students passing the examinations were the comparative statistics of the three examinations: October, 1951, April, 1952, and October, 1952.

These statistics show, the committee believes, that the examinations given in April and October, 1952, provided a fairer test of the legal knowledge of those who took the examination than did the October, 1951, examination. 

The highlights of those statistics are: 

1. The percentage attaining 70 percent on first reading increased from 

15 percent in October, 1951, to 41.9 percent in April, 1952, and 37.1 percent in October, 1952. These figures show either that the questions were easier or the grading more lenient, or both, in 1952 than in October, 1951. In any case, results definitely show a trend to judge the students more by their first reading marks than by their good or poor fortune on reappraisal. 

2. The lowest average on any question in October, 1951, was 45.3 percent, as compared to 57.9 percent in April, 1952, and 54.5 percent in 

October, 1952. The highest grade average on any question in each of the respective examinations was: 73.9 percent, 74.9 percent, 73.9 percent. The spread between high and low as therefore reduced from 28.6 percent in October, 1951, to 17 percent in April, 1952, and 19.4 percent

21 in October, 1952. This reduction in spread the committeee considers wholesome.

IV. COMMENDATION OF PROGRESS 

This committee wishes to commend the Committee of Bar Examiners and its secretary, Mr. Goscoe O. Farley, for its scrupulously fair and complete cooperation with this committee in furnishing it with all information and statistics concerning the bar examinations. 

This committee further commends the Committee of Bar Examiners and its secretary for the improvement which they made in the 1952 bar examinations as compared with October, 1951. This committee recognizes the extremely difficult task of the Bar Examiners and fully appreciates the long hours of unselfish and difficult work they do in an effort to prepare and give an examination which is a fair test of the applicants' legal knowledge and which will admit those qualified to practice law without opening the ranks of the legal profession to persons not qualified to maintain the present high standards of the profession or its service to the public.

The members of this committee, all practicing attorneys, are fully cognizant of the impossibility of giving a perfect bar examination. They believe, however, that the present Committee of Bar Examiners headed by Graham L. Sterling, Jr., of Los Angeles as chairman and Kenneth G. 

McGilvray of Sacramento as vice chairman, has made great progress toward the goal of this committee: 

1. To establish a bar examination which sets standards reasonably high without being unduly difficult. 

2. To maintain those standards as uniformly as possible from year to year so that the well-prepared student in any given year has just as good a chance of passing the bar examination as the well-prepared student of any other year.

INVESTIGATION OF CHARITIES 1. PREVIOUS REPORTS 

In its progress report to the Legislature, submitted in March, 1952, the committee reported the results of its investigations to date with respect to this subject and recommended further study. 

Since the submission of its progress report, the committee has made additional investigations and has held further hearings upon the subject. 

II. FURTHER WORK OF THE COMMITTEE A. HEARING OF JULY 14, 1952, LOS ANGELES 

The committee held a hearing at the State Building in Los Angeles on July 14, 1952, at which time the following persons were heard:

George A. Dockweiler, President of the Board of Directors of the Sister Elizabeth 

Kenny Foundation, Inc., for Southern California;

Dr. Joseph D. Robinson, representing the Los Angeles County Tuberculosis and 

Health Association;

Dr. William D. Evans, President of the Los Angeles County Heart Association; 

Dwight L. Clarke, President, Los Angeles County Branch, American Cancer Society;

Evelyn Spaulding, General Manager, Department of Social Service, City of Los 

Angeles; 

Mrs. Edward Suchman, a taxpayer; 

Howard Miller, Executive Director, Crippled Children's Society of Los Angeles County;

Joseph Dowling, representing Southern California Chapter of the National Foundation for Infantile Paralysis; 

Mary Thayer, Executive Secretary, Arthritis and Rheumatism Foundation;

Donn B. Tatum, President, Board of Directors, United Cerebral Palsy Association of Los Angeles County.

Much of the testimony received from the foregoing witnesses was cumulative and similar to the information previously received from various witnesses at the committee's San Francisco hearing which was referred to in the committee's progress report. 

In substance, it appears that all of these groups solicit funds from the public through various "drives" for the purpose, in one manner or another, of combatting the particular disease or ailment with which the individual organization is concerned. 

1. Division of Campaign Receipts 

The funds received from these solicitations are then divided in varying proportions between "local chapters," "state headquarters," and "national headquarters." For example, the testimony of the representa tive of the Los Angeles County Tuberculosis and Health Association indicates that contributions are made to a state organization on a sliding scale amounting in the preceding fiscal year to 7.9 percent and to its national organization of 5 percent. Representatives of the Los Angeles 

County Heart Association testified that 30 percent of its income was sent to its state organization, of which 30 percent the state organization ( 23 )

retains 5 percent and forwards the remaining 25 percent to the American Heart Association, its national organization. The representative of the 

Los Angeles Branch of the American Cancer Society informed the committee that one-half of the money received (amounting to $339,000 in the preceding fiscal year) was retained in Los Angeles County and the remaining 50 percent was divided between the state and national organizations. The representative of the Crippled Children's Society of Los 

Angeles County states that approximately 28 percent of its income was forwarded to the state and national organizations, with the state organization receiving about 20 percent and the national organization the remainder. Representatives of the Los Angeles Chapter of the National 

Foundation for Infantile Paralysis pointed out that this organization retains one-half of what it receives in contributions and the remaining one-half is forwarded to its national headquarters. The Executive Secretary of the Arthritis and Rheumatism Foundation testified that 40 percent of the money it raised locally was forwarded to its national organization, and the President of the United Cerebral Palsy Association stated that when this organization had nationally sponsored campaigns, 60 percent of the income was retained by the national organization which, however, paid all of the expenses; otherwise, the national organization received 25 percent of all moneys collected by the local chapter. 

Similar testimony was received by the committee in previous hearings, indicating that the pattern above referred to as an example is universally applied to charitable solicitations and the handling of funds received therefrom. At the outset, therefore, it appears that the committee investigations and its conclusions cannot in any event be complete, since large proportions of the funds collected in California are sent out of the State (generally to headquarters in New York City) where its expenditure is governed in a manner beyond the control of this State. 

2. Expenditure of Campaign Receipts. 

The committee has learned that a surprisingly large number of these charitable organizations make expenditures solely for what their representatives describe as "research." Generally, expenditures for this research take the form of grants to universities and other educational institutions. In some instances the grants were made to named individuals and in others directly to a school or department thereof. As to what duplication of research is thus created, the committee does not have adequate information. However, it is a fair presumption that unless the educational institution itself designates the nature of the research, the particular project may well be a duplication of something already undertaken by the university or by other research groups. It is apparent, therefore, that no conclusion can be reached as to the benefit to the public of research sponsored through these fund-raising drives. 

It is, of course, true that some of the organizations make direct grants to needy individuals who have been afflicted with the particular disease. 

For example, testimony from the National Foundation for Infantile Paralysis indicates that substantially all of the funds retained by the local chapter are used for payment of hospital care, medical services and other services auxiliary thereto, all pertaining to the treatment and care of polio. The national chapter of this association does engage in substantial research, but in general its research projects are limited to

those approved by such organizations as the American Medical Association, the Physicians and Surgeons Organization, American Hospital Association, etc.

In connection with the nature of the expenditure of these funds, the committee raises the question as to whether in responding to the appeals of these various organizations the public does not in fact believe that it is contributing for the care of needy patients. Campaign literature from many organizations seems to imply that the funds are requested for the alleviation of distress to individuals who are not otherwise able to provide the necessary care for themselves. In making this observation the committee does not intend to imply that research as to the causes of these various diseases is not valuable, but the question is raised as to whether the public is aware of the use to which its funds will be put. 

3. Campaign and Administrative Costs 

The committee has deemed it significant to inquire as to the percentage of total receipts utilized by the particular organization for campaign and administrative costs. Testimony indicates that these percentages vary substantially, but that in the larger organizations the percentage is understandably lower because of the amount of money collected and also it may be assumed because of more careful administration. For example, the Los Angeles County Tuberculosis and Health Association estimates 

4.9 percent for administrative costs and 14 percent for the cost of the campaign; the Los Angeles County Heart Association estimates 16 percent for campaign purposes and 13 percent for administrative expenses; the Los Angeles Branch of the American Cancer Society estimates 9 percent for campaign expenditures and the administrative expenses 5 percent; the Crippled Children's Society of Los Angeles County estimates administrative and campaign expenses approximately 15 percent; the Los Angeles Chapter of the National Foundation for Infantile 

Paralysis estimates 12.6 percent campaign expenditures and 3 percent local administrative costs as compared to a national average of 4.4 percent.

There appears to be no "rule of thumb" by which an accurate statement can be made as to the proper amount to expend for administrative and campaign expenditures, but in connection with this question, as well as in connection with the whole problem of the regulation of charities, the testimony of Evelyn Spaulding, General Manager of the Department of Social Service, City of Los Angeles, appears to be most enlightening. It is, therefore, hereinafter set forth in detail in Section D hereof.
