C. EXPERIENCE AND PROCEDURES OF THE DEPARTMENT OF SOCIAL SERVICE, CITY OF LOS ANGELES The following brief statement by Evelyn Spaulding, General Manager of the Department of Social Service, City of Los Angeles, indicates in general the procedures followed in that city and her views as to state regulation. "1. Ordinance 77,000 of the Los Angeles Municipal Code authorizes the Board of Social Service Commissioners of the City of Los Angeles to enforce its provisions relating to the regulation or control of solicitations for charitable, philanthropic and patriotic purposes. "2. (a) All organizations desiring to conduct a solicitation for a charitable, philanthropic or patriotic purpose, must file a ‘notice of intention to solicit' at least 10 days prior to the date selected for the solicitation, signed by two officers, together with adequate information about its service and financial standing and the exact use for which the funds are to be raised. "(b) Suitable investigation is made by trained investigators to determine the adequacy of the service rendered or proposed to be rendered. Each notice of intention is given individual attention, and if it appears the agency and its service are worthy an information card is issued which must be shown by the solicitor to each prospective donor. "(c) The information card contains the important facts about the organization and is intended to guide the person solicited in his judgment of the advisability of making a contribution. This card can be recalled if improperly used by the solicitor. "3. (a) A limit of 15 percent of total receipts has been established, which cover all expense of a general solicitation. "(b) Not later than 30 days after the completion of a campaign or conduct of an event of whatever character to raise charitable funds a 'report of results' must be filed, signed by two officers, containing a detailed account of all expenses and showing the amount actually remaining for the accomplishment of the service for which the appeal was made. "All charitable agencies bearing the commission's endorsement are required to file annually a service report and a financial report and if found satisfactory the agency is eligible for re-endorsement for the ensuing year. "All 'reports of results' are checked by our accountant. Audits are requested and received from all organizations of a national or state character conducting local campaigns. These audits are on file in our office for examination by any persons desiring to know what funds solicited locally are applied toward maintenance of national or state or local organizations. "4. The matter of proposed state-wide control of charitable solicitations seems to recur biannually. There is certainly nothing new about it. It is usually brought up by individuals having a very imperfect understanding of the many elements involved. In the case of Los Angeles it is quite evident that after an experience of

38 years in the regulation of such matters during which a system has been devised that it is acknowledged to be unsurpassed by any other city of the United States the control of fund raising for charitable purposes is far more efficient than could possibly be achieved under state control or supervision. Such control is properly handled on a local and not a state level. "State control of charitable solicitations would probably be vested in the State Department of Social Welfare which is organized and maintained principally to administer benefits to persons who may qualify under state law to receive the particular charity applying to their personal needs. It operates with and authorizes the expenditure of funds which have been collected through taxes. It is not experienced in the field of fund raising for charitable purposes and is not qualified to pass upon the merits of charitable appeals. There is a vast difference between the responsibility of expending state funds for relief, and the effective investigations of organizations proposing to make public appeals for funds to be expended for various philanthropic causes or purposes. "It would be necessary to set up in each office of the State Department of Social Welfare an efficient personnel experienced in such matters. "These can be handled much better under the direction of a nonpolitical board or commission composed of representative citizens of the community more interested in the welfare of its charitable institutions than the establishment of a system that might develop into a dictatorship of private charities. There is a possibility that much confusion would result for the State of California is too large to have the same objectives in all of its areas. "Some of the larger cities and even the smaller ones have established and are maintaining systems of checking the solicitation of funds for charitable uses which are eminently satisfactory and are providing adequate protection to the contributing public. "Regulation of solicitations for charitable purposes is peculiarly a matter of municipal home rule which has long been the established policy of this State. State-wide control would result in duplication of effort and additional expense to our agencies where the policy of the law should be to encourage and aid rather than impose limitations and restrictions." 

1. Testimony of Evelyn Spaulding, General Manager 

Evelyn Spaulding, called as a witness by the committee, assumed the witness stand and testified as follows:

Q. By Chairman Busch: You are Miss Evelyn Spaulding? 

A. Yes. I am General Manager of the Department of Social Serv ice, City of Los Angeles.

Q. Miss Spaulding, I think it would be helpful to the committee if you would give us a general statement of the ordinance that you have here in the City and County of Los Angeles respecting the licensing or granting of permits, whichever it is, to charitable organizations operating in this vicinity, and if you wish to give us

some information regarding the state law, if you are familiar with that, that might be helpful.

A. The Department of Social Service was set up in 1908 by ordinance, and in 1925 it was set up through charter, and at that time the charter provided a commission. 

The commission has supervision over the charitable solicitations within the City of Los Angeles. 

Q. How many members does the commission have?

A. Five members appointed by the mayor and confirmed by the city council, and they are picked for their knowledge of the general welfare program in the city. 

There are three women and two gentlemen serving on the commission, and they represent the various faiths; Jewish, Catholic and Protestant and the colored. 

That will give you a general idea of our setup. 

Q. May I ask preliminarily, how long have you been with the department?

A. I am in my nineteenth year, and 10 years as its general manager.

The Social Service Commission has jurisdiction over charitable solicitation drives, in that it regulates and controls charitable and philanthropic organizations. 

Q. What mechanics are set up under the ordinance, assuming that some organization desires a permit to solicit funds for charitable purposes in the City of Los Angeles, what does he do?

A. They are required by ordinance to file a notice of intention 

10 days prior to any solicitation or release of any publicity in behalf of any solicitation, and we have to bear in mind this covers only philanthropic, charitable and patriotic. The wholly religious organization is not required to file. 

Q. Does this organization control the Red Cross? 

A. Yes, it controls all and eliminates no particular agency.

Q. Does the ordinance make it mandatory for any organization or individual seeking to solicit funds for charitable purposes to obtain a permit from your department?

A. It is not a permit. The permit system went out in about 1913.

At that time we worked under a permit system, but at this time we work under an information card, and that is after the intention of notice is filed and an investigation is made as to who is raising the money, how much money is to be raised, who is to get paid for their services and what services that particular organization or association is rendering, that information is imparted on an information card. That information card is put in the hands of the solicitor.

They are required by law to carry that information card, and if there is a mailing program or a brochure, or whatever the appeal is, it must include a copy of the information card. 

This information card sets forth facts. In the event there is any misrepresentation through any of their phamplets or publicity, if they read the legal card, which, of course, is submitted by the department as we write the card, the agency doesn't write it, we write

30 it from the facts that are divulged from the information submitted, when the information card is read it will give the true picture of whether or not the organization is for research, service, or how much money the agency is out to raise. 

Q. Do you set out the breakdown of those expenditures on that card? 

A. Yes, sir.

Q. I assume this ordinance applies to individuals or corporations or associations dealing in interstate solicitation, or does it only apply to charitable organizations operating solely within the 

State of California?

A. If a national organization seeks funds and is within the city limits of Los Angeles, we ask them to file, and they usually do. 

Q. If they don't, have you had any violation develop?

A. Yes. We tell them we will have to inform the public making any inquiry as to solicitation, that they have refused to file or submit their financial statement. 

Q. Has there been a constitutional question raised in regard to your ordinance?

A. No. 

Q. It this method of dealing with them a practical one, that is, advising them that you will publish the notice to the public that they are not complying with the ordinance? 

A. Yes, it has been very successful. 

Q. Why couldn't you prosecute?

A. There are limitations within the City of Los Angeles. 

Q. Assuming that they did it in the city?

A. If it is done by mail completely, then you have no tie-in for your case, only if they come into the city itself. If it is a national organization that has a local representative, then they must, because we have naturally the solicitation within the city, but if it is by direct mail coming in from out-of-state, naturally, you have only the letter of solicitation and not the contribution. 

Q. There has been no challenge to the ordinance in the courts, I take it? 

A. No, sir. 

Q. Go ahead, please.

A. I want to show you a copy of the information card, because 

I was quite interested this morning in your statement regarding the information.

At the time of the notice of intention is filed, the information card is issued, and it sets forth the complete information of the agency; their goal, their estimated expense, their program, expenditures that are proposed.

Many times it is a proposed program based on an established goal by the board of directors.

Another thing we are working with, in that if a board of directors which might be interested in raising $500,000, in order to establish a program to cover the $500,000 they set so much aside for certain activities, but in the event they are only able to make $200,000, that is, the public responds only to that amount, they naturally submit these other things that we have requested, that is,

what they have requested funds for. We have asked the agency not to go out for a program beyond a reasonable amount, in that if they are asking for research funds or other phases of their work, then that particular account would have to be covered with a percentage right across the board to show faith of the public, and not to merely wipe them out on something they asked for but did not receive. Since they are held down to an obligation they are not willing to set up various categories for their funds to be budgeted to. 

Now, that information card, you see, will be either a good one or a bad one, and it is just as good as the agency is able to reflect its service. If the agency is doing a good job, they have a good information card, and naturally as the public becomes educated to the information on the card, they know whether or not, after their own interpretation, they become accustomed to the card in order to determine its worthiness. 

If the agency has repeatedly gone into the field for funds and shown a tremendous high rate of expenses, it is listed on the card. 

It also states if the Social Service Department feels that an expense is abnormally high and they cannot recommend a contribution to the agency. That is in a manner of guidance to the contributing public.

Q. It would be necessary for a solicitor that makes annual solicitations to obtain a permit each year that they solicit, is that right?

A. Yes, in other words, the fiscal year is from July 1st to 

June 30th. 

Q. And the card is just issued on a fiscal year basis? 

A. No. If the drive is, say, two or three months, in other words, ordinarily in the health agency, which you have heard here, the majors, they have given times during the year allotted to them, so there is no overlapping, so the commission has established somewhat of a guide stick for them and their campaign runs for that period.

Q. But these cards are just issued on an annual basis? 

A. Not if the agency is just going on the basis of using a given time.

Q. But the card expires at the end of the calendar year, is that right?

A. It expires on the date given on the card and then automatically expires on June 30th.

Q. In other words, we will say the American Cancer Society, the 

Los Angeles Branch, would have annually to come in and give you this information and obtain the information card, and at the end of that time they submit a report form as well, an audit covering that? 

A. Yes.

Q. In those cases where the solicitation is by mail what is done regarding presenting this card?

A. They are required to put them in a letter. They bring it down to the sizes and that is the responsibility of the agency to fit in the mailing. Some of them put it right in on their pamphlets.

Here is one from the Heart and one from the United Jewish Welfare Fund, but they all have various methods of submitting them to the public. 

Q. (By Senator O'Gara): What do you do about radio and newspaper advertising, do you recall any references to the card?

A. The radio and newspapers, seemingly its outlet is through the 

Department of Social Service. The Newspaper Publishers Asso ciation clear with the department and get facts from the information cards which are read to them at the time of clearance and determination at that time whether or not they will release, the same with TV and radio. 

Q. They don't refer to the card?

A. We read the card to them at the time they make the inquiry. 

Q. I don't mean that in radio or newspapers that neither of those refer to the card?

A. Not in the sense of reading the card to the general public. 

Q. Does it tell the general public that the cards are available, or anything like that; invite them to ask for the cards? 

A. No; newspapers and TV do not do that. 

Q. You don't find that is necessary?

A. No, because we ordinarily have the conversation with the particular representative at the time the commission feels that the organization should not solicit, and they eliminate the appeal. 

Q. (By Chairman Busch): You do exercise a discretion under your ordinance, that is, if the department determines that an applicant is not a proper person to receive the card, you refuse to give it to them, is that right, or can you do that under your ordinance?

A. It is not a matter of refusing, it is a matter of asking for information, and if they are able to submit the information and follow the ordinance, which, of course, is a pretty strict one, then they are able to get out and solicit.

Q. If a person came up and said he was an ex-felon and applied for that card, would you put on the card that he is an ex-felon? 

A. No, that is taken up before the Board of Social Service, that is, the Social Service Commission. This commission sets up regular operational policies which would be accepted for a standard agency, and this particular felon would find it quite difficult in getting that kind of a board to let him go to work for them. 

Q. (By Senator Tenney): Do you turn down petitions from time to time? 

A. Yes. Our motto is to nip them in the bud. 

Q. Can they still go ahead and solicit?

A. No. 

Q. What do they do? 

A. It is a violation. 

Q. A misdemeanor? 

A. Yes. 

Q. (By Chairman Busch): What do they charge them for; this soliciting, what are they charged with? 

A. Failure to file notice of intention. 

Q. (By Senator Tenney): Suppose they do?

A. Then they are denied for a particular reason. 

Q. Does the ordinance cover that?

A. No, it doesn't in words, but if a person files a notice of intention and the commission feels that this should not be conducted for reasons, then they confer with the particular individual that files, then if they insist upon going ahead with the solicitation, which they will do at the meeting, the commission will issue a card and set forth on that card; it is a red card, the reason why the public should not support this, and at the same time give publicity to the papers that such and such an individual has been issued a red card and it behooves the public to read the card. 

Q. (By Chairman Busch): Theoretically anybody could obtain this card, but if the commission were not satisfied, they would put the information out there in such a manner that it would be conspicuous to the public, and that is really your control, is that right? 

A. Yes, it is controlled too, because the commission does have. the power to publicize any finding on any investigation concerning any solicitation within the city. 

Q. Is that set up in the ordinance? 

A. Yes. 

Q. (By Senator Tenney): There is a penalty for going ahead even though the petition has been denied, that is a criminal proceeding, is it? 

Senator O'Gara: I don't understand that they insist, they just give him a red card. 

Q. (By Senator Tenney): There is no criminal penalty if they comply with this and still are denied, they can still go ahead and solicit? 

Senator O'Gara: If he can't get any money, if they have a red card- 

Senator Tenney: Who knows about the card? 

Q. (By Senator O'Gara): How many red cards do you give out in a year?

A. Very few, because usually they say they will withdraw the notice.

Q. (By Senator Tenney): In other words, it is as effective as you think it is, is that right? 

A. Yes. 

Q. (By Chairman Busch): Have you heard of why they haven't passed an ordinance that makes it necessary to obtain a permit? 

A. We had an ordinance, and the Dark Case found it unconstitutional.

Q. So the City of Los Angeles changed its ordinance pursuant to that litigation? 

A. Yes; the Dark Case.

Q. It is our understanding that a statute or ordinance would not be constitutional in so far as interstate organizations are concerned if it made it mandatory to obtain a permit. 

Q. (By Mr. Bohn): I just happened to see in this publicity of the Christmas Seal Drive that the Tuberculosis Association has a 

2-L-3870

little card at the bottom which apparently bears your signature, is that correct?

A. That is the endorsement. In other words, we have another ordinance that has no connection whatsoever with this particular information card.

In other words, an agency can go out and solicit and yet it doesn't necessarily meet the requirements of an endorsed agency, but if an agency would like to have endorsement by the commission, they submit additional information concerning their services, a study made and an endorsement is granted that agency, and that agency will stay within 15 percent expenses in their fund raising, and has complied with particular standards as set forth in that ordinance, they are given an endorsement by the department. That is what you see here in addition to the information card, because they are an endorsed agency, they like it to be known. 

Q. In other words, in addition to the information procedure which you outlined, there are some agencies which are so standard and which furnish such information as your group considers properly appropriate, you are willing to go one step further and that is in effect to endorse them and state to the public that that is a worthy cause for their contributions?

A. Yes; that is wholly voluntary on the part of the agency. 

They request the endorsement.

In the middle of the solicitation they may withdraw their applications if they want to, and it is no violation if they do not become an endorsed agency.

Q. Hasn't the experience been that an agency that can advertise the fact that they have your endorsement, doesn't it have more of a fund-raising appeal than one that is not? 

A. Right; yet that same agency will go under stricter criticism from the department, because they want to maintain a standard that keeps them within this endorsement program and they are continually revising their program to live up to their endorsement, so they are trying to do the job in the community that the community expects of them, and that is the type of agency that serves the community and it is something that the community pays for. They, in turn, naturally will receive the support. 

Q. One question in connection with that, you said that you have a standard which is worthy of endorsement; one of the amount of money spent for the campaign.

Do those standards in any way vouch how much money will be spent at the New York or eastern headquarters, or do you inquire into that?

A. Yes, we inquire into exactly how much goes to the national. organization.

For instance, let me break down the agencies for you. We have what we call the major agencies. There are the major health agencies, which you people heard this morning.

We then have the United Jewish Welfare Fund, the Y. M. C. A., the Community Chest. Those are the ones that solicit radio and television aid. 

Now, what was your question again?

Q. My question was whether or not you were cognizant of the proportion or amount of money that was sent somewhere else; to a national organization?

A. The major agencies, naturally, for instance the health organizations, about two years ago we had about four of them, now we have about seven. They are established usually on a national basis, so the national sends a representative to a local area, which, of course, Los Angeles is a good area to establish a program, and their particular policy or program that the local follows is usually set up so that the representative or agent that establishes locally, follows a pattern, so if they are a separate corporation in order to stay within the national, they have to follow their policies to the degree what percent national is going to receive, and in some cases it is state too.

Sometimes they are completely autonomous. Sometimes they are in a chapter. If they are in a chapter, naturally they are guided wholly by national policy, so if national policy says "We must have 50 percent of the gross of local contributions" then local must send it.

Our board is very interested in what national does with 50 percent, so national must in turn send us a national audit. From that we determine how much is administrative and how much is campaign. on a national basis, and how much is for service. 

If the audit reveals that amount of money is being used correctly, then the commission will go along with the program. If not, they will ask local to work on national to cut down the amount that goes to national and use it on local basis for local service. 

Q. Do you have in addition to that any rule of thumb when looking at a situation that you consider to be a certain percentage as being proper to go to a national organization?

Suppose there is a situation that 10 percent of the money were kept in Los Angeles and 90 percent was sent to some national organization, would you disapprove solely on that ground or would you attempt to ascertain what happens to the 90 percent, before disapproval?

A. We usually recommend against any local group sending 90 percent to a national organization unless it is wholly research, then there is a question in our mind that how can such a local community such as this which has the responsibility of supporting services in a community, how much of the 90 percent of the funds collected going to a national research, how much of that would mean a national program, because the amount isn't so great on that basis, we discovered 90 percent going to a national, we would tell the agency that the public isn't too interested in supporting a national program, but the public is interested in what local services they can render for our own community, and that, of course, starts when the agency is attempting to get established here, and that usually starts from correspondence between the local and national and means that they change the amount from 40 and 50 to 25, perhaps.

I know of one or two agencies that have done it.

Q. Is there any definite rule of thumb of any percent that you think is proper?

A. No. 

Q. In your judgment what percentage is normally proper to leave the community?

A. It all depends on the type of service. If it is a type of local service that is needed in the community, I am against sending any of it to national, because national is always located in a given area and let the support come from them. 

Of course, if they were establishing chapters all over the United States, they have to get their money from somewhere, and naturally you know they are going to try to get it from some local representatives.

Even on a national basis if they are supporting a national program, to a degree it might be good in exchanging a small amount, say, 10 percent might be considered a goodly amount, because they can exchange ideas and services that in some cases might help the local establish agencies.

Q. The reason I asked the question before, the committee has heard at previous hearings different percentages that go from these various health-type solicitations to a national organization, and the lowest that I recall having heard was the 10 percent mentioned by Judge Dockweiler this morning in connection with the Sister Kenny Fund. 

If I understood his testimony correctly, that was an individual negotiation rather than a general situation. 

We have heard, as I recall, as high as 50 percent of the amount collected locally, not in Los Angeles County, but in the north, going forward to a national organization. Do you have any that high? 

A. Yes, the National Infantile has 50 percent.

You see, when local contributes 50 percent to national, they also meet emergencies that strike in any particular area.

In this particular case, 14 percent of what this local got here was sent into national out of 50 percent contributed, so they meet the emergencies of the various chapters. That is one of the agencies that runs 40 or 35 or-Cerebral Palsy is 25, I think.

Q. (By Chairman Busch): There was something said here by one of the previous witnesses to the effect that this organization was relieved of filing any report with the State Department of Social Welfare of the State of California, or else that was done through your commission. I wasn't quite clear as to the exact detail, but is there any practice or any cooperative arrangement between your commission and the State Department of Social Welfare relative to the filing of financial reports or making investigations of these organizations?

A. Yes, I believe your ordinance states that the agency must file an audit with the state department, and after that was passed your representative did get in touch with our commission and asked if they would be willing, just by inquiry, to send off a letter as to whether or not the audit was filed by the agency soliciting in Los Angeles, and they would accept that audit filed with them and they were very happy to do that.

Q. Does the Department of Social Welfare make any investiga tion, to your knowledge? 

A. Not to my knowledge; not that I know of.

Q. (By Senator O'Gara): What about the County of Los Angeles outside of the city limits, do they follow your recommendations?

A. The County of Los Angeles adopted our ordinance prior to amendment, in that we amended our ordinance some years ago to include any promoter that comes from outside the city, to post bonds and secure a license, or those agencies that are not endorsed, the requirement is for them to post a $2,000 bond and secure a license, and any solicitor that aids them must secure a $5,000 bond. 

Any person soliciting for charitable purposes-you can use the telephone if you want and solicit and ask him, as a friend, to support an agency, but if you are doing this for pay, it is prohibited by ordinance. 

Q. Is that true in both the city and the county?

A. I don't believe the county has adopted that. I am not too sure, but outside of that they do have our ordinance. It is just one of those things, I don't think they have budgeted any amount to enforce it, and they usually call us about an agency that does file. 

In other words, they check every agency that is brought to our attention through an individual or newspaper publicity, and we remind them of the ordinance requiring them to file, and they file. We had 1,800 filings last year.

Q. (By Chairman Busch): How large a staff do you have? 

A. Ten employees including myself. 

Q. Are the commissioners themselves paid? 

A. They serve for $5 a meeting, once a week.

Q. What does the general expense of the commission during a year's time amount to? 

A. Around $35,000. 

Q. That takes care of just the City of Los Angeles? 

A. City of Los Angeles. 

Q. Excepting for the extracurricular work that you perform in the County of Los Angeles, is that right? 

A. That is right. 

Q. (By Senator O'Gara): You say there were 1,800 filings, and how many information cards did you issue to how many different organizations out of the 1,800 filings, how many actually received the information card?

A. I couldn't give you the exact amount of those that are denied, I imagine about 90 percent of those would receive an information card.

Many of them come in without filing. In other words, we ordinarily interview the individual prior to a filing, which is always the best way. We ask them enough about the organization to determine whether or not they can exist, and tell them we are quite sure that they would not be able to make it, and then they don't put the filing through. 

Q. Do you have endorsement cards; do you issue how many of those in a year?

888 A. That is a little hard to determine—we would issue an information card, one or more to each one- Q. I don't mean how many individual cards, I mean how many organizations receive endorsement cards? 

A. There are about 160 endorsed agencies out of 1,800. 

Q. How many agencies do you have altogether; organizations and individuals that have these cards? Some of them are individuals and some of them are agencies, and I would like a breakdown as to that.

A. I think there are about six hundred and some odd agencies, I am not sure about that. 

Q. (By Senator Tenney): What fee do they pay when they apply?

A. No fee, only for the promoter and the solicitor. 

Q. What are these fees?

A. If it is a promoter they have to pay $25 for a year, that is a license. Then they also must have a $2,000 bond, which, of course, they get, and the solicitor has a registration fee of $1. 

Q. (By Chairman Busch): What is a promoter ? 

A. That is a person that has the idea and sells the idea for a fee. 

He is strictly in charge of the complete fund raising program of the agency.

Q. These established agencies, they don't require a promoter's license, do they? 

A. Not the endorsed agencies. 

Q. The others do? 

A. Yes, if they are not an endorsed agency. If the agency does not show an endorsement, it doesn't mean that they are not denied an endorsement, it means that they have not applied or aren't ready to apply.

Q. Suppose that an individual comes in and applies for a card and gives you the information required by your ordinance, is he in addition to that required to post a bond with your commission? 

A. If they are not an endorsed agency, yes.

In the first place, the corporation files, that is, the name of the agency, they file the notice of intention, and the person that they hire to raise the funds is the promoter. He is hired for a fee to do the job. 

Q. And he must post a bond if he doesn't represent an endorsed corporation?

A. That is right. Even if he does represent an endorsed corporation, if he just comes in just for the time of the campaign, he would have to file, but if he is on the paid staff the year around, he is not required to file if he is with an endorsed agency. 

Q. (By Mr. Bohn): Do you happen to know offhand how many other cities in California have ordinances similar to the one that you have here? 

A. I think there are about six. 

You say "similar" to what we have? I don't think there are any that are similar to ours, but there are six different cities that have ordinances.

Q. That seek in one way or another to regulate the same things that you are attempting to regulate?

A. Correct. 

Q. Would you have any objection to furnishing the committee with a list of those agencies that applied for one reason or another and withdrew their application, or for one reason or another did not receive a card, or a red card? 

A. For what period of time? 

Q. Just for the last year.

A. I think I could get you the names of the agencies, yes, but as I say, most of our work is ground work prior to any filing at all. 

Q. The purpose of my question is to seek to get a list of those groups soliciting, that for one reason or another are not deemed worthy of solicitation in the City of Los Angeles, or does not have the requisite information, or otherwise do not meet your standards. 

A. Yes. Ordinarily that crops up where an individual is anxious to start some sort of service in behalf of the person that has thought up the idea.

Sometimes we don't find out with many agencies until they have collected a certain amount of money, and then we review their report.

You see, after the agency conducts a solicitation, if it is based on an annual solicitation, perhaps they have an idea and they figure they could get their filing in, at this particular time an information card is issued for a year if it is just a small solicitation. 

If we do not have too much faith in that particular program as it is presented to us, they still have a constitutional right to solicit, and at that particular time if we feel we cannot discourage it, an information card is issued, but it is issued on a condition after three months that we will review the financial report, which will be required at that given time, and determine the worthiness of the agency.

When we do review that we can tell them, "Now, we have to tell this story to the public, you are not doing what you are supposed to, and you are not going to get the support of the public and we will have to publish this report that you have submitted. Do you want us to do that?" "That They will immediately say, "No, we will discontinue that. is our method of discouraging agencies, and we do quite a bit of that. 

Q. It is that list that I am seeking to obtain for the committee. 

Q. (By Chairman Busch): Can you give us some idea of the aggregate amount of funds solicited in Los Angeles for each year for charitable purposes?

A. We have a figure for last year that runs around $36,000,000. 

Q. What does that include? 

A. That includes all charitable and patriotic agencies, but a certain amount of that is not wholly within the City of Los Angeles. 

In other words, we ordinarily try to keep within the chest area. 

Q. What is that area?

A. It takes in some of the outlying districts. 

Q. It doesn't take in the whole County of Los Angeles? 

A. I am not too sure of that. No, it doesn't.

Q. (By Senator Tenney): Just Los Angeles City? 

A. It extends beyond Los Angeles City. It takes in Bell and Maywood and Inglewood, and I think they do some work in Compton, but I am not too sure of that territory. 

Q. (By Chairman Busch): That area contributes about $36,000,- 

000 annually? 

A. Yes. 

Q. That is for charitable, philanthropic and patriotic purposes? 

A. Yes. 

Q. (By Senator Tenney): Can you give us an illustration of a patriotic organization that solicits funds?

A. The American Legion. 

Q. Do you mean the poppy drives? 

A. Yes. 

Q. Organizations like the V. F. W.? 

A. Yes. Like the Purple Heart, all organizations, whether they are patriotic or otherwise are required to file. 

I have noticed that some organizations are eliminated by certain ordinances, but that could be proven unconstitutional. All of them have to be considered operating the same as the others, but you can't eliminate them by saying that the patriotic organization does not have to file, it is not a healthy condition, and you will go along some length of time and find yourself involved in a legal mess. 

Q. (By Chairman Busch): In the processing of these applica tions, assuming that you are satisfied, after making an investigation, you as an individual, do you still have to refer the application to the commission for confirmation or final action?

A. The commission reviews practically all filings. 

Q. Do they do that in every case, or upon some question being raised by the person issuing the card? 

A. Let's put it this way: The commission reviews all filings, but they have hearings on special cases. 

Q. The review would be more or less perfunctory as to most of the filings unless some question was raised, is that right? 

A. That is right.

Chairman Busch: No further questions. 

Q. (By Senator Tenney) : Organizations that solicit funds that are used outside of the State and outside of the Nation, are there any such organizations? 

A. Yes. 

Q. Do you know what they are?

A. There is the United Jewish Welfare Fund. 

Q. Any others?

Chairman Busch: Of course, the Red Cross. 

The Witness: Yes. You are speaking of the major organizations, but you have so many groups like the Hadassah and Palestine groups. 

Q. (By Senator Tenney): Are those funds to be used outside of the 

United States? 

A. Yes.

Q. What about fraternal organizations, is that solicitation under your direction?

A. No, if they confine it to members, it is only where it deals with the general public.

Q. (By Senator O'Gara): I think, Miss Spaulding's testimony evi dences that this is very big business here and she seems to be regulating it very well, and I think if you give the committee those cards that you use, for our records, I think it might be helpful in passing state legislation or passing regulations along to the community, the red card or the endorsement card or the information card. 

A. Yes.

Senator Tenney: Also we should have a list of those organizations that have been, shall we say, licensed, no, that is not correct, but whatever it is, upon which cards have been issued and those that have been specifically approved should be designated. 

The Witness: I don't quite get that, sir.

Q. (By Senator Tenney): If I remember the figure correctly, 600 

or so that have been issued these information cards which were approved or endorsed. What I would like to have is a list of all the organizations that have complied with the ordinance and issued the permit or the card, whatever the procedure is, together with those, and distinguishing those that have been approved and their addresses. 

A. Who is going to furnish the personnel to get those lists made? 

After all, we keep that in alphabetical order in our files. We don't list them. Ordinarily, we have our endorsed organizations, and we are happy to give them out.

Q. (By Chairman Busch): Couldn't you have available a list of the agencies that have made applications?

Q. (By Senator Tenney): Are they so voluminous? 

A. Yes, we had 1,800 filings last year.

Q. (By Chairman Busch): The 600 figure only referred to. 

A. Organizations only? 

Q. (By Senator O'Gara): We would be very much interested in getting the names of organizations that got the red card. 

Senator Tenney: I think it would be worth the committee paying for it, if necessary, to make a study of this matter. 

The Witness: Every agency that is a member of the Community 

Chest is an endorsed agency. That is one of the requirements. Immediately upon the application of the chest for funds of the chest the question to them is "Are you an endorsed agency?”

If they are not, it throws them back in the department, and we are at that time able to review the agency and its services. If they cannot comply with the items necessary for endorsement, then we notify them. Any agency that you have in the chest, you have an endorsed agency.

Q. (By Chairman Busch): Would there be any practical way of getting the information at our cost?

A. I would like you to write a letter to the commission and let them instruct me, they are the boss.

I am just the general manager, and anything they order me to do 

I will be happy to do.

Q. But it is a public agency; there is no question about the information being available, is there?

A. It would have to be prepared, that changes so. 

Q. If there is no objection, we will instruct Mr. Bohn to write the necessary letters to obtain the information for us, and whatever charge there is it will be a charge to the committee. (By Mr. Bohn): I think I know your views on this particular question, but the committee will be interested in having it. 

Do you believe that a statute on a state-wide basis prescribing regulation of charities is a desirable thing, or do you think it should be left for local jurisdiction or control?

A. No, I am not in favor of state-wide supervision, and the reason 

I say that, it is not personal at all.

We live with these charities. We work with them. They are rendering a service to the community. The thing that we try to do, that is, what the commission tries to do is to guide them and counsel with them and to show them their errors as they go along.

Most of these people that serve on the board of the agencies are people that are giving of their time to the community. They are outstanding citizens, and there happens to be a job to do and they want to do it.

Some of them have complete know-how and some do not. If they show good will and are sincere in their program, which most of them are, they need guidance. Their directors are under the same program in that they too need the guidance. They are trying to do a good job in the community, because the people look to them. 

State-wide supervision is too far away from the source. You have to counsel with these people probably at least once a month. You do discuss things that come up. It is competitive, let us put it that way. 

As you say, charity is big business. You get somebody interested in a particular program. They forget what the truth is about their program, they are in for a job, they get state-wide aid, and see what can be done on the basis of an appeal. There is no reflection of what they are doing and the actual service they are rendering. 

This can be handled on the basis of an all year around staff. It is different where someone comes in a particular job. We have the problem too, we try to work on that the minute it hits. We say to them, "No, you can't do that because of so and so. 

We are interested in all of the causes and the State would probably be interested in passing a law in regard to the notice of intention, or something like that, but you don't get to the heart of the community. You don't see what fits in that particular program. You have no idea of overlapping causes, because you are not close enough to it.

If they do have a problem they would have to come upstate or downstate. You have so many solicitations in a big area like Los 

Angeles. You have these tag days, flower days, you have the patriotic organizations that are carried through on certain days because of tradition, and that is only on a given day. 

State-wide, how would you handle it, by ordinance, that is a question there. 

Q. (By Senator O'Gara): It occurs to me that this committee might very well recommend, Miss Spaulding, that a state law be enacted which would require compliance with a state law unless

it was a county, like yours, where they had set up an ordinance adopting certain minimum standards as rigid as yours, so that in other areas where there is no protection of this kind people would have state protection that would regulate charities. 

A. Where they didn't have any legislation governing, I think it might be all right to put it in on a state level, but I don't believe where you can get the interest in a local area on charities you should come in state-wide, that is just my opinion. 

Q. On the other hand, I think the committee might set up minimum standards. I think we heard in San Francisco that we have some kind of a charity ordinance. I don't think it is nearly as complete as yours, we might consider that we might want to set minimum standards that these cities would abide by if they want home rule on the situation. I don't think they have control in San Francisco like you.

A. They have an ordinance in San Francisco like they do in Los 

Angeles, only it's operated through the police department. 

Q. I should know. I helped draft it.

A. I read the ordinance and asked what they do with applications, and they said they pigeonhole it.

There has to be a particular body that has to be interested in a particular charity.

The first ordinance, A. B. No. 2069, where they wanted to put it in the sheriff's department compared with a crime, or something, you see where that would go. 

Q. (By Senator Tenney): You do have this problem: Some of the agencies of the chest, we learned a number of years ago they were using their facilities, for instance, Communist schools, and one organization, the entire amount allocated went to salaries at a Communist school. 

Is there any control in your organization over that at all?

A. I think a chest, just like the rest of us, is a victim of circumstances.

For instance, that particular report that the chest is supporting an agency, just because someone comes in with a statement, you can't go on the basis of just a statement. 

Q. We had sworn testimony. 

A. Do you want the chest to file on them? What would you think would be the procedure on it?

Q. As a result of that, many people that don't want to contribute to any communist causes wouldn't contribute to those. 

A. I know that the chest isn't contributing to that agency at the present time. 

Q. One they are not, but one other they are.

A. Just like this, we have a notice of intention that someone files, and they are communists. We have to issue an information card. 

We may have to put on this information that according to such and such a report they are fellow travelers. Then the latest report comes down that we didn't have a right to put that down. We know they are communists. People have called us and said, "You have issued an information card to a communist organization."

If the national government can't take any stand on it, what can we do? 

Q. The only point that we have in our mind is could the State do anything?

A. I believe if the State could do something, that the local government would do it, because we are all patriotic. Everyone in our department is, and we would be glad to do it. 

It bothers us when we have to write an information card and when it is a communist group, we know we have to write it. We used to put it on the card, but we don't any more. 

Q. Can you give us a list of them?

A. I couldn't give you a list of the communist organizations. 

Q. Can you give us a list of the organizations? 

A. Who am I to determine if they are communist? 

Q. Well, we could subpena them you know.

A. It is a problem, it is above me. I know we get calls that so and so is a communist and working for the agency. We used to have a notice of intention or a letterhead, and Mr. Brown and Mr. Smith would happen to be there and see the notice or letterhead and state, "This man is a communist," but we don't do that any more, so that if he is a communist, they say he has a right as an individual, so what is my answer to that one?

Q. (By Chairman Busch): Can you give us a list of those cards that have been issued and those that have been denied, who have made applications and have not received cards? 

A. Yes.

Q. We could work it out by process of elimination. 

A. You will have to get a letter to the commission as to what you would like. 

Q. We are just groping in the dark on this committee.

A. There is only one thing that we are particularly interested in, we believe, and I know the City of Los Angeles has a right to be proud of the regulation that we have here and it isn't something that we have picked up, that is, because of necessity, because it has been in operation since 1908 and it has grown with the community, and if somebody can offer something that is better or somebody wants to say, regulate or control beyond the point that we feel what we are doing, fine, but the mere fact of passing an ordinance and having what has been accomplished in the city dissipated, we are. against it and we do hope that before you do anything that you will show our commission the courtesy of discussing it with them, because they were pioneers in the field, and I believe our department can give you more statistics, more information than any agency within the community, and more than any city in the United States can. 

Q. Does that include New York? 

A. Yes. 

Q. What is their general practice?

A. I am not too sure, but I know that we have the most complete information. In other words, all of our nationals file their audit. on what they are doing,

Q. From your discussions here, the commission makes a determination as to issuing a card and it makes a determination as to the necessity of the particular organization, in light of the community needs, that is true, isn't it? You don't limit your investigation to the possibility of fraud, in other words? 

A. Oh, no.

Q. In the organizational operation you take into consideration the circumstances of the community, its general needs and the money that it made available for, we will say charitable purposes in this community, is that correct?

A. That is right. We try to eliminate, as best we can, duplication, but you also have to bear in mind that an individual or a group has a constitutional right, and irrespective of the fact that you do point out if they insist upon going ahead with the program, then all you can do is educate the contributing public against that particular cause, because it is a racket or duplication or something that is in conflict with an existing cause. 

Q. In a sense, your undertaking is by indirection, that you really can't do directly, is that right? 

A. That is right.

Q. (By Senator Tenney): Let me ask another question. Organi zations for instance such as a current one, I forget the full name of it, but it is a communist organization, a defense organization to solicit and raise funds for the defense of the 14 communists on trial now, does that come under your jurisdiction? 

A. Yes. 

Q. Have they made application? 

A. Yes. 

Q. Has that been issued? 

A. Yes. 

Q. That is for the 14?

A. It is for the counsel. The public is requested to pay counsel for the different groups that are unable to pay, it comes under philanthropy.

Q. In other words, if we had an organization that suddenly came into being called, for instance, The American League for the 

Defense of the North Koreans, the Red Chinese fighting the Americans in Korea, you would have to issue a permit? 

A. If it is for welfare purposes.

Q. Well, for the welfare of the North Korean and Chinese armies. 

A. In what sense?

Q. In other words, you have an organization that is soliciting funds to go out of the United States, assuming an extreme, The American Committee for Medical Aid, we will say, to the North 

Korean and Red Chinese, you would have to issue that card? 

A. Surely. We have had agencies file to send money to Birobijian right in Russia. 

Q. (By Chairman Busch): You say you have to do that because the law requires it? 

A. That is right.

Q. (By Mr. Bohn): The card, however, would divulge the purpose for which the money was going to be spent?

A. Right. 

Q. In that particular case would that be one of the situations that you would issue a red card?

A. No. 

Q. (By Senator Tenney): In other words, the card for defense of the communists here on trial, would that card state that purpose? 

A. Yes. We used to state on the card that the funds were for 

Birobijian, and naturally some people called us about it, for issuing a card to Communist Russia, so we say, "Yes, we have to do it.” 

They resent it, do you blame them?

Give us some laws and I will be very happy to enforce that one, sir.

Senator Tenney: We will keep working on it. 

Chairman Busch: Any further questions? 

Thank you very much, Miss Spaulding.
