Non-compliance with or violation of Article 4, Chapter 4, Los Angeles Municipal Code is a misdemeanor punishable by a fine or imprisonment or both.

4. Organizations Filing Applications for Charitable Solicitations in the City of 

Los Angeles

In order to indicate the substantial nature of the problem and the number of organizations interested in charitable campaigns, the committee has caused to be tabulated all organizations making applications under the Los Angeles ordinance for the year 1951, and it has found that these organizations may be generally grouped as follows: 

128 organizations endorsed by the Social Service Commission; 

1,353 individuals or organizations to whom information cards were issued; 

75 organizations which withdrew or failed to pursue the application after having filed.

It is, therefore, apparent that in the City of Los Angeles alone during the year 1951 there was a total of at least 1,556 individuals or organizations which were interested in the field of charitable solicitation and of the groups that actually engaged in solicitation the Committee's Progress Report indicates that some $36,000,000 was collected. 

A complete list of all of the organizations above referred to will be found attached as Appendix B to this report so that they may be readily compared with organizations filing reports and information with the 

State Department of Social Welfare as provided by Section 147.2 of the Welfare and Institutions Code and in order that there may be some indication of the scope of this problem. At this point it should be noted that to the best of the committee's knowledge no other county or city in the State maintains the comprehensive records previously indicated by the City of Los Angeles, and the committee, therefore, deems it impossible to give an accurate statement as to the number of organizations operating in this field on a state-wide basis. It is for this reason that the committee is recommending legislation to strengthen the state statutes so that this information may be obtained and some element of regulation placed into effect on a state-wide basis. 

D. EXPERIENCE AND PROCEDURES OF THE DEPARTMENT OF SOCIAL WELFARE, STATE OF CALIFORNIA 

The Department of Social Welfare has furnished to the committee a list of all organizations which have complied with Section 147.2 of the Welfare and Institutions Code, which organizations are as follows: 

American Cancer Society-California Division; California Heart Association; California Tuberculosis and Health Association; San Francisco

Heart Association; San Mateo County Chapter of American Heart 

Association; Save the Children Federation.

Since only six organizations filed with the State of California, as compared with 1,556 organizations operating in the City of Los Angeles alone, it seems clear that state control under the present statutes is completely ineffective.

In connection with the foregoing information the department offers the following further comment: "Since the department is merely the depository of such reports and has no enforcement powers, very little attention has been paid to the law by the organizations involved. We are proposing to the 

Legislature that the law be changed so that either the department has enforcement powers or the section be eliminated from the code."

III. RECOMMENDATIONS AND CONCLUSIONS 

The foregoing information is submitted by the committee primarily as an indication of the scope and magnitude of the problem involved. 

In this connection it desires to point out that there are two major problems in the way of making a conclusive and accurate determination in this field: 

1. There is a lack of information on a state-wide basis which is not obtainable without an amendment to Section 147.2 of the Welfare and Institutions Code; 

2. As previously pointed out, many of the charitable organizations send large proportions of their receipts outside of the State of California and conduct interstate campaigns and are, therefore, in part beyond the control of this Legislature. 

Accordingly, your committee recommends as follows: 

1. That a resolution be adopted by both houses of the California 

Legislature memorializing the United States Congress to create a committee for the purpose of investigating charitable solicitations which are undertaken on a national scale or which otherwise are beyond the control of any state legislature. Further, that upon the creation of such a committee by the United States Congress, the Senate create an interim committee for the purpose of correlating the problem and its solution in California with the work of the national investigation; 

2. That Chapter 1.7 of Division 1 of the Welfare and Institutions 

Code dealing with the solicitation of charitable funds in this State be amended so as to provide penalties for the failure to file reports now required by said section and to provide enforcement machinery therefor.

COMPARATIVE NEGLIGENCE I. INTRODUCTION 

At the 1951 General Session of the Legislature the so-called doctrine of "Comparative Negligence" was considered and referred to this interim committee for study. At that time the following bills bearing upon the matter were introduced, which said bills are hereinafter reproduced in full for the purpose of indicating the varying approaches that have been made to the problem as follows:

A. ASSEMBLY BILL NO. 2588 BY MR. ROSENTHAL 

An act to add Section 1714.1 to the Civil Code, relating to contributory negligence.

The people of the State of California do enact as follows: 

SECTION 1. Section 1714.1 is added to the Civil Code, to read: 

1714.1. Where any person suffers injury or damage, or where such injury or damage has resulted in death, as the result partly of his own fault and partly of the fault of any other person or persons, a claim in respect of that injury, damage or death shall not be defeated by reason of the fault of the person suffering the injury, damage or death, but the damages recoverable in respect thereof shall be reduced to such an extent as the trier of facts find just and equitable having regard to the share of the claimant or decedent as the case may be in the responsibility for the injury, damage or death.

B. ASSEMBLY BILL NO. 1310 BY MR. SMITH 

An act to amend Section 1714 of, and to add Section 3333.1 to the Civil Code, relating to responsibility for negligence. 

The people of the State of California do enact as follows: 

SECTION 1. Section 1714 of the Civil Code is hereby amended to read as follows: 

1714. Every one is responsible not only for the result of his wilful acts, but also for an injury occasioned to another by his want of ordinary care or skill in the management of his property or person, except as far as but when the latter has, wilfully or by want of ordinary care, brought the injury upon himself, the latter's wilful conduct or want of ordinary care must be taken into consideration, by way of mitigation, in estimating the damages. The extent of liability in such cases is defined by the title on compensatory relief.

SEC. 2. Section 3333.1 is hereby added to the Civil Code, to read as follows: 

3333.1. Contributory negligence shall not bar recovery in an action. by any person or his legal representative to recover damages for negligence resulting in death or in injury to person or property, if such negligence was not as great as the negligence of the person against whom ( 51 )

recovery is sought, but any damages allowed shall be diminished by the jury in the proportion to the amount of negligence attributable to the person recovering. 

C. ASSEMBLY BILL NO. 3200 BY MESSRS. CONDON AND HAGEN 

An act to add Section 1714.3 to the Civil Code, relating to liability for torts.

The people of the State of California do enact as follows: 

SECTION 1. Section 1714.3 is added to the Civil Code, to read: 

1714.3. Notwithstanding any other provision of law, where any person suffers injury or damage, or where such injury or damage has resulted in death, as the result partly of his own fault and partly of the fault of any other person or persons, a claim in respect of that injury, damage or death shall not be defeated by reason of the fault of the person suffering the injury, damage or death, but the damages recoverable in respect thereof shall be reduced to such an extent as the trier of facts finds just and equitable having regard to the share of the claimant or decedent as the case may be in the responsibility for the injury, damage or death. 

D. SENATE BILL NO. 1766 BY SENATOR WARD 

An act to add Section 3333.5 to the Civil Code, relating to the diminution or apportionment of damages and the defense of contributory negligence. 

The people of the State of California do enact as follows: 

SECTION 1. Section 3333.5 is added to the Civil Code, to read: 

3333.5. In all actions hereafter brought either at common law or pursuant to any statute general or special to recover damages resulting from negligence for personal injury or where such injuries have resulted in death or for injury to property, the fact that the person injured or the owner of the property or person having control over the property may have been guilty of contributory negligence where that question may by law be litigated shall not bar a recovery but damages shall be diminished in proportion to the amount of negligence attributable to the person injured or to the owner of the property or the person having control over the property.

The committee held several hearings on the subject of "comparative negligence," and, in addition, made an exhaustive survey of the authorities in the field since this proposed legislation was deemed to be of major importance. In general, most of the witnesses appearing before the committee seemed to agree that some legislation should be adopted alleviating the harshness of the contributory negligence rule. However, there was no agreement as to how this should be done. Furthermore, many references were made to related problems which it was said bear upon the desirability of changing the present doctrine of contributory negligence. Chief among these was the doctrine of "last-clear chance' and the problems arising out of negligence actions where there are more than two parties involved.

3323 

II. ANALYSIS OF LEGISLATIVE COUNSEL 

At the outset the committee determined that it would be desirable to have an impartial analysis from the Legislative Counsel of the legal problems involved in the doctrine of comparative negligence, together with some suggested legislation. In response, the Legislative Counsel prepared a summary analysis of the problems which is hereinafter set forth verbatim: 

"SUMMARY OF COMPARATIVE NEGLIGENCE LAW IN UNITED STATES 

As applied at common law, the rule of comparative negligence is usually stated as follows: Though the plaintiff in an action is negligent, and even though his negligence operates concurrently with the negligence of the defendant proximately to cause injury to the plaintiff, he may recover if the degree of his negligence is slight when compared with that of the defendant (38 Am. Jur. 916). This rule of comparative negligence was adopted and followed at an early period in actions at law by the courts in some states. (Galena and C.U.R.R. v. Jacobs, 20 Ill. 478; Augusta & S.R.R.V. v. McElmurry, 24 Ga. 75; Wichita & W. R.R. v. Davis, 37 Kan. 743; McCulloch V. Horton, 105 Mont. 531; Whirley v. Whiteman, 38 Tenn. 610; Stucke v. Milwaukee & M.R.R., 9 Wis. 182.)

However, courts which previously approved and followed the comparative negligence rule have since disavowed and repudiated it, so that it is doubted whether, in the absence of statute, the rule exists in any American jurisdiction at the present time (See authorities cited in footnote 7, 22 So. Cal. L.R. 277.)

The comparative negligence rule is rejected at common law not because it is unsound as a matter of logic, technical theory, or abstract speculation, but because experience has shown that it is not practicable, since there is no yardstick to measure the relative fault of the parties. (Herrell v. St. Louis-San Francisco R. Co., 324 Mo. 38.) It is said that "a court of law cannot undertake to apportion the damages arising from an injury caused by the cooperating negligence" of each. (Menger v. Laur, 55 

N.J.L. 205.)

Although the courts have rejected the doctrine, legislatures in many states have enacted a comparative negligence rule at least with respect to limited classes of negligence actions, such as the class designated “employer's liability cases." (See Cal. Labor Code Sec. 2801; 38 Am. Jur., Sec. 233.)

Since it is presumed that your request was directed to general comparative negligence law no further mention will be made of the special laws such as employer liability statutes. 

Statutes calling for the application of a rule of comparative negligence in negligence cases generally have been adopted in six states. They are Louisiana, Georgia, Nebraska, South Dakota, Wisconsin, and Mississippi.

The statutes of these states, or cases construing them, generally provide that the contributory negligence of the plaintiff will not bar his recovery, but that the negligence of the parties shall be apportioned and recoverable damages diminished accordingly. The statutes may be classified into two general types: those which nullify contributory negligence as a bar entirely, regardless of the amount or degree of negligence

attributable to the respective parties; and those which nullify contributory negligence only if a certain amount or proportion of negligence is found to be attributable to the respective parties. 

Louisiana's comparative negligence doctrine was enacted in 1825 and has been consciously ignored by the courts of that state in rigidly adhering to the doctrine that a plaintiff's contributory negligence bars recovery. (Inman v. Silver Fleet of Memphis, et al., 175 So. 436 (La. App., 1937.) 

In Georgia, Nebraska, South Dakota, and Wisconsin, the comparative negligence statutes fall into the second classification above and have been limited, either by the terms of the act or by construction, to cases in which the plaintiff is less than 50 percent at fault. The Mississippi statute is in the first classification above, and is truly comparative, since the plaintiff's negligence is considered in relation to defendant's negligence irrespective of whether it is less than, equal to, or greater than such negligence. Under the former statutes, plaintiff's negligence is first compared with defendant's to determine whether the comparative negligence rule applies; if it does so apply because plaintiff's negligence is less than defendant's, the apportionment of damages follows and plaintiff recovers the diminished amount.

The pertinent text of these statutes is set out below with brief comments. Georgia

Georgia Code Annotated, Section 105-603, provides: 

"If the plaintiff by ordinary care could have avoided the consequences to himself caused by the defendant's negligence he is not entitled to recover. In other cases the defendant is not relieved, although the plaintiff may in some way have contributed to the injury sustained."

No comparison of negligence between plaintiff and defendant is required by the statutes; however this comparison and apportionment of damages has been supplied by court construction. The Georgia courts have also held that the rule applies only when the negligence of the plaintiff is less than that of the defendant. (Benton Rapid Express Inc., v. Sammons, 63 Ga. App. 23). Nebraska 

Revised Statutes of Nebraska, 1943, Section 25-1151, provides: "In all actions brought to recover damages for injuries to a person or to his property caused by the negilgence of another, the fact that the plaintiff may have been guilty of contributory negligence shall not bar a recovery when the contributory negligence of the plaintiff was slight and the negligence of the defendant was gross in comparison but the contributory negligence of the plaintiff shall be considered by the jury in the mitigation of damages in proportion to the amount of contributory negligence attributable to the plaintiff; and all questions of negligence and contributory negligence shall be for the jury." (Emphasis added.)

Under this rule, Nebraska courts require that the contributory negligence of the plaintiff be slight, and in addition, that the negligence of the defendant be no less than gross or great in comparison with the slight or small contributory negligence of the plaintiff.

དྲ The Nebraska courts in applying this "slight-gross rule” have held that the court may, if the facts warrant, determine whether the plaintiff's negligence was slight in comparison to the negligence of the defendant in spite of the last clause of the statute. 

South Dakota Chapter 160, Laws of South Dakota, 1941, adopts the Nebraska enact ment in haec yerba. The South Dakota courts have applied the statute in substantially the same manner as the Nebraska courts. Wisconsin

Wisconsin Statutes (1949), Section 331.045 provides: 

66 

Contributory negligence shall not bar recovery in an action by any person or his legal representative to recover damages for negligence resulting in death or in injury, to person or property, if such negligence was not as great as the negligence of the person against whom recovery is sought, but any damages allowed shall be diminished in the proportion to the amount of negligence attributable to the person recovering.' (Emphasis added.) Mississippi

Section 1354, Mississippi Code of 1942, provides: 

66 'In all actions hereafter brought for personal injuries, or where such injuries have resulted in death, or for injury to property, the fact that the person injured, or the owner of the property, or person having control over the property may have been guilty of contributory negligence shall not bar a recovery, but damages shall be diminished by the jury in proportion to the amount of negligence attributable to the person injured, or the owner of the property, or the person having control over the property." (Emphasis added.)

Under this statute, if the defendant is guilty of any negligence, however slight, causing or contributing to the plaintiff's injury, the plaintiff is entitled to recover damages no matter how gross his own negligence might have been. The purpose of comparing the negligence of the plaintiff with that of the defendant is not to resolve the issue of liability (one of the purposes of the other four statutes), but to determine the percentage of his total damage that the plaintiff is entitled to recover. 

Thus, it is theoretically possible for a plaintiff who is 99 percent negligent to recover partial damages from a defendant who is 1 percent negligent. 

In 1951, legislation was introduced in 16 states to enact general comparative negligence rules. They were as follows: Arizona, Arkansas, California, Colorado, Kansas, Massachusetts, Michigan, Missouri, New 

York, North Dakota, Ohio, Oregon, Pennsylvania, Tennessee, Utah and

Washington. (Number 344 Ins. L.J. 674.) Summary of Contributory Negligence Law in California 

In California, the negligence of the plaintiff which is a contributing cause of the injury is a defense in an action based upon negligence of the defendant. (Straten v. Spencer, 52 Cal. App. 98). The standard of due care which determines contributory negligence is the same as that which determines negligence, i. e., the conduct of a reasonably prudent person under the circumstances.

The burden of proof of contributory negligence is on the defendant (Rush v. Lagomarsino, 196 Cal. 308), and must be pleaded as an affirmative defense unless it appears on the face of the complaint or from the evidence in the plaintiff's case; but it is not an admission of negligence by the defendant. (See Hoffman v. Southern Pacific Co., 84 Cal. App. 337).

Contributory negligence, like negligence, is ordinarily a question for the jury, and becomes a question of law only when the court determines that but one interference can reasonably be drawn from the evidence. (Zibbell v. Southern Pacific Co., 160 Cal. 237).

To be a defense, the negligence of the plaintiff must be a proximate cause of the injury. (Rush v. Lagomarsino, supra.) If the plaintiff's negligence is the sole cause of his injury, of course he cannot recover; but it is not necessary to go this far. The defense of contributory negligence is available even though the defendant was negligent and his negligence was a legal cause of the injury if the negligence of plaintiff concurs as a legal cause, i.e., proximately contributes in some degree. (Straten v. Spencer, supra.)

Comparative Negligence Doctrine Rejected. The concept of comparative negligence as limiting or nullifying the effect of contributory negligence as a bar to recovery has been rejected in California, at least in general negligence situations. (Solko v. Jones, 117 Cal. App. 372.) Under 

Labor Code Section 2801, the contributory negligence of an employee will not bar recovery in an action against a negligent employer if the plaintiff's negligence was slight and that of the employer gross in comparison; but the plaintiff's damages may be diminished by the jury in proportion to his negligence.

Contributory Negligence Per Se. The rules governing contributory negligence per se or negligence as a matter of law, are the same as those governing negligence per se. (See Meincke v. Oakland Garage, Inc., 11 

Cal. 2d. 255.)

Imputed Contributory Negligence. The principles governing imputed contributory negligence determine imputed contributory negligence. A person's contributory negligence will be imputed to the plaintiff so as to bar recovery only in situations where the plaintiff would have been liable for that person's negligence. (See Bryant v. Pac. Elec. Ry. Co., 174 Cal. 

737.) The rule of imputed negligence does not apply to guests; i.e., a guest in a vehicle, including a person jointly engaged with the driver in a pleasure excursion, without a common business purpose, is not affected by the contributory negligence of the driver. (Pope v. Halpern, 193 Cal. 168.)

The relationship of parent and child or husband and wife in themselves furnish no basis for imputation of contributory negligence. (Zarzana v. 

Neve Drug Co., 180 Cal. 32; see also Bowen v. Kirzirian, 105 Cal. App. 

286.) There are two important exceptions to this general rule: (a) where a parent sues for damages for loss of services of the child, the parent's negligence in permitting the child to play in dangerous places on the street will bar his recovery. (Seperman v. Lyon, etc. Co. 97 Cal. App. 

654.) (b) where a husband is guilty of contributing negligence, recovery for the wife's injuries is denied on the ground that the recovery is community property. (Zaragosa v. Craven, 33 Cal. 2d. 315.) The wife may,

however, recover for consequential damages such as medical expenses. (Louie v. Hagstrom's Food Stores, 81 Cal. App. 2d., 601, 615.)

Last Clear Chance Doctrine. The doctrine of "last clear chance" is an exception to the rule of contributory negligence, and allows recovery in spite of the plaintiff's negligence, if the defendant had the last clear chance to avoid the injury. The California rule has been stated as follows: where the plaintiff, by his own negligence, has placed himself in a position of danger from which he cannot extricate himself, or of which he is obviously unconscious, and the defendant, seeing and knowing his peril or facts from which a reasonable man would believe him to be in peril, and being able by use of ordinary care to avoid injuring the plaintiff fails to use such care, the defendant will be liable. (Arnold v. S.F., etc. Ry., 175 Cal. 1.) The California doctrine is applied to allow recovery by the plaintiff only when the defendant is actually aware of the facts which indicate the dangerous situation of the plaintiff. This limited rule of last clear chance is sometimes called the doctrine of "discovered peril." (See 

Starck v. Pac. Elec. Ry. Co., 172 Cal. 277.)

Discussion Relating to California Comparative Negligence Legislation. 

A brief review of the statutes relating to comparative negligence in effect in other states leads us to believe that we should become more thoroughly acquainted with your desires before we undertake the drafting of a comparative negligence rule for California. As noted in the brief summary of these statutes, they fall into two general classifications, i.e., those which permit apportionment in cases where the plaintiff is up to 99 percent negligent.

While it is possible to draft and forward alternative bills taking into consideration the above variations, there are several other problems which should be considered in order to draft an effective statute. 

Mr. Charles O. Gregory, in his book entitled, Legislative Loss Distribution in Negligence Actions, (1936), notes that all of the existing statutes in the United States fail to provide an adequate rule for the negligence action involving more than two parties. While California procedural rules liberally allow joinder of parties and causes of action arising out of the same transaction, the substantive rule refusing contribution among tortfeasors (except in nuisance cases) would illogically delimit the operation of any comparative negligence rule applied in a case involving two or more defendants. The resistance to comparative negligence rules and contribution among tortfeasors alike is based on the reluctance of courts to help a party who was himself "in the wrong." In a multipleparty action under a comparative negligence statute all parties are "in the wrong" and for this reason the legal writers advocate an extension of the comparative negligence rule to include contribution among tortfeasors. (See Gregory, Legislative Loss Distribution in Negligence Actions (1936); Prosser, Handbook of the Law of Torts, (1941), Section 

53; Campbell, 10 Years of Comparative Negligence, (1941), Wis. L. 

Rev. 289.)

Whether or not contribution among tortfeasors is allowed in connection with a comparative negligence rule, the problem of insolvency of one or more defendants in a multiple-party case should be considered. 

If contribution among tortfeasors is allowed, an allocation of negligence and loss is made, and the plaintiff executes a joint and several judgment

against one defendant, the latter may join the other defendant and receive a judgment requiring contribution. If the other defendant is insolvent, the burden of this loss is upon the defendant who paid plaintiff's damages. If, on the other hand, contribution among tortfeasors is not allowed, but plaintiff takes a several judgment, he may execute for the apportioned amount against each defendant but will not recover the total amount unless all are solvent. 

The question raised by the above situation is whether the risk of insolvency should be borne by the plaintiff or by the solvent defendant or whether it should be distributed between them equally or in ratio of their respective degrees of fault. 

There are certain other procedural aspects which should be considered in drafting a comparative negligence statute. One of these pertains to the desirability of requiring that all interested parties join an action to permit an allocation of negligence and loss among all the parties by the same jury. Section 389 of the Code of Civil Procedure does not appear to require joinder except in regard to parties necessary to a complete determination of the controversy between plaintiff and defendant. 

A system of loss distribution which seeks to solve the multiple-party cases on an equitable basis should provide for special verdicts or findings. 

The confusion which would result from any other course seems apparent when one considers the number of interdependent claims that would have to be settled in an action involving three or more negligent parties and the negligence of each contributed, in varying percentages, to the damage of the others. While the California courts are empowered to require such special findings (Section 625, C. C. P.), it would seem advisable to especially provide for them in a proposed statute. 

The above brief comments do not purport to suggest all of the problems which should be considered in drafting a whole statute on the subject of comparative negligence; rather, they are intended to show that the existing legislation in other states has been criticized by legal writers as being inadequate and to elicit your views as to the type of statute which you desire drafted. 

Very truly yours, RALPH N. KLEPS Legislative Counsel By WILLIAM D. HEEKIN, Deputy" III. REPORT OF THE CONFERENCE COMMITTEE OF THE STATE BAR OF CALIFORNIA EX 

The Conference of State Bar Delegates of the State Bar has had under discussion and study for some time the proposed adoption in California of the doctrine of "Comparative Negligence." The committee has recently been advised that the State Bar through action of its Board of Governors has officially approved this doctrine. Accordingly, a report of a 

Committee of Conference of State Bar Delegates analyzing the problems and suggesting certain legislation in connection therewith is hereinafter set forth for the general information of the Legislature and others interested in this matter.

"REPORT OF CONFERENCE COMMITTEE ON COMPARATIVE NEGLIGENCE (1951 Conference Resolutions Nos. 8 and 19) 1. History of the Committee 

At the 1951 annual meeting of the conference, the above two resolutions relating to comparative negligence were considered, as was the report to the Board of Governors of the State Bar of a committee of the Conference on Comparative Negligence. At the conclusion of the discussion the conference took the following action : 

1. The matter of comparative negligence was referred to this committee "with specific instructions," not further defined. 

2. The conference declared that it favors the principle of comparative negligence. 

3. A motion to direct the committee to study only comparative negligence was defeated. Presumably this motion was directed at the fact that the report of the committee that was then before the conference discussed also the subject of contribution among joint tort feasors. 

2. Scope of Committee's Responsibility 

The committee has had three general meetings, two at San Francisco and one at Los Angeles. At its first general meeting the committee concluded that its primary responsibility was to present to the 1952 meeting of the conference of State Bar delegates a statute amending the law of California so as to embody in it the rule of comparative negligence. This the committee believes to be the meaning of the "specific instructions" given it. The committee felt that by reason of the action of the conference, it was free to consider such related topics as contribution among joint tort feasors, third party practice, and perhaps other matters, but that it was not required to do so, its primary duty being the preparation of a comparative negligence statute.

Three subcommittees were appointed to study the problem further: 

A southern subcommittee, of which Mr. E. Briggs Howorth of Los

Angeles was chairman, and of which James Focht, San Diego; Clarence Runkle, Los Angeles; Raoul D. Magana, Los Angeles; Joseph A. 

Ball, Long Beach, and Robert R. Willard, Ventura, were members;

A Central Valley Committee, of which James A. Cobey, Merced, was chairman, and James K. Barnum, Fresno, and Patrick McCarthy, Sacramento, were members; and

A Bay area committee of which William S. Parrish, Oakland was chairman, and Ben C. Duniway, San Francisco; Ingemar Hoberg, San 

Francisco, and John F. Moran, San Francisco, were members.

The subcommittees were instructed by the general committee that the statute to be proposed was to be a full comparative negligence statute, and was not to contain the limitations appearing in the Wisconsin statute, which provides that a plaintiff may recover only if his negligence is "less than" that of the defendant. All but two of the members of the committee concur in this view. It seems to us that the Wisconsin type of statute leaves the rule that contributory negligence is a complete bar to recovery at least fifty percent in effect, and is not, therefore, a true comparative negligence statute.

In preparing its draft the committee had before it a history of State Bar proceedings relating to the doctrine of comparative negligence, going back to 1940, the report of the committee on comparative negligence

to the conference of 1950, the report of the same committee to the Board of Governors of 1951, resolutions Nos. 8 and 19 presented to the 1951 conference, the draft of a statute embodied in the 1951 committee report, a draft of a proposed statute prepared by Dean William L. Prosser of the Law School of the University of California, and the following statutes: The Federal Employers Liability Act, The Wisconsin Statute, The English Statute, known as The Law Reform (Contributory Negli gence) Act, 1945, and The English Act relating to third party practice and contribution among joint tort feasors known as "The Law Reform (married women and tort feasors) Act, 1935," The Ontario Statute, Chapter 115, revised statutes of Ontario, 1937, and the Con tributory Negligence Act of Alberta, Chapter 18 of 1937. The commit tee also had an opportunity to examine and study an exhaustive article on the subject prepared by Dean Prosser, but not yet released for publication. We also were able to have Dean Prosser attend our last general meeting at San Francisco, to discuss the draft of a statute. We wish to take this opportunity to express to Dean Prosser our appreciation of his cooperation and assistance, which was most helpful to us. 

3. Matters Considered, But Eliminated 

A. Contribution Among Joint Tort Feasors. All members of the committee recognize that the problem of contribution among joint tort feasors is in some ways related to the problem of comparative negligence. However, the majority of the committee feels that it is essentially a separate problem. It is a problem that now exists. It is a problem that the State Bar has studied from time to time, when the matter of comparative negligence was not even under consideration. Many lawyers believe that there should be a statute providing for contribution among joint tort feasors, whether the doctrine of comparative negligence is adopted or not. 

According to Dean Prosser, some 18 states now have statutes on the subject, and two more allow contribution among joint tort feasors without a statute. The commissioners on Uniform State Laws have withdrawn the uniform act promulgated by them some years ago, and are now studying a revision. Dean Prosser is a member of the revising committee, which has already met with attorneys and others representing the insurance industry to discuss various problems involved. There have been a number of objections to the Uniform Act, and of the few states that have adopted it, several have amended it extensively. The principal objections have related to the effect of the act upon releases and the statute of limitations. There has also been some dispute about third party practice. And all but seven of the states having a statute on the subject permit proceedings for contribution only after a joint judgment has been recovered against the joint tort feasors. It thus appears that the matter is now under intensive study and that there are serious problems involved, quite apart from the matter of comparative negligence. 

The majority of the committee believes that the subject is of sufficient importance, and sufficiently difficult, so that it would be a mistake to combine a statute on contribution among joint feasors with a statute

Ed 51 on comparative negligence. If a comparative negligence statute is adopted, and if the rule denying contribution among joint tort feasors remains in effect, joint tort feasors will be no worse off than they now are. 

Two members of the committee believe that the State Bar will not be doing a complete job in the field of comparative negligence unless it also sponsors an act providing for contribution among joint tort feasors. 

The committee is unanimous in recommending to the conference that it study the desirability of adopting a statute providing for contribution among joint tort feasors, and that a special conference committee be created with instructions to report on the subject to the next session of the conference. 

B. Third Party Practice. The majority of the committee believes that the present rules in California for joinder of parties, for cross complaint and counter-claim, and for bringing in new parties, will take care of most of the problems that will arise if a comparative negligence statute is adopted. The majority also feels that this subject, like the subject of contribution among joint tort feasors, is really a separate subject and one which could well be studied, whether or not a comparative negligence statute is adopted. If there are now undesirable gaps in third party practice rules in this State, they exist now and will continue to exist, whether or not the rule of comparative negligence is adopted. 

Three members of the committee believe that the statutes relating to third party practice should be amended so as to permit or require the bringing into one action of every party to an accident or occurrence giving rise to a claim for damages for negligence, as otherwise there may be cases in which the rule of comparative negligence will work an injustice because not all parties contributing to the damage can be brought before the court.

The committee is unanimous in recommending to the conference that the subject of third party practice, especially as it relates to the rule of comparative negligence, should be studied by the conference, and that a special committee of the conference should be appointed for this purpose, with directions to report to the next session of the conference. It may well be that the same committee could study both contribution among joint tort-feasors and third party practice.

C. Apportionment of Costs. It was suggested that if a comparative negligence statute is adopted the code should be amended so as to permit the court to apportion costs in cases in which the doctrine of comparative negligence is applied. A majority of the committee feels however, that this is unnecessary and perhaps undersirable. 

4. Special Problems That the Committee Believes Should Be Covered 

A. Special Verdicts. In Wisconsin and in one or two other states, in 

Canada and in England, special verdicts are required in cases where the doctrine of comparative negligence applies. It is apparently felt in those jurisdictions that without a special verdict neither the trial court on motion for a new trial nor the appellate court in reviewing the matter has any means whereby it can intelligibly determine whether the jury actually followed the court's instructions on comparative negligence.

In some of these jurisdictions the special interrogatories put to the jury go into great detail regarding the cause of the accident, the amount of negligence attributable to each party, etc. A majority of the committee does not favor any statute which would require detailed special interrogatories of this type, feeling that such a requirement would confuse the jury and be a prolific source of error. On the other hand, a majority of the committee also feels that a special verdict or finding of a very simple character can be required and that if it is required it will be genuinely helpful both to the trial court and to the appellate court in passing on a motion for a new trial or an appeal. The special verdict required in 

England is the simplest type, and this is the type of verdict that the committee recommends. It is believed that if this type of special verdict, and no other, is required, juries will not be confused, and the court will have a basis for appraising the action of the jury intelligently. 

A substantial minority of the committee believes that a different form of provision for special verdict should be embodied in the statute. If the form that they desire were embodied in the proposed statute, it would read substantially as follows: 

"(c) In any action to which subsection (b) of this section applies, if the trier of fact renders a special verdict or makes findings of fact, the following shall be determined: (1) The total amount of the damages sustained by each party claiming damages; and (2) Whose negligence has contributed proximately to such damages; and (3) The proportionate amount of negligence of each party claiming damages which proximately contributed to such damages, expressed in percentages. 

There is also a substantial minority of the committee that feels that no special verdict at all should be required. They consider the requirement unnecessary, perhaps confusing, and a possible source of error. 

B. Doctrine of Last Clear Chance. The majority of the committee believes that this doctrine was developed by the courts to mitigate the harshness of the law of contributory negligence. In this State, the rule is judge-made, and is expressed by the courts in terms of proximate cause. 

The majority of the committee believes, however, that in fact, as the rule has been applied in recent cases, the statement that the negligence of the party having the last clear chance is the sole proximate cause of the injury and that the negligence of the other party is not a proximate cause of the injury, is fictitious. No member of the committee desires to abolish the ordinary rules of proximate cause. A party should not have his recovery diminished if his negligence did not in any way cause the accident. 

A majority of the committee feels that in most cases to which the doctrine of last clear chance is applied, the negligence of the party not having the last clear chance was in fact a cause of the injury, and that if a negligent party is to be permitted to recover under the rule of comparative negligence, the doctrine of last clear chance as it exists in California should be abolished. Accordingly the draft of the statute that we present does abolish the doctrine.

5. Recommendations 1 it 

A. Comparative Negligence Statute. The committee recommends that the conference recommended to the Board of Governors that the following statute be approved by the State Bar and made a part of its legislative program: 

"Section 1714 of the Civil Code is hereby amended to read as follows: 

"1714. Responsibility for Wilful Acts, Negligence, etc. 

66 (a) Everyone is responsible, not only for the result of his wilful acts, but also for an injury occasioned to another by his want of ordinary care or skill in the management of his property or person, except so far as the latter has; wilfully or by want of ordinary eare, brought the injury upon himself. The extent of liability in such cases is defined in subsection (b) of this section and by the title on compensatory relief. 

(b) In all actions for damages for personal injuries or for wrongful death or for injury to property, including those in which the defendant has had the last clear chance to avoid the injury, the contributory negligence of the person injured, or of the deceased, or of the owner of the property, or of the person having control over the property, shall not bar a recovery, but the damages awarded shall be diminished in proportion to the amount of negligence attributable to said injured person or to said deceased or to the owner of the property or to the person having control over the property. ‘(c) In any action to which subsection (b) of this section applies, the court shall make findings of fact or the jury shall render a special verdict which shall state: (1) the total damages which would have been recoverable if the person injured or the deceased or the owner of the property or the person having control of the property had not been negligent and (2) the extent to which those damages are to be diminished. 66 "Section 625 of the Code of Civil Procedure is hereby amended to read as follows: 

"625. In an action for the recovery of money only, or specific real property, the jury, in their discretion, may render a general or special verdict, except that in an action to which subsection (b) of Section 1714 of the Civil Code applies, the jury shall render a special verdict as provided in subsection (c) of that section. In all other cases the court may direct the jury to find a special verdict in writing, upon all, or any of the issues, and in all cases may instruct them, if they render a general verdict, to find upon particular questions of fact, to be stated in writing, and may direct a written finding thereon. The special verdict or finding must be filed with the clerk and entered upon the minutes. Where a special finding of facts is inconsistent with the general verdict, the former controls the latter, and the court must give judgment accordingly." (Matter stricken is to be deleted; new language is italic.) 

B. Further Study. The committee recommends that the matter of contribution among joint tort-feasors and the matter of third party practice in relation to negligence cases be studied by the Conference of State Bar Delegates, and that a special committee or committees be appointed for

those purposes and directed to report thereon to the next meeting of the Conference of State Bar Delegates. 

Respectfully submitted,

BEN. C. DUNIWAY, Chairman JAMES FOCHT, Vice Chairman INGEMAR HOBERG WILLIAM S. PARRISH JAMES A. COBEY JAMES K. BARNUM PATRICK MCCARTHY CLARENCE Runkle E. BRIGGS HOWORTH RAOUL D. MAGANA JOSEPH A. BALL JOHN F. MORAN ROBERT R. WILLARD" IV. REPORT OF THE CALIFORNIA STATE CHAMBER OF COMMERCE 

The California State Chamber of Commerce is officially in opposition to the adoption in California of the doctrine of "Comparative Negligence." Its Legal Research Department submitted to the committee a comprehensive analysis of the laws, cases and problems arising from the doctrine which it is believed is so complete and of such interest to the members of the Legislature and of the Bar as to warrant its being reprinted in full and attached as Appendix C to this Report. 

V. ARGUMENTS IN FAVOR OF THE ADOPTION OF THE DOCTRINE OF "COMPARATIVE NEGLIGENCE" IN CALIFORNIA 

The following article by Richard P. Oliver, Esq., presents additional arguments in favor of the adoption of the comparative negligence rule in California. Accordingly, the same is printed in full. The article by the 

Honorable William J. Palmer to which Mr. Oliver refers, although it contains many carefully reasoned arguments against the adoption of the rule, is not reprinted, since it is considered that the case against the adoption of the rule is fully covered by the brief submitted by the California 

State Chamber of Commerce. (Appendix C) "LET US BE FRANK ABOUT COMPARATIVE NEGLIGENCE By Richard L. Oliver * 

(Reprinted from the Los Angeles Bar Bulletin.) 

In a recent article entitled, "Let Us Be Frank About Comparative Negligence," the Honorable William J. Palmer raised various questions and objections to the proposed comparative negligence law which was adopted by the Conference of State Bar Delegates at its last convention.

Judge Palmer is one of our ablest jurists. His questions and objections are entitled to thoughtful consideration by the Bar and to frank and * Mr. Oliver, a Los Angeles attorney, is President of the Los Angeles Chapter of the 

National Association of Claimants and Compensation Attorneys.

complete answers from those who believe we should have a comparative negligence law.

The adoption of comparative negligence by the Conference of State Bar Delegates was not a sudden impulsive act. On the contrary, it reflects the deliberate and almost unanimous vote of the delegates which was taken after years of study in previous conferences and in special committees assigned to the subject. 

I speak for an association of attorneys who represent injured people in negligence cases and who endorse the progressive action of the Conference of State Bar Delegates. We wish to help bring about this longneeded reform. The present article, of course, does not presume to speak for the Conference of State Bar Delegates, or for any individual delegate. 

Judge Palmer's arguments are not new. Opponents of comparative negligence have made similar arguments for years to previous conferences of delegates and to the special study committees. 

Whether comparative negligence is inevitable may be a matter of opinion. That there is a steady trend toward it is a fact. In 1945 comparative negligence was adopted in England, the original home of contributory negligence. Incidentally, not one of the alarming consequences feared by Judge Palmer from the adoption of comparative negligence has occurred in England during the last seven years. 

Comparative negligence is also the law of Canada, Australia, New Zealand, France, Germany, Spain, Italy, Norway, and Sweden. It has been the law of Admiralty for centuries. A few years ago, Congress adopted it in the Federal Employers' Liability Act. Some states have adopted it either completely, as in Mississippi, or in a modified form, as in Nebraska, South Dakota, and Wisconsin. Bills providing for comparative negligence are being considered by the legislatures of various states, including California. Many able jurists, such as Dean Prosser of the University of California, favor comparative negligence.

This trend towards comparative negligence is in response to the basic fact that for our modern automobile age the law of contributory negligence has been tried and found wanting. Perhaps it could cope with the occasional tort that arose in the horse and buggy age when the doctrine was born. But the injustices caused by this harsh doctrine are flagrantly exposed by the automobile which kills thousands and maims hundreds of thousands in America each year. More Americans were killed or injured by automobiles during the last 50 years than were killed or injured during both World Wars.

We have no adequate system to provide for this enormous number of civilian automobile accident casualties. Instead, we continue to use an archaic contributory negligence law, which was never designed for the automobile age, and which denies any relief whatever to great numbers. of accident victims. Ofttimes, these unfortunates, together with their families, exhaust their savings and are thrown upon relief agencies or public charities. The social and economic havoc thus created cries out for legal reform with abrogation of such a barbaric doctrine. 

We believe comparative negligence would substantially solve the economic and social problems created by the accidents of the automobile age. Furthermore, its adoption would not change our judicial structure or interfere with our present free enterprise system of private insurance. 3-L-3870

One argument made against comparative negligence is based upon the fear that its adoption might cause a large increase of insurance rates. This argument overlooks the fact that insurance rates are now determined almost entirely by the comparative negligence principle. 

This is true because over 95 percent of all claims are now settled before decision by a court or jury, and every lawyer knows that in settlement negotiations we compare negligence and compromise liability. Such settlements are comparable to amounts that would be paid in settlements under comparative negligence. It is only after trial that the amount is either all or nothing. Inasmuch as more than 95 percent of the insurance dollar is now paid out on the comparative negligence principle, the use of comparative negligence for all cases would have only slight effect upon the total amount paid out by insurance companies and the resultant insurance rate.

This analysis is substantiated by the actual insurance rates in Wisconsin, where a modified comparative negligence law has been in effect for years. At present, the highest basic liability insurance rate for 5 and 

10 limits in Wisconsin is $41 as compared to $45 for the same highest basic rate in California where contributory negligence prevails. If comparative negligence contains a serious threat to insurance rates, would this not have been reflected noticeably in the Wisconsin rate during the past 15 years? Furthermore, the adoption of comparative negligence by

England in 1945 has not caused a noticeable change of insurance rates. 

Opponents of comparative negligence contend that its adoption would increase litigation. However, the experience of Wisconsin and of England under comparative negligence proves exactly the contrary and shows that it tends to decrease litigation. The probable explanation is that under comparative negligence, the opposing parties start settlement negotiations from closer starting points than exist under contributory negligence, and thus the chances of getting together on settlement without trial are proportionately increased. 

It is also argued that "the teeniest-weeniest bit of negligence" does not defeat recovery and that, instead, it is the slightest contribution of any ordinary negligence as a proximate cause of the accident which bars recovery. As attorneys, we know that technically this is true, but we also know that, as a practical matter, juries do apply the "teeniest-weeniest bit of negligence" reasoning. Insurance attorneys can still argue such examples as the drop of ink in a glass of water, or the drop of bluing in a pail of water, or the dot of chalk within a large circle on the blackboard, and juries do not distinguish between the slightest amount of ordinary negligence and the slightest contribution of such negligence as a proximate cause of the accident. Even Judge Palmer in his examples falls into the same mental habit by referring to a driver as being 10 percent to blame for an accident without the further refinement regarding proximate cause.

It is also urged that comparative negligence would cause an increase in the padding of claims. It is difficult to attach much weight to such a contention. Of course, there are a dishonest few who now try to pad claims under contributory negligence and who would do the same under comparative negligence. However, the average person would not stoop to such a practice under comparative negligence, any more than he does

now under contributory. Under either system, honest people would remain honest, and crooks would remain crooks.

It is said that an attorney employed on a contingent fee arrangement has a personal interest in the outcome of the case. Practically all attorneys have been employed at times under such contracts and know from personal experience that they give such clients services equal in quality, dignity, and understanding to those rendered all other clients. Furthermore, it is difficult to see how such a client risks "nothing or little, having nothing to lose and everything to gain," unless we are also prepared to to say that medical expenses, hospital expenses, loss of wages, and pain and suffering amount to "nothing or little." 

A novel argument advanced is that comparative negligence might cause motorists to become more reckless. This argument apparently assumes that motorists now exercise more caution for fear they might not recover damages if contributorily negligent. Just why a driver would be negligent and invite injury to himself in order to collect only a part of his damages under comparative negligence has yet to be explained. If this argument is sound, then we should revoke the drivers' licenses of all people who carry large health and accident insurance policies, for fear they may become reckless in order to collect on their insurance. 

Pursuing this line of reasoning further, would not comparative negligence encourage more careful driving on our highways since each motorist would know he would be held responsible for the consequences of his own careless misconduct regardless of the conduct of anyone else. 

Today, under contributory negligence, he can ofttimes drive carelessly with impunity by showing that someone else was also careless. Is this conducive to cautious driving?

In criminal law, we do not permit a robber to escape responsibility for his crime by showing that an accomplice assisted him in the robbery. 

Yet, today, drunk driver can run down an old lady in a crosswalk and then escape all responsibility to her by showing that even though the old lady had the right of way she should have taken a second or third look and gotten out of the drunk driver's way. We are told that "the countenance of the law is indeed stern and purposeful" and that this result is necessary to maintain the "moral fibre" of our people. 

Presumably, this means the "moral fibre" of the old lady who was run down in the crosswalk. We who believe in comparative negligence are also concerned in doing something about the "moral fibre" of the drunk driver and of other negligent drivers who at present escape all responsibility for their wrongful conduct. Why should not the "countenance of the law be stern and purposeful" toward both parties to an accident instead of toward the victim alone?

It is also argued that comparative negligence would operate to the financial advantage of attorneys who specialize in personal injury work. 

There is good reason for believing it would do just the reverse. Contributory negligence creates a litigation hazard so serious that the legal services of personal injury specialists are in demand. Many general practitioners associate such specialists for their personal injury cases. 

If the hazard of contributory negligence is removed through adoption of comparative negligence, the general practitioner will tend to handle his own personal injury cases the same as any other part of his practice and the specialist will no longer be in such demand. Furthermore, no

attorney would benefit unless his client, the accident victim, received a greater benefit.

However, this argument diverts attention from the real question, which is whether comparative negligence or contributory negligence is best fitted to serve society in the present automobile age. We should endorse the one which is best for society as a whole without regard to its financial effect upon the individual attorney, or the Bar as a whole. 

It is argued that comparative negligence would be hard to administer because it is difficult to precisely apportion such an indefinite thing as negligence. This argument apparently ignores the fact that our present day juries who sit on Federal Employers' Liability cases find no such difficulty in apportioning liability under comparative negligence. If we follow this line of reasoning, then we should abolish all claims for unliquidated damages, and all claims for pain and suffering whether past, present, or future, because they are so indefinite and difficult to precisely evaluate.

Judge Palmer argues that "No one yet has expressed or even conceived a reason why John Jones, who was chiefly to blame for an accident that would not have happened if he had exercised only ordinary care, should have a cause of action against anyone else involved in the accident." 

If this blanket assertion is correct, then it would appear that no reason has ever been expressed or even conceived for all of the laws that provide for such causes of action in Admiralty, under Federal Employers' Liability, in the State of Mississippi, and in the countries of England, Canada, Australia, New Zealand, France, Germany, Spain, Italy, Norway and Sweden!

Opponents of comparative negligence argue that a person who is mostly to blame for an accident should not recover anything from one who is less to blame. An example is cited where you are 90 percent to blame and suffer $100,000 in damages in an accident where I am 10 percent to blame. Under contributory negligence you get nothing from me, but under comparative negligence, I owe you $10,000.

Let us analyze this example that is considered so terrible by those who wish to retain contributory negligence. Now, in order to have been damaged to the extent of $100,000 you undoubtedly have suffered horrible injuries such as blindness, or perhaps the loss of both hands, or of both feet, or you have a broken back with serious permanent disability. Under comparative negligence, you must bear 90 percent or $90,000 of this loss yourself. You desperately need to be paid the $10,000 damages which 

I caused to you in order to get medical and hospital attention. When you stand $90,000 of the loss, haven't you been penalized enough for your own negligence without also being forced to stand the additional $10,000 loss which I caused you? Why should I be permitted to walk away without paying for the damage I have caused to you? Is it not just to require that each person should pay damages in the same proportion that he is to blame in causing those damages? 

Now let us turn the example around. You receive the same terrible injuries and $100,000 in damages in an accident where you are 10 percent to blame and I am 90 percent to blame. Under comparative negligence you stand 10 percent of the loss or $10,000 which you caused, and I pay to you $90,000 or 90 percent of the $100,000 damage which I caused. 

But, under the present barbaric contributory negligence law, you must

stand the entire $100,000 in damages and I go carefree of any responsibility even though I was 90 percent to blame and I caused $90,000 of your $100,000 loss. Is it really necessary that we preserve such a travesty of so-called justice in order to preserve the "moral fibre" of accident victims? Such a savage result may resemble a game or an athletic contest to some people, but to a bread winner with a broken back, or to a surviving widow with children, it is inhuman. 

The thousands of victims of this injustice in our courts are unorganized, silent and helpless. Their only voices are the attorneys who speak in their behalf. We do so sincerely, earnestly and without apology." 

VI. RECOMMENDATIONS AND CONCLUSIONS 

The committee is of the opinion that the doctrine of "comparative negligence" should be adopted in California, and in this connection recommends the following statute:

A. Recommended Statute 

An act to amend Section 1714 of; and to add Section 1714.

1 to, the Civil 

Code, relating to contributory AND SECTION 625 OF THE CODE OF CIVIL PROCEDURE, RELATING TO COMPARATIVE 

negligence.

The people of the State of California do enact as follows: 

SECTION 1. Section 1714 of the Civil Code is amended to read: 

1714. Everyone is responsible, not only for the result of his wilful acts, but also for an injury occasioned to another by his want of ordinary care or skill in the management of his property or person, except so far as the latter has wilfully brought the injury upon himself. The extent of liability in such cases is defined by Section 1714.1 of this eode in subsection (b) of this section and by the title on compensatory relief. (b) In all actions for damages for personal injuries or for wrongful death or for injury to property, including those in which the defendant has had the last clear chance to avoid the injury, the contributory negligence of the person injured, or of the deceased, or of the owner of the property, or of the person having control over the property, shall not bar a recovery, but the damages awarded shall be diminished in proportion to the amount of negligence attributable to said injured person or to said deceased or to the owner of the property or to the person having control over the property. (c) In any action to which subsection (b) of this section applies, the court shall make findings of fact or the jury shall render a special verdict which shall state: (1) the total damages which would have been recoverable if the person injured or the deceased or the owner of the property or the person having control of the property had not been negligent and (2) the extent to which those damages are to be diminished. 

SEO. 2. Section 1714.1 is added to said code, to read: 

1714.1. Where any person suffers injury or damage, or where such injury or damage has resulted in death, as the result partly of his own fault and partly of the fault of any other person or persons, a claim in respect of that injury, damage or death shall not be defeated by reason of the fault of the person suffering the injury, damage or death, but the

damages recoverable in respect thereof shall be reduced to such an extent as the trier of facts find just and equitable having regard to the share of the claimant or decedent as the case may be in the responsibility for the injury, damage or death.

SEC. 2. Section 625 of the Code of Civil Procedure is amended to 

625. In an action for the recovery of money only, or specific real property, the jury, in their discretion, may render a general or special verdict, except that in an action to which subsection (b) of Section 1714 of the Civil Code applies, the jury shall render a special verdict as provided in subsection (c) of that section. In all other cases the court may direct the jury to find a special verdict in writing, upon all, or any of the issues, and in all cases may instruct them, if they render a general verdict, to find upon particular questions of fact, to be stated in writing, and may direct a written finding thereon. The special verdict or finding must be filed with the clerk and entered upon the minutes. Where a special finding of facts is inconsistent with the general verdict, the former controls the latter, and the court must give judgment accordingly.
