C. Federal Employer's Liability Act 

The federal rule on contributary negligence as it relates to employer's liability reads as follows: 

Tit. 45 Sect. 53 USCA 

Contributory negligence; diminution of damages

In all actions hereafter brought against any such common carrier by railroad under or by virtue of any of the provisions of this chapter to recover damages for personal injuries to an employee, or where such injuries have resulted in his death, the fact that the employee may have been guilty of contributory negligence shall not bar a recovery, but the damages shall be diminished by the jury in proportion to the amount of negligence attributable to such employee: provided, that no such employee who may be injured or killed shall be held to have been guilty of contributory negligence in any case where the violation by such common carrier of any statute enacted for the safety of employees contributed to the injury or death of such employee. Apr. 22, 1908, c. 149, Sect. 3, 35 Stat. 66.

RETIREMENT OF JUDGES I. THE INQUIRY 

The committee also considered the subject of judges' retirement. Two hearings were had at which representatives of the Conference of California Judges appeared. The information presented to the interim committee by the legislative committee of the conference was predicated upon a study made by Mr. Justice Wood of the District Court of Appeal. The basic features of various public retirement systems now in operation in this State, as well as those of other jurisdictions, were included in the study for such suggestive value as they might have in the study of the 

California Judges Retirement Law (Deering's Act 4849a). From these discussions it became readily apparent that such retirement act had not kept pace with other retirement statutes insofar as the protection of widows is concerned.

Illustrative of the inequitable condition which now obtains in the Judges Retirement Act: if a judge were to retire today and if he were to die tomorrow, his widow would receive nothing—not even the amount which the judge had contributed to the retirement fund. The committee feels that the obvious unfairness of such legislation should be corrected. 

To remedy this situation two proposals were made by the judges conference: (1) amend the present act to provide for a specific percentage of the judge's retirement allowance to be paid to his widow for the remainder of her life, or (2) amend the present act to include desirable provisions now contained in other retirement systems. 

The first proposal appears to be subject to some debate. Although it would increase the disbursements from the fund, it is unlikely it would impair the solvency of the fund. However, in view of the small number of judges who have retired since the inception of the act, there does not appear to be sufficient information now available which the committee believed could be used as a basis for any valid conclusion at this session of the Legislature. Hence, it is recommended that no action be taken on this proposal at this time.

II. CONCLUSIONS 

It is, therefore, the conclusion of the committee that the second proposal is the only practical step that can now be taken and that this proposal should be adopted at the 1953 Session of the Legislature. Under such provisions the retired member is given the option of providing for his surviving spouse by taking less than his normal retirement pay (expressed as the "actuarial equivalent"), and the unused portion, if any, is then paid to the widow. Thus, consideration is given to the widow without increased demands on the retirement fund. 

It was also brought to the attention of the committee that the present Judges Retirement Act had not been brought in line with the retirement provisions relative to constitutional officers. In particular, no provision ( 76 )

is made for those whose service as a judge is discontinued other than by death or retirement, nor is provision made for any change which might occur in the salary of the office which the retired judge last held. 

III. RECOMMENDATIONS 

The committee, therefore, recommends the adoption of the following bill: 

SENATE BILL No. 1487 

Introduced by Senators Busch, Tenney, O'Gara, Way, and Desmond 

January 16, 1953 

Referred to Committee on Governmental Efficiency

An act to amend Sections 2 and 4 of the Judges' Retirement Act (Chapter 

770 of the Statutes of 1937), and to add Sections 1.8, 4.5, 5.1, 5.2, and 

5.3 thereto, and to amend Sections 75032 and 75061 of, and to add 

Section 75033 to, the Government Code, and to add Article 3.5, comprising Sections 75070 to 75073, inclusive, to Chapter 11 of Title 8 thereof, relating to the retirement of judges in respect to the nature, amount, and manner of payment of benefits to and in respect to retired judges.

The people of the State of California do enact as follows: 

SECTION 1. Section 1.8 is added to the Judges' Retirement Act, to read: 

Sec. 1.8. Notwithstanding any other provision of this act or of the

Judges' Retirement Fund Act, if the service of a judge is discontinued by any means other than death or retirement pursuant to this act, he shall have the right to elect within ninety (90) days thereafter, and without right of revocation, whether to allow his accumulated contributions to remain in the fund. Failure to make such election shall be deemed an irrevocable election to withdraw his accumulated contributions. A judge who so elects to allow his accumulated contributions to remain in the fund shall, upon his application therefor to the State Controller after attaining age 65, be retired, and receive a retirement allowance based upon the judicial service with which he is credited, in the same manner as other judges, except that his retirement allowance is an annual amount equal to 5 percent of the compensation payable, at the time payments of the allowance fall due, to the judge holding the office which the retired judge last held prior to the discontinuance of his service as judge, multiplied by the number of years of service with which the retired judge is entitled to be credited at the time of such discontinuance of his service, not to exceed eight years.

SEC. 2. Section 2 of said act is amended to read: 

Sec. 2. Every justice or judge retiring as provided in Section 1 of this act, shall, during the remainder of his life, receive an allowance equal to one-half the salary which he last received as the incumbent of a payable, at the time payment of the allowance falls due, to the judge holding the judicial office to which he was last elected by the people. Said allowance shall be paid by the State at the times and in the manner provided for the payment of salaries of justices of the Supreme Court.

SEC. 3. 

Section 4 of said act is amended to read: 

Sec. 4. Every judge retired for disability shall receive a retirement allowance payable in the same manner as the allowance above provided, which shall be computed as follows: (a) If eligible to retire under the provisions of Section 1, he shall receive the allowance provided in Section 2. (b) Except as provided in Section 5, if he is not eligible to retire under the provisions of Section 1, he shall receive an allowance equal to onehalf of the salary which he last received as the incumbent of a judicial office, payable, at the time payments of the allowance fall due, to the judge holding the judicial office last held by him, which allowance shall be paid to him for five years from the date of his retirement, and thereafter for a period of time equal to the period, if any, by which his aggregate service as a judge exceeds five years. 

SEC. 4. Section 4.5 is added to said act, to read: 

Sec. 4.5. Every retirement allowance payable under this act for time commencing on the effective date of this section to any judge who was retired for service or for disability prior to that date is hereby increased to the amount it would be if Section 2 and Section 4 of this act, as those sections exist on the effective date of this section, had been in effect on the effective date of the retirement of the judge to whom such retirement allowance is payable.

This section does not authorize any decrease in any retirement allowance, nor does this section give any retired judge, or his successors in interest, any claim against the State for any increase in any retirement allowance paid or payable for time prior to its effective date. 

SEC. 5. Section 5.1 is added to said act, to read: 

Sec. 5.1. In lieu of the retirement allowance for his life alone, a judge or retired judge may elect, or revoke or change a previous election prior to the approval of the previous election, to have the actuarial equivalent of his retirement allowance as of the date of retirement applied to a lesser retirement allowance, in accordance with one of the optional settlements specified in Section 5.2.

Such elections, revocation, or change of election shall be made by a writing filed with the State Controller prior to the making of the first payment on account of any retirement allowance. 

SEC. 6. Section 5.2 is added to said act, to read: 

Sec. 5.2. (a) Optional settlement one consists of the right to have a retirement allowance paid him until his death and if he dies before he receives the amount of his accumulated contributions at retirement, to have the balance at death paid to his surviving spouse or estate. (b) Optional settlement two consists of the right to have a retirement allowance paid him until his death and thereafter to his surviving spouse for life. (c) Optional settlement three consists of the right to have a retirement allowance paid him until his death, and thereafter to have one-half of his retirement allowance paid to his surviving spouse for life. (d) Optional settlement four consists of such other benefits as are the actuarial equivalent of his retirement allowance, that he may select subject to the approval of the State Controller.

SEC. 7. Section 5.3 is added to said act, to read: 

Sec. 5.3. If, during the life of a judge who has elected to receive an optional settlement in lieu of a retirement allowance for his life alone, or during the life of a surviving spouse beneficiary under such a settlement upon whose life contingency the optional settlement elected depends, the compensation payable to the judge holding the judicial office to which the retired judge was last elected by the people prior to his retirement is increased, the amounts payable to the retired judge or to his surviving spouse, or both, shall be recomputed and increased to be the actuarial equivalent of the increased amount of the retirement allowance to which the retired judge would be entitled if he had not elected an optional settlement. However, this section does not authorize any decrease in any retirement allowance or payment to any beneficiary, nor does it give any retired judge or his beneficiary any claim against the State for any increase in retirement allowance or other benefit for time prior to such increase in the compensation of the incumbent judge. 

SEC. 8. Section 75032 of the Government Code is amended to read: 

75032. Every judge retiring pursuant to Section 75025, shall, during the remainder of his life, receive an allowance equal to one-half the salary payable, at the time payment of the allowance falls due, to the judge holding which he last received as the incumbent of a the judicial office to which he was last elected by the people. The allowance shall be paid by the State at the times and in the manner provided for the payment of salaries of justices of the Supreme Court. 

SEC. 9. Section 75033 is added to the Government Code, to read: 

75033. Notwithstanding any other provision of this chapter, if the service of a judge is discontinued by any means other than death or retirement pursuant to this chapter, he shall have the right to elect within ninety (90) days thereafter, and without right of revocation, whether to allow his accumulated contributions to remain in the fund. Failure to make such election shall be deemed an irrevocable election to withdraw his accumulated contributions. A judge who so elects to allow his accumulated contributions to remain in the fund shall, upon his application therefor to the State Controller after attaining age 65, be retired, and receive a retirement allowance based upon the judicial service with which he is credited, in the same manner as other judges, except that his retirement allowance is an annual amount equal to 5 percent of the compensation payable, at the time payments of the allowance fall due, to the judge holding the office which the retired judge last held prior to the discontinuance of his service as judge, multiplied by the number of years of service with which the retired judge is entitled to be credited at the time of such discontinuance of his service, not to exceed eight years. 

SEC. 10. Section 75061 of the Government Code is amended to read: 

75061. Every judge retired for disability shall receive a retirement allowance payable at the times and in the manner provided for the payment of salaries of justices of the Supreme Court. The retirement allow ance shall be computed as follows: 

(a) If eligible to retire under the provisions of Section 75025, he shall receive the allowance provided in Section 75032. (b) Except as provided in Section 75080, if he is not eligible to retire under the provisions of Section 75025, he shall receive an allowance equal to one-half of the salary which he last received as the incumbent of a

judicial office, payable, at the time payments of the allowance fall due, to the judge holding the judicial office last held by him, which allowance shall be paid to him for five years from the date of his retirement, and thereafter for a period of time equal to the period, if any, by which his aggregate service as a judge exceeds five years. 

SEC. 11. Article 3.5 is added to Chapter 11 of Title 8 of the Govern ment Code, to read:

Article 3.5. Payment of Benefits 

75070. In lieu of the retirement allowance for his life alone, a judge or retired judge may elect, or revoke or change a previous election prior to the approval of the previous election, to have the actuarial equivalent of his retirement allowance as of the date of retirement applied to a lesser retirement allowance, in accordance with one of the optional settlements specified in Section 75071.

Such election, revocation, or change of election shall be made by a writing filed with the State Controller prior to the making of the first payment on account of any retirement allowance. 

75071. (a) Optional settlement one consists of the right to have a retirement allowance paid him until his death and if he dies before he receives the amount of his accumulated contributions at retirement, to have the balance at death paid to his surviving spouse or estate. (b) Optional settlement two consists of the right to have a retirement allowance paid him until his death and thereafter to his surviving spouse for life. (e) Optional settlement three consists of the right to have a retirement allowance paid him until his death, and thereafter to have one-half of his retirement allowance paid to his surviving spouse for life. (d) Optional settlement four consists of such other benefits as are the actuarial equivalent of his retirement allowance, that he may select subject to the approval of the State Controller. 

75072. If, during the life of a judge who has elected to receive an optional settlement in lieu of a retirement allowance for his life alone, or during the life of a surviving spouse beneficiary under such a settlement upon whose life contingency the optional settlement elected depends, the compensation payable to the judge holding the judicial office to which the retired judge was last elected by the people prior to his retirement is increased, the amounts payable to the retired judge or to his beneficiary, or both, shall be recomputed and increased to be the actuarial equivalent of the increased amount of the retirement allowance to which the retired judge would be entitled if he had not elected an optional settlement. However, this section does not authorize any decrease in any retirement allowance or payment to any beneficiary, nor does it give any retired judge or his beneficiary any claim against the State for any increase in retirement allowance or other benefit for time prior to such increase in the compensation of the incumbent judge. 

75073. Every retirement allowance payable under this chapter for time commencing on the effective date of this section to any judge who was retired for service or for disability prior that date is hereby increased to the amount it would be if Section 75032 and Section 75061, as those sections exist on the effective date of this section, had been in

effect on the effective date of the retirement of the judge to whom such retirement allowance is payable.

This section does not authorize any decrease in any retirement allowance, nor does this section give any retired judge, or his successors in interest, any claim against the State for any increase in any retirement allowance paid or payable for time prior to its effective date. 

SEC. 12. Sections 8 to 11, inclusive, of this act become operative only if Chapter 11 of Title 8 of the Government Code is enacted by the Legis lature at its 1953 Regular Session, and in such case at the same time as said Chapter 11 of Title 8 takes effect, at which time the sections of the Judges' Retirement Act added or amended by this act are repealed.

ILLEGAL USE OF NARCOTICS 1. PREVIOUS REPORTS 

In its progress report to the Legislature, submitted in March, 1952, the committee reported the results of its investigations to that date with respect to narcotics.

Since the submission of its progress report the committee has held an additional hearing on this subject and herewith reports the results of the same.

II. FURTHER WORK OF THE COMMITTEE 

The committee held an additional hearing in Los Angeles on August 

25, 1952, at which time the following persons were heard:

Dr. Joseph Engholm, Chairman, Pasadena Narcotics Education Com mittee; and The Honorable Pat McGee, Assemblyman, 64th District.

The committee has observed that in many communities of the State various civic groups have formed committees and study groups for the purpose of seeking at least a partial solution of the narcotics problem, particularly as it applies to minors. An excellent example of such an organization and its views on the subject is well illustrated by the testi mony of Dr. Joseph J. Engholm: 

"A. The Pasadena Narcotics Education Committee which was formed particularly at the request of the youth of Pasadena who were convinced that the spread of narcotics among the youth was getting to the point where it required special attention and they needed some real help. They asked us to meet with them and out of this, through the Chamber of Commerce of the City of Pasadena and other civic groups, grew this committee to study the problem particularly as it related to the youth." "A. We found in our study that the possibility of rehabilitation of narcotic addicts is almost an impossibility. I say almost an impossibility because Dr. Victor Vogel, head of the United States 

Public Service Hospital in Lexington, Kentucky and Fort Worth,

Texas says there is a possibility of 10 percent if he gets them in the hospital. Our experience in this area runs about 3 percent. Our committee does not want to be alarmists. We want to be realists and preventists. In the three-year period in the federal hospitals the teen age population increased 2,000 percent. Even though there might have been a few over 20 when they first started taking it, when there is a 2,000 percent increase in that three-year period, it is extremely significant * * *'' “*** I am not in the enforcement business. I am a dentist and a private citizen. I am engaged in this because I am interested in the youth only. I have youngsters of my own and I want to protect them. It is selfish in one way and yet it is because after all, no matter whether they are black or white they are still citizens ( 82 )

SENATE İNTERIM JUDICIARY COMMITTEE REPORT 

83 of our country and are as important as any one else. A couple of weeks ago the father of a boy who is an executive of an advertising firm in Los Angeles, living in Santa Anita Oaks, was faced with the problem of his son becoming an addict. It is really a difficult problem. ***'" ***This is so extremely important. Even though we know education is probably the greatest power we have, it is extremely slow and in order to put the finger in the dike to prevent the flood from coming over our youth, we have some proposed legislation we would like to present in order to get rid of the peddler. If we can get rid of the peddler and the source of supply, then it would not be available to the youngsters or to the adults, either. ***"'

Dr. Engholm, on behalf of the Pasadena Narcotics Education Com mittee, presented to the Senate Interim Judiciary Committee a written statement as to the narcotics problem and specific recommendations for legislation as follows: 

STATEMENT OF THE PASADENA NARCOTICS EDUCATION COMMITTEE 

"The unlawful use and disposition of narcotics and dangerous drugs constitutes an ever increasing problem which cannot be disposed of through indifference or a kid-glove policy. We have no alternative but to get tough. "In presenting this proposal for changes in penalties for narcotics offenses, we are fully aware of the fact that heavier penalties cannot serve as panacea for this danger. "Laws by themselves are not the answer, for the law comes into play only when the offense is committed. The law deals only with the effect--it does nothing to eliminate the cause of the use of narcotics. "In the long run, the effective answer to narcotics is in a thoroughgoing educational program, coupled with concerted efforts on the part of all citizens, individually and in groups, to provide a more stable atmosphere in the family and community to eliminate the deep-seated reasons for which people turn to narcotics as an escape. "We also see an urgent need for medical and psychiatric facilities for treatment of addicts, and for intensive medical research for both prevention and cure. "These are ideals which will not be reached easily or soon. "In the meantime, our immediate efforts must be directed toward eliminating the profitable ventures of those who viciously prey on people by providing, for a price, the illegal narcotics to anyone who has the money to pay. "The Narcotic Act of California, is found in the California Health and Safety Code. It makes up approximately 800 sections of the penal law of this State. There are additional laws in four or five other codes. The sections on penalty which we propose to strengthen are in the Narcotics Act. We do not propose to change the wording of

. 

the present law to any appreciable extent. We seek merely to increase the penalty for offenses already designated as such in the act. (See attached for proposed amendments.) 66 'An examination of the act will disclose that practically all irregularities amounting to violation of law are specifically dealt with. The majority of these are designated misdemeanors. The penalties are not particularly severe considering the gravity of the problem. In almost every type of situation involving the misuse of lawful means of obtaining drugs and narcotics, or the wilful abuse of some medical or quasi-legal right, the violation comes within the misdemeanor category. Practically all of the situations which suggest themselves in which a relatively innocent or uninformed person might become involved fall within this class. We even leave Section 

11715 which deals with forgery of prescriptions or their alteration or fictitious use in its present state. We do this deliberately because it is at present sufficiently severe, and because we feel that this is a field in which the true dealer in narcotics in the completely vicious sense rarely enters. "Here are our considered answers to most commonly raised objections: "1. The penalties are too severe, particularly for minors who may be innocently involved. "Any person who has any color of right or justifiable excuse for the misuse or abuse of narcotics regulations would not be caught in our proposed severe penalty sections. Addicts are designated misdemeanants; and a special provision exists for a person who considers himself an addict to be given an examination by the state department to establish it. An addict is handled in the municipal courts under the portion of the law which makes addiction a misdemeanor. "Naturally a juvenile does not come within the purview of this code since any person under the age of 18 comes under the California Youth Authority and not the regular penal laws. A minor between 18 and 21, if deserving, can be certified to the juvenile court by any adult court judge. "One addict must induce at least six other persons to become users in order to support his own habit, which costs as much as $60 each day. The other alternative is robbery, burglary, prostitution, and even murder. We are dealing in big business. A specified amount of heroin purchased in Mexico for $7,000 can be sold in this country for between $250,000 and $500,000 after having been diluted. Criminals will stop at nothing to make that kind of profit. "Most states provide death penalty for those who deprive others of their physical lives by murder. Physical death is merciful compared to the moral and spiritual death caused by narcotics. "2. The penalties are not severe enough. We should provide a death penalty. "There is a great deal of sentiment in favor of death penalty, but considered judgment indicates that the already difficult job of getting a conviction would become impossible. Juries which would

not hesitate to find a man guilty on a charge bearing a two- to tenyear term would be reluctant to do so if it meant sentencing the man to death. "3. This takes away this judge's prerogative of determining sentences, probation, parole, etc. "A judge who is aware of the full import of narcotics traffic will not want to allow probation or parole anyway. "4. What's wrong with the law as it now stands? "With profits from narcotics ranging upward from $150 a week, who will mind 90 or 180 days in jail now and then? The peddler can come right back and resume his trade as though he had just been away on vacation. "5. Is the narcotics situation really so serious? "Someone in public health once said that so long as a community had one death a year from diphtheria, diphtheria remained a serious problem. "The recorded increase among the youth in Los Angeles alone during the past 10 years is 2,000 percent. "We also know that for every arrest, there are a minimum of 10 more who escape. So long as one provider of narcotics is at large, the problem is a serious one. 

66 'Additional Facts: 

"1. Narcotics addiction is a one-way road. "2. Adults who deal in narcotics are absolute moral degenerates. 

All other crimes known to man accompany the narcotics traffic. These merchants of human destruction are seeking to build their trade among the youth of our communities. "3. There is no existing medical use for either marijuana or heroin-no justifiable reason for any person having it in his possession or under his control. “4. Our legislation is not directed at the addict, but at the peddler who creates and sponsors the addict for profit. The addict never has any more narcotics in his possession than a shot or two for himself. In the vast majority of instances he can afford only one badly needed shot at a time, and uses it immediately. 

RECOMMENDATIONS OF THE PASADENA NARCOTICS EDUCATION COMMITTEE FOR AMENDMENTS TO HEALTH AND SAFETY CODE 

As approved by Youth Conference in the City of Pasadena on May 

12, 1952. Sponsored by Narcotics Education Committee who urge your support for the adoption of these amendments : 

Amend H. & S. Code, Section 11712, to read: 

Any person convicted under this division for having in possession any narcotic, or of violating the provisions of Sections 11530 or 11557 is guilty of a felony punishable by imprisonment in the state prison for not less than two years, nor more than 20 years, and shall not be eligible for parole until he has served the minimum sentence.

If such a person has been previously convicted of a felony under the laws of the United States or of this or any other state, and if

the previous conviction of a felony is charged in the indictment or information and is found to be true by the jury, upon a jury trial, or if found to be true by the court, upon a court trial, or is admitted by the defendant, he shall be imprisoned in the state prison for not less than five years, nor more than fifty years, and shall not be eligible for parole until he has served the minimum sentence. (Does not apply to juveniles.)

Amend H. & S. Code, Section 11713, to read: 

Any person convicted under this division for transporting, selling, furnishing, or giving away, or offering to transport, sell, furnish, or give away, any narcotic is guilty of a felony punishable by imprisonment in the state prison for not less than 10 years, nor more than 50 years, and shall not be eligible for parole until he has served the minimum sentence. 

If such a person has been previously convicted of a felony under the laws of the United States or of this or any other state, and if the previous conviction of a felony is charged in the indictment or information and is found to be true by the jury, upon a jury trial, or is found to be true by the court, upon a court trial, or is admitted by the defendant, he shall be imprisoned in the state prison for life without possibility of parole. (Does not apply to juveniles.) 

Amend H. & S. Code, Section 11714, to read: 

Every person who hires, employs, or uses a minor in unlawfully transporting, carrying, selling, preparing for sale, peddling or using any narcotic is guilty of a felony punishable by imprisonment in the state prison for life without possibility of parole. (Does not apply to juveniles.)

In explanation of his proposals Dr. Engholm's testimony continued and is repeated here verbatim for the reason that the discussion in connection therewith is typical of the differences of opinion the committee found throughout the State on any specific legislation to change the narcotics statutes. “*** We do not propose to change the wording here materially. We propose to change the severity of the penalty to get rid of the peddler. Before I read that, let me state this. The average incarceration for a peddler of narcotics, the federal sentence, has been 18 months. When you take $3,000 and go to Europe, or $5,000 to $7,000 and go to Mexico and buy a kilo, 2½ pounds of marijuana, and skyrocket your investment many times over, 36 months in a federal penitentiary is a nice vacation. They buy it in relatively pure form and adulterate it with lactose or milk sugar, and the capsules sell for $5 apiece in this area. We have some compassion for the man who has to have narcotics because he would suffer terrific pains. He must be incarcerated. We have to do something if we are to rehabilitate him. But when a fellow is a despicable peddler selling the stuff for the almighty dollar, there is nothing bad enough for him, as far as we are concerned.

"We have tried to be as lenient as possible. Personally I would like to put them away for good, but the people won't go for that type of thing, so we have to put it as life imprisonment. 

"This is the Narcotics Act, Division 10 of the Health and Safety 

Code, under Article 4, Sections 11712, 11713 and 11714. Those are the places we propose changes in order to make the penalties so severe that we hope it will not be a calculated risk for the peddler and that he will go free. We want to increase the penalties to get rid of them. I understand they did the same thing in Memphis, Tennessee. They may go some place else and I feel terribly sorry about that, but if we get after them strong enough everywhere we will get rid of them.
