The same way with furnishing to minors * 

"Mr. Gentry: *** I don't handle felonies, but I handle the results of felonies. There was a case of a 15-year-old boy who was a user since he was 13. Who made him a user? What can you do now?

He was in Camarillo. They will send him to Lexington. There was a 

17-year-old girl whose parents spent $17,000. She is a potential murderer. Nobody can do anything with her. Somebody made her a user. She has to have it. My obligation is to the thousands of kids and not to one man who may through some lack of morality do this. "Dr. Engholm: I don't suppose there can be any law that might not hurt some innocent victim somehow or other. "Chairman Busch: If you pass laws which are too drastic, everybody will hit at them and you won't get enforcement. "Senator Tenney: You don't get enforcement under the circumstances generally with a jury because they find some way to figure out an angle. "Dr. Engholm: Will the jury determine whether or not the man is guilty or what the penalty is? 66 'Senator Tenney: Unfortunately they take both into consideration. "Dr. Engholm: They are not supposed to do that. They are supposed to act on the evidence. "Senator Tenney: True, but if you bring in a boy who gave a girl a marijuana cigarette, he is 22 years old and the jury know he will be

10 years without eligibility for parole, you have a situation in which they feel it would be unjust and probably they would find him not guilty. "Mr. Gentry: Since marijuana is not addiction forming could. you limit it to 'other than marijuana'? "Senator O'Gara: We would not to make an exception. "Dr. Engholm: The big danger is marijuana. It is the springboard to heroin addiction. We have to stop them before they get started. Maybe we could put something in here for first offense of giving away, something like that, one year in the county jail. That would give them a chance to find out. "Senator O'Gara: Marijuana is bad enough. We might have a special section for marijuana and have it under misdemeanor. "Mr. Gentry: If you put heroin under that category they will give it away. "Dr. Engholm: Very few of them ever try heroin until they try marijuana. "Chairman Busch: You want to make the penalties drastic. "Dr. Engholm: We want to make the penalties drastic for the peddler. "Mr. Gentry: We know that the Legislature anl the Senate in committee will change it and cut it down. There are those who want the death penalty. We cut that down. We assume you people will cut this down, but that you will come up with something that will aid us and make it considerably more severe than it is at the present time and possibly stop people from ministers to fathers and mothers from calling up the judges and saying, 'Now, he is a nice boy. He needs probation.' So within 90 days he is out on the street and back again. Judges are under great pressure. They are human beings. 

We are not criticizing them. We want to take it out of his hands. "Dr. Engholm: That is right, and we don't want parole. "Senator O'Gara: You want to get rid of the peddler. "Dr. Engholm: That is right. It is not that we want to hurt any innocent addict, but we want to keep it away from the kids. We know it has gotten to be a real problem and the only thing we can see is to make the sentence severe enough to get rid of the peddler. "Senator Tenney: I will go right along with hitting the peddler. "Chairman Busch: I will, too. I think the whole committee feels that way about it. "Senator Tenney: We know there are cases, however, where it would be equally unjust to send them to the penitentiary. "Dr. Engholm: The same thing is true with the last sentence where we talk about use by minors. Immediately it is life sentence. 

They are potential murderers. They get the youngsters started and they finally end up on heroin. It is much more merciful to put a gun to their heads than to put a needle in their arms and kill their souls. Some people will say, 'Someone for the first time who never knew anything about this law will go out and get caught and they are in for life without having a second chance.' As much as we would like to keep it this way, we would put a paragraph in saying for the first sentence they get so much time in an institution with

some psychiatric help to let them know what will happen to them if they do it again.' 

The Honorable Pat McGee, Assemblyman from the 64th District, had the following suggestions to offer which, it will be observed, differ in approach to some of the suggestions made by Dr. Engholm and further illustrates the wide divergence of views on the solution of this problem: "I am Assemblyman Pat McGee of the 64th District. I have two points to make. I think the law should be changed, one law to deal exclusively in the field of heroin and make penalties as severe as possible. You can't get it in this country without some act of illegality. "Senator Tenney: Anything other than marijuana. "Mr. McGee: They start with heroin. It is not prescribed in this country. Make the penalties extremely severe on the peddler and less severe possibly on the user. Start by making it more severe there and less severe for marijuana. "My second point. I would recommend that in case of first offenders in the nonheroin field probation be given back to the courts for this reason. I am not for making the penalties less severe. 

Actually probation works a more severe penalty because in Los Angeles they get 90 days on the first offense. They are out in 90 days with no supervision, whereas if the court had given probation for a two or three year period the probation department and the court have control over the person after he gets back from the first offense. On the first offense for the fellow you mentioned, these courts have not been excessively lenient, in my opinion, but they crowd them up in the jails for 90 days, it is the first offense. He had it in the car or somebody gave it to him at a party. They have got to do that because they have no chance, but after 90 days no one has supervision. Now, if the court could sentence them to two years in the County Jail, provided that when the 90 days was served the balance of the two years would be on probation, then should he break probation there would be two offenses, the new one where he breaks probation and the old one because the court can take in the old one and slap the two years back on him. If possible, I would recommend reinstitution of probation for those reasons. 

III. CONCLUSIONS AND RECOMMENDATIONS 

As indicated in its Progress Report to the Legislature, the Senate Interim Judiciary Committee, being constituted primarily as a screening committee for proposed legislation, has felt that only the most specific legislative proposals in this field should be examined. Particularly is this true in view of the existence of a special Senate Interim Committee on Narcotics and Hypnotics (Senate Resolution 187) under the chairmanship of the Honorable Fred H. Kraft.

Accordingly, the committee has only the following specific recommendations to make: 

1. That Subdivision (h) of Section 700 of the Welfare and Institutions 

Code be amended to delete the reference to habitual use of cigarettes

and to add within its scope the habitual use of marijuana, hypnotics and barbituates; 

2. That further discretion be given to the courts in the ultimate solution of this problem by removing the minimum sentence now pro vided in Health and Safety Code Section 11713; 

3.

That in order to further permit proper discretion in the courts and probationary officials, that Section 11715.6 of the Health and Safety 

Code be modified; 

4. That adequate provision be made in the budget for personnel for the enforcement of narcotics laws.

PUBLICATION OF ALL OPINIONS OF THE SUPREME COURT I. INTRODUCTION 

The committee's attention was directed to an inquiry with regard to a proposed amendment to Section 59.5, Code of Civil Procedure, relating to the publication of the opinions of the Supreme Court. 

The section as it now reads is as follows: 

"Section 59.5. The reports shall be published under the general supervision of the Supreme Court. The court may correct clerical errors in the opinion as filed, or authorize them to be corrected. 

It shall not in any manner alter the written opinion as to substance, argument, or authority cited, or omit any portion of the opinion as filed. All opinions filed shall be printed in full in the law reports. 

Proof sheets of the opinions shall be furnished by the official reporter to the Supreme Court from time to time as the cases are set up in galleys and corrections made.”

II. THE INQUIRY 

In its preliminary discussions the committee determined that the views of the bench and bar should be obtained as to (1) any problems which now exist with regard to the printing of Supreme Court opinions and (2) if any problems do exist, what is the appropriate procedure for correcting the same.

Pursuant to this determination and in order to narrow the inquiry, communications were sent to many distinguished members of the bench and bar, including all members of the Supreme Court, as well as the 

Presiding Justices of the District Court of Appeal and Past Presidents of the State Bar of California, requesting their views as to the following amendment (new material is italicized) to the code section under discussion: "Section 59.5. The reports shall be published under the general supervision of the Supreme Court. The court may correct clerical errors in the opinion as filed, or authorize them to be corrected. 

It shall not in any manner alter the written opinion as to substance, argument, or authority cited, or omit any portion of the opinion as filed. Nor shall it omit any opinion filed or any portion thereof. All opinions filed, whether majority, concurring, dissenting or otherwise, shall be printed in full in the law reports. Proof sheets of the opinions shall be furnished by the official reporter to the Supreme Court from time to time as the cases are set up in galleys and corrections made. 

The committee received many replies to its inquiry containing a variety of suggestions, approvals and disapprovals of the proposed amendment and wishes to express its deep appreciation to the many 4-L-3870 (97)

distinguished jurists and attorneys who contributed their thoughts and time in connection with this problem. 

Some of the suggestions pointed out that regardless of the amendment of this section, all of the provisions of the Code of Civil Procedure relative to the reporting of the decisions of the Supreme Court and the district courts of appeal should be revised to meet the changes which have been made in the system of preparing, filing and publishing decisions since these provisions were originally enacted. 

In addition to other comments received, the committee received a communication signed by all members of the Supreme Court which reads as follows: 

"SUPREME COURT OF CALIFORNIA Chambers of the Chief Justice 

Mr. John A. Bohn, Counsel

STATE BUILDING SAN FRANCISCO Senate Interim Judiciary Committee 

459 Holbrook Building 

58 Sutter Street, San Francisco 4, California 

February 9, 1953

DEAR MR. BOHN: This is in reply to letters which you recently addressed to the members of this court asking for our opinion concerning a proposed amendment of Section 59.5 of the Code of Civil Procedure to provide that all opinions filed, "whether majority, concurring, dissenting or otherwise," shall be published in the official reports. 

We believe that the proposed amendment is unnecessary and will serve no useful purpose. For many years it has been the practice of this court to require publication in the official reports of all opinions (majority, concurring and dissenting) in cases where the Supreme Court and district courts of appeal are required to write opinions by Article VI, Sections 2 and 24, of the Constitution. A considerable portion of the business of these courts, however, may be transacted without the necessity of a written opinion and is ordinarily handled by minute orders which do not give any reasons or grounds for the decision. For example, in the fiscal year ending June, 1952, the Supreme Court acted upon 1,532 matters and disposed of 1,361 by minute orders. In the latter group there were 69 petitions for rehearing, 628 petitions for hearing after decision in a district court of appeal, 133 original proceedings and 521 miscellaneous matters. During the same period the district courts of appeal similarly acted by minute order in disposing of 373 petitions for rehearing, 298 original proceedings and 700 miscellaneous matters. When a petition for hearing or rehearing is denied in a case decided by a written opinion, a notation of the fact of denial is printed in the official bound volumes at the end of the opinion together with any statements filed in connection with the denial. As to all other minute orders the consistent practice, since the Supreme Court was established over 100 years ago, has been not to print them in the bound volumes of the official reports, and the members of the court are unanimously of the opinion that such minute orders should not be so published. They are printed, however, in the advance reports, which are published by 

Bancroft-Whitney Company.

1 When an original proceeding has been disposed of by a minute order, a justice has occasionally filed a written statement giving his reasons for agreeing or disagreeing with the order of denial, but since the minute order itself is not printed in the official reports, the practice has likewise been not to publish such statements in the bound volumes. All such statements, however, are public records which are, of course, always available to the press. The Reporter of Decisions has been instructed that when such statements are filed a copy is to be given to Bancroft-Whitney Company, and the court has suggested to that company that any such statements should be printed in the advance reports as part of the minutes. 

The Reporter of Decisions has also been instructed to see that a copy of any such statements is furnished to the West Publishing Company, which also publishes advance reports, for use as it may see fit. 

In connection with the proposed amendment, your attention is called to Section 16 of Article VI of the California Constitution, which provides: "The Legislature shall provide for the speedy publication of such opinions of the Supreme Court and district courts of appeal as the Supreme Court may deem expedient, and all opinions shall be free for publication by any person." (Italics added.) 

Yours very truly, (Signed)

PHIL S. GIBSON, Chief Justice 

JOHN W. SHENK, Associate Justice

DOUGLAS L. EDMONDS, Associate Justice JESSE W. CARTER, Associate Justice ROGER J. TRAYNOR, Associate Justice 

B. REY SCHAUER, Associate Justice 

HOMER R. SPENCE, Associate Justice"

III. CONCLUSIONS AND RECOMMENDATIONS 

The committee believes that no recommendations for change are desirable at this time but recommends to any successor committee that conferences be held with the Judicial Council to ascertain if there is need for a general revision of all sections pertaining to the publication and reporting of opinions of the Supreme Court and the district courts of appeal.

COMMISSION ON QUALIFICATIONS, ARTICLE VI, SEC- TION 26, CALIFORNIA CONSTITUTION I. INTRODUCTION 

The committee deemed it desirable to explore the advisability of revising that portion of Section 26 of Article VI of the Constitution, relating to the "Commission on Qualifications" for the selection of judges.

Section 26 of Article VI reads as follows: 

"SEC. 26. Within 30 days before the sixteenth day of August next preceding the expiration of his term, any justice of the Supreme Court, justice of a District Court of Appeal, or judge of a superior court in any county the electors of which have adopted provisions of this section as applicable to the judge or judges of the superior court of such county in the manner hereinafter provided, may file with the officer charged with the duty of certifying nominations for publication in the official ballot a declaration of candidacy for election to succeed himself. If he does not file such declaration the Governor must nominate a suitable person for the office before the sixteenth day of September, by filing such nomination with the officer charged with said duty of certifying nominations. "In either event, the name of such candidate shall be placed upon the ballot for the ensuing general election in November in substantially the following form: For

Shall (title of office) (name) Yes be elected to the office for the term expiring January ? (year) No "No name shall be placed upon the ballot as a candidate for any of said judicial offices except that of a person so declaring or so nominated. If a majority of the electors voting upon such candidacy vote 'yes,' such person shall be elected to said office. If a majority of those voting thereon vote 'no,' he shall not be elected, and may not thereafter be appointed to fill any vacancy in that court, but may be nominated and elected thereto as hereinabove provided. "Whenever a vacancy shall occur in any judicial office above named, by reason of the failure of a candidate to be elected or otherwise, the Governor shall appoint a suitable person to fill the vacancy. 

An incumbent of any such judicial office serving a term by appointment of the Governor shall hold office until the first Monday after ( 100 )

the first day of January following the general election next after his appointment, or until the qualification of any nominee who may have been elected to said office prior to that time. "No such nomination or appointment by the Governor shall be effective unless there be filed with the Secretary of State a written confirmation of such nomination or appointment signed by a majority of the three officials herein designated as the Commission on Qualifications. The Commission on Qualifications shall consist of (1) the Chief Justice of the Supreme Court, or if such office be vacant, the acting Chief Justice; (2) the presiding justice of the 

District Court of Appeal of the district in which a justice of a District Court of Appeal or a judge of a superior court is to serve, or, if there be two such presiding justices, the one who has served the longer as such; or, in the case of the nomination or appointment of a justice of the Supreme Court, the presiding justice who has served longest as such upon any of the District Courts of Appeal; and (3) the Attorney General. If two or more presiding justices above designated shall have served terms of equal length, they shall choose the one who is to be a member of the Commission on Qualifications by lot, whenever occasion for action arises. The Legislature shall provide by general law for the retirement, with reasonable retirement allowance, of such justices and judges for age or disability. "In addition to the methods of removal by the Legislature pro vided by Sections 17 and 18 of Article IV and by Section 10 of this article, the provisions of Article XXIII relative to the recall of elective public officers shall be applicable to justices and judges elected and appointed pursuant to the provisions of this section so far as the same relate to removal from office. "The provisions of this section shall not apply to the judge or judges of the superior court of any county until a majority of the electors of such county voting on the question of the adoption of such provisions, in a manner to be provided for by the Legislature, shall vote in favor thereof. "If the Legislature diminishes the number of judges of the superior court in any county or city and county, the offices which first become vacant, to the number of judges diminished, shall be deemed to be abolished."

II. THE INQUIRY 

The committee ascertained that for some time there have been intermittent suggestions that the Commission on Qualifications for the selection of judges, set forth in Section 26 of Article VI of the Constitution, should be broadened to include additional representation. It was variously suggested that the President pro Tempore of the Senate, the Speaker of the Assembly, the President of the State Bar and perhaps others could be added to this commission with advantage in the selection of judges. However, the committee found little widespread interest in the suggested changes and equally little unanimity of opinion as to the persons to be added, if any, to the commission. 

In the course of its inquiry the committee asked the views of the following persons: 

The Honorable Phil S. Gibson, Chief Justice, Supreme Court; 

The Honorable Edmund G. Brown, Attorney General;

The Honorable A. F. Bray, Justice, District Court of Appeal, First 

Appellate District; and President, Conference of California Judges;

The Honorable Emil Gumpert, President, The State Bar of Cali fornia;

The Honorable Jerold E. Weil, Secretary and Counsel, The State 

Bar of California;

The Honorable Minor Moore, Presiding Justice, District Court of 

Appeal, Second Appellate District;

The Honorable Raymond E. Peters, Presiding Justice, District Court of Appeal, First Appellate District; 

The Honorable Thomas P. White, Presiding Justice, District Court of Appeal, Second Appellate District;

The Honorable Annette Abbott Adams, Presiding Justice, District 

Court of Appeal, Third Appellate District;

The Honorable John T. Nourse, Presiding Justice, District Court of 

Appeal, First Appellate District;

The Honorable Clement L. Shinn, Presiding Justice, District Court of Appeal, Second Appellate District;

The Honorable Charles R. Barnard, Presiding Justice, District Court of Appeal, Fourth Appellate District. 

The State Bar advised that the Board of Governors had created a committee on the appointment and selection of judges which is undertaking an extensive study. It was, therefore, believed that no recommendations could be made until the work of the committee had been completed. 

The issues involved are very clearly set forth in a letter from the Honorable Raymond E. Peters, Presiding Justice of the District Court of

Appeal. 

"STATE OF CALIFORNIA DISTRICT COURT OF APPEAL State Building--Civic Center SAN FRANCISCO MR. JOHN A. BоHN, Counsel California Senate Interim Judiciary Committee 

459 Holbrook Building, 58 Sutter Street 

San Francisco 4, California 

July 30, 1952 "My dear Mr. Bohn: I am in receipt of your letter of July 22d stating that your committee is considering a possible revision of Art. VI, § 26, of the Constitution, and inviting me to express my views on the subject. I am glad to do so. "There are two criticisms that I have heard about the Qualifications Commission as presently set up in Art. VI, § 26. First, that the base of the commission is not broad enough. The present commission consists of an appellate judge, the Chief Justice and the Attorney General. It has been argued that the Legislature, the State Bar, and perhaps the public, should be included. As part of this same criticism 

I have heard it argued that the two judges on the commission-the appellate court justice and the Chief Justice-might be appointed by the same Governor whose judicial appointment is under consid

---

1. "The second criticism is more fundamental. It is that the present 

Commission only operates after the Governor has made his nomination, and frequently released it to the press. It is argued that such a Commission does not act as a real check on the Governor, for the very practical reason that after the Governor has made known his nomination, the Commission will be reluctant to blacken the nominee's reputation by a finding that he is totally unqualified. It has been urged that the Commission (broadened in base) should not be limited to passing on a nominee's qualifications, but should also be empowered to prepare a list of nominees, from which list (or a succeeding list) the Governor must select an appointee-the so-called 

Missouri plan. "Let me discuss these problems in order. The criticism that the base of the Commission is not broad enough certainly warrants careful consideration. The Legislature, the Bar and the public have a great interest and stake in judicial appointments. From an ideal standpoint I believe that these interests, in addition to the appellate justices and the Attorney General, should be represented in the Commission. If the base of the Commission were broader, certainly, to that extent, there would be a better check on the Governor's nominee. It should be pointed out, however, that at the present time, purely by voluntary action on the part of Governor Warren, the State Bar is informally represented. The Governor will not complete a judicial appointment until the State Bar has approved the nomination. The State Bar has set up a regular committee for this purpose. Of course, this is a voluntary setup and may be abolished by future Governors. "I don't think the criticism that the Governor might appoint two members of the Commission is entitled to very much weight. The possibility of that happening again is very slight indeed, and does not warrant, in my opinion, putting into operation the cumbersome and costly machinery of amending our Constitution. "On the whole, so far as the possible broadening of the base of the Commission is concerned, I do not think that that reform alone will accomplish any material improvement. As long as the Governor has unlimited power of nomination, no Commission, no matter how broad its base, will act as a real restraint upon him. The real power is exercised by the person or body that has the power of nomination. "That brings me to the second problem-should the Commission be empowered to nominate a list of qualified nominees from which the Governor must select an appointee? This presents an important and fundamental question. Traditionally, in America, the Governor or President has exercised an unlimited power of nomination. That is traditionally an executive power. I assume, without knowing it to be a fact, that almost any Governor would resist the suggested impairment of his executive powers. It has been tried in Missouri and apparently works well there. Perhaps other states have tried the Missouri plan. If so, the results should be carefully studied. "My conclusions are as follows: "1. It is very difficult to know whether the present Commission acts as a real check on the Governor. So far as I know, in fifteen

years of operation, the Commission has refused to certify but one nominee *** "Whether the Governor, because he knows that appellate justices must be approved, exercises greater care than he otherwise would do in making his original selection, is something that cannot be ascertained. Superior and Municipal Court appointees do not have to be approved. Are better appointments made to the appellate courts than to the trial courts? I don't know. "2. Broadening the base of the Commission will, in my opinion, not materially increase the veto power of that body. Whether the 

Commission consists of 3, 5 or 7, as long as the Governor can nominate any lawyer of five years' experience, and can release that nomination to the public before the Commission acts, the Commission will be reluctant to disapprove the nomination. There will always be a natural reluctance to brand the nominee as unqualified. Moreover, it will be a rare occurrence, indeed, where the Governor does not have at least one or two friends on the Commission. They would be very hesitant to charge publicly that the Governor has selected an unqualified man for the Judiciary. "3. The real power rests with the man or group that makes the original selection. Whether the Missouri plan should be adopted in this state is a problem about which I have no fixed opinion. If it were to be adopted, in my opinion the Governor should have the power of appointing one or two members to the Commission so that his views can be given consideration. "4. I am firmly convinced that the basic plan set up by Art. VI, § 26, of the Constitution is sound, and in the public interest. It has resulted in taking our high court justices out of politics. It has demonstrably increased the efficiency of our courts, because no longer do such justices have to spend at least part of their time keeping their political fences in repair, and keeping their eyes on an impending election. Moreover, the Supreme Court justices have statewide jurisdiction, and the appellate courts have district jurisdiction. If a Supreme Court justice had to run in a statewide competitive election against some well-known Superior Court judge who, because of his trial position, gets his name in the papers frequently, it would cost at least $50,000. Since judges are non-partisan, where will he get the money to run? Obviously, from lawyers who practice before him. That is unsound. The same applies to the appellate court justices to a lesser degree. "I am also convinced that Art. VI, § 26, has resulted in securing the services of better qualified men than would have been secured by the competitive election method. Obviously, appointees to our high courts are usually selected from the more successful practitioners. Obviously, a lawyer would hesitate to give up a lucrative practice if he knew that he would have to run in a competitive election with the grave possibility of defeat. "5. I believe that any attempt to amend Art. VI, § 26, should be made only after the most careful consideration. The section, in spite of its limitations, was a great step forward. Of course, I know that various individuals and groups are opposed to judges not being elected in competitive elections. Any attempt to modify the section

may be attended by the possibility of encouraging such persons and groups to sponsor a repeal of the entire section. That possibility should be investigated by your committee. "6. If a revision of the section is decided upon, some consideration should be given to the problem as to whether the benefits of the section should be extended to the trial courts. At present any county may adopt the section, but not one has done so. Consideration should be given to the possibility of making the section mandatory as to trial court appointments. "I am very much interested in this problem, and am very happy to have had this opportunity of expressing myself on it. I will be glad to cooperate with you and your Committee at any time. "Sincerely, (Signed) 

"RAYMOND E. PETERS 

"Presiding Justice

REP:h" 

Other comments received by the committee referred to some phase of the problem as outlined in the letter from Justice Peters. 

In addition the attention of the committee was called to the proposals of the State Bar for an enlargement of the Commission on Qualifications contained in the report of its "Committee on Selection, Qualification, Tenure and Removal of Judges," published in the July-August, 1944, issue of The State Bar Journal at page 335. These proposals in brief are as follows: 

"1. That the present Qualifications Commission be increased by two members, so that it would have the following members: Chief 

Justice of the Supreme Court; Senior Presiding Justice of the Dis trict Court of Appeal; Attorney General; President of the State 

Bar of California; and President Pro Tem of the Senate. "2. That instead of a majority approval of the commission being sufficient to confirm an appointment, the vote of four members would be required. “3. That where a member of the District Court of Appeal is to be appointed, such appointee should have been a resident of the Appellate District in which he is to serve, for five years immediately preceding his appointment. "4. That a proviso shall be inserted which shall provide, generally, that in the event either the Chief Justice of the Supreme Court or the member of the District Court of Appeal serving on the Qualifications Commission had been appointed to his position by the then Governor of the State of California, such person should not serve on the Qualifications Commission, but the Justice on each of said courts, senior in point of service, who had not been so appointed by the Governor, would serve upon said commission.” 

III. CONCLUSIONS OF THE COMMITTEE 

The committee believes that in the words of Justice Peters, "any attempt to amend Article VI, Section 26, should be made only after the most careful consideration." It, therefore, has no specific recommendations to make at this time, but believes the subject is worthy of further study.

MISCELLANEOUS AMENDMENTS I. SPONSORED BY THE CALIFORNIA LAND TITLE ASSOCIATION 

The committee heard various proposals for statutory changes recommended by the California Land Title Association, and of the proposals made, the following have the approval of the committee and have been introduced in the Legislature sponsored by all members of the committee. Following each bill will be found a brief explanation of the reason for the proposed amendment.

A. SECTION 1600 OF THE PROBATE CODE 

An act to amend Section 1600 of the Probate Code, relating to requests for special notice.

The people of the State of California do enact as follows: 

SECTION 1. Section 1600 of the Probate Code is amended to 

1600. At any time after the issuance of letters of guardianship, any relative of the ward, in person or by attorney, or the attorney for any department or bureau of the United States Government, or the attorney for any department, bureau or agency of this State, or any of its political subdivisions, which makes or awards compensation, pension, insurance or other allowance for the benefit of the ward's estate, or any person who may be otherwise interested in the estate, may serve upon the guardian or upon the latter's attorney, and file with the clerk of the court where the proceedings are pending, with a written admission or proof of such service, a written request, stating that he desires special notice of the filing or commencing of any or all of the following: (1) Petitions for the sale, lease or mortgage or confirmation of sale of any property of the ward. 

(2) Accounts. 

(3) Application for removal of the ward's property to a foreign jurisdiction. (4) Petitions for partition of any property of the ward. (5) Petitions for allowances of any nature payable from the ward's estate. (6) Petitions for the investment of funds of the estate. (7) Proceedings for the removal, suspension, or discharge of the guardian or final termination of the guardianship. 

Memorandum on Proposed Amendment to Section 1600 of the Probate Code 

Purpose. The right to request special notice in guardianship proceedings is at present limited, in Section 1600 of the Probate Code, to any relative of the ward or the attorney for any department or agency of the State or Federal Government which is paying to the ward's estate a pension, insurance, compensation, etc. The amendment would extend this right to request special notice to persons "otherwise interested," the ( 106 )

same language now used in Probate Code Section 1202, relating to special notice in the estates of decedents. 

Background. It is quite possible that someone other than a relative or a government attorney will have a legitimate interest in certain guardianship proceedings. For example, suppose the guardian petitions for the sale, lease or mortgage of real property of the ward. Among persons "otherwise interested" would be a vendee under a contract of sale covering such property, who is accorded the right, by Section 1537, to seek a conveyance of such property to himself. At the present time he could not file a request for special notice of such proceedings. 

Furthermore, it appears to have been the policy of the Legislature to conform the rules of the Probate Code relating to guardians to those relating to the administration of property in the estates of decedents, insofar as possible. Thus, Section 1534 provides that sales by guardians should conform "as nearly as may be, to the provisions of this code concerning sales by administrators, unless otherwise specially provided in this chapter." (Similar conformity references are found throughout other guardianship sections; e.g. Sections 1537, 1538.) 

B. SECTION 296.42 OF THE PROBATE CODE 

An act to amend Section 296.42 of the Probate Code, relating to the estates of deceased persons and the determination of simultaneous death or the order of death. 

The people of the State of California do enact as follows: 

SECTION 1. Section 296.42 of the Probate Code is amended to read: 

296.42. At the time appointed, the court, upon proof that due notice of the hearing has been given, shall proceed to hear the petition and any objections thereto that may have been filed or presented; and if, after a full hearing, the court is satisfied that the named persons are dead and that there is no sufficient evidence that they died other than simultaneously, it shall make an order to that effect. Such order when it becomes final shall be a binding determination of the facts therein set forth and conclusive as against the personal representatives of the deceased persons determined to have died simultaneously named in the order and against all persons claiming by, through or under any such deceased persons. If the court is satisfied that the named persons are dead and that they did not die simultaneously then the court shall make an order setting forth the order in which such persons died. The probate court which first acquires jurisdiction under Section 296.41 shall have exclusive jurisdiction to determine, by its order, that there is no sufficient evidence that the named persons died otherwise than simultaneously or to determine the order in which the named persons died. 

Memorandum on Proposed Amendment to Section 296.42 of the Probate Code Section 296.42 is part of the Uniform Simultaneous Death Act. At the present time the act authorizes the court, if it finds that two or more named persons are dead and that there is no sufficient evidence that they

died other than simultaneously, to make an order to the effect that death was simultaneous. However, if there is evidence that death was not simultaneous, the court has, at the present time, no authority to determine the order in which such persons died.

It is the purpose of the proposed amendment to provide that the court, in the event there is evidence that death was not simultaneous, may, on the basis of the evidence introduced at the hearing, make an order setting out the order in which such persons died. It is obvious that, if there was presented to the court evidence showing death was not simultaneous, such evidence must have shown that one of such persons died first. It follows that the court should have the authority to determine, in this proceeding, the order in which death occurred.

C. SECTIONS 1180 AND 1181 OF THE CIVIL CODE 

An act to amend Sections 1180 and 1181 of the Civil Code, relating to officers who may take proof or acknowledgment. 

The people of the State of California do enact as follows: 

SECTION 1. Section 1180 of the Civil Code is amended to 

1180. The proof or acknowledgment of an instrument may be made at any place within this state State before a justice or clerk of the supreme court, a justice of any district court of appeals, or judge of a superior court, or judge of a municipal court, or judge of a justice court .

SEC. 2. Section 1181 of the Civil Code is amended to read: 

1181. The proof or acknowledgement of an instrument may made in this State, within the city, county, city and county, district for which the officer was elected, or appointed, before either: 

1. A clerk of a court of record; 

2. A county recorder; 

3. A county clerk; 

4. A court commissioner; 

5. A notary public. 

6. A judge of a justice court. or 

Memorandum on Proposed Amendment to Section 296.42 of the Probate Code 

The purpose of the proposed amendments is to eliminate a conflict between Section 179 of the Code of Civil Procedure and Civil Code Sec tion 1181 as to the power of judges to take acknowledgments.

Code of Civil Procedure Section 179 provides "Every judge or justice shall have power in any part of the State to take and certify" acknowledgments, etc. (emphasis added). Civil Code Section 1181, on the other hand, provides, in effect, that a judge of a justice court may take acknowledgments within the district for which he was elected. It is, therefore, apparent that the Civil Code section would not authorize a judge of a justice court to take acknowledgments outside of his own district, while the Code of Civil Procedure would permit any judge to take acknowledgments anywhere in the State.

This conflict would be eliminated by deleting the reference to judges of justice courts from Section 1181 of the Civil Code, and inserting it in

Civil Code Section 1180 which provides, as does C. C. P. Section 179, that the power to take acknowledgments extends to any place within the State.

A further amendment would add to Section 1180 of the Civil Code, which lists at the present time judges of the supreme and superior courts as having power to take acknowledgments anywhere in the State, justices of the district courts of appeal, municipal and justice court judges. This amendment would not change the law, since C. C. P. Section 179, supra, now provides that every judge or justice has such power in any part of the State. It would, however, remove any possible misunderstanding which might arise from the fact that Section 1180 in its present form lists some judges and not others. Also the word "state" appearing in Section 

1180 would be changed to capitalize the first letter, in conformity with other code sections. The following measures were also recommended for adoption by the California Land Title Association. However, in each instance the committee recommended amendments to the original drafts. Accordingly, there is hereinafter set forth the original draft of each bill with an explanation of the purpose of the measure and thereafter the final draft of each bill with an explanation for the change from the original language. 

D. SECTION 1263 OF THE CIVIL CODE 

An act to amend Section 1263 of the Civil Code, relating to contents of declaration of homestead. 

The people of the State of California do enact as follows:

SECTION 1. Section 1263 of the Civil Code is amended to read: 

1263. The declaration of homestead must contain: 

1. A statement showing that the person making it is the head of a family, and if the claimant is married, the name of the spouse; or, when the declaration is made by the wife, showing that her husband has not made such declaration and that she therefore makes the declaration for their joint benefit; 

2. A statement that the person making it is residing on the premises, and claims them as a homestead; 

3. A description of the premises; 

4. An estimate of their actual cash value; 

5. Such declaration of homestead may further contain a statement of as to the character of the improvement or improvements which have been placed upon the property sought to be homesteaded with sufficient detail to show that it is a proper subject of homestead, and that no former declaration has been made, or, if made, that it has been abandoned and if it contains such further statement and the declaration is supported by the affidavit of the declarant, annexed thereto, that the matters therein stated are true of his or her own knowledge, such declaration, when properly recorded, shall be prima facie evidence of the facts therein stated, and conclusive evidence thereof in favor of a purchaser or encumbrancer in good faith and for a valuable consideration.

Memorandum on Proposed Amendment to Civil Code Section 1263 

Section 1263 states that a declaration of homestead may contain a "statement of the character of the property." There is little doubt that what is intended is a statement of the physical character of the improvements, showing that the property is a proper subject of homestead. This is shown by the language of the statute, requiring that the statement must be in "sufficient detail to show that it is a proper subject of homestead.” 

However, this is sometimes misunderstood, and the recorded statement, instead of indicating the physical character of the property, will state that it is held as community property, etc. The proposed amendment would make it clear that what is intended is information as to the improvements on the homestead property. 

It should be noted that, while the statement above referred to is optional, and is not necessary to the validity of the homestead declaration, (McNabb v. Byrnes, 92 Cal. App. 337) it is important because, as to purchasers or encumbrancers for value and in good faith, it is conclusive evidence of the facts therein stated. And such a good faith purchaser could defeat the judgment creditor of the homestead claimant. 

AMENDED DRAFT 

An act to amend Section 1263 of the Civil Code, relating to contents of declaration of homestead. 

The people of the State of California do enact as follows: 

SECTION 1. Section 1263 of the Civil Code is amended to read: 

1263. The declaration of homestead must contain: 

1. A statement showing that the person making it is the head of a family, and if the claimant is married, the name of the spouse; or, when the declaration is made by the wife, showing that her husband has not made such declaration and that she therefore makes the declaration for their joint benefit; 

2. A statement that the person making it is residing on the premises, and claims them as a homestead; 

3. A description of the premises; 

4. An estimate of their actual cash value; 

5. Such declaration of homestead may further contain a statement of the character of the property sought to be homesteaded, showing the improvement or improvements which have been affixed thereto, with sufficient detail to show that it is a proper subject of homestead, and that no former declaration has been made, or, if made, that it has been abandoned and if it contains such further statement and the declaration is supported by the affidavit of the declarant, annexed thereto, that the matters therein stated are true of his or her own knowledge, such declaration, when properly recorded, shall be prima facie evidence of the facts therein stated, and conclusive evidence thereof in favor of a purchaser or encumbrancer in good faith and for a valuable consideration.

Committee Memorandum on Amended Draft 

It will be observed that the amended proposal retains the present language of the statute and that parenthetic language is inserted referring to improvements. The purpose of this change in language between the two drafts is to avoid any inference that the amendment is intended to restrict the permissible content of the statement. 

E. SECTIONS 1013 AND 1013.5 OF THE CIVIL CODE 

An act to amend Section 1013 of the Civil Code and to add a new section to said code to be numbered 1013.5, relating to removal of improvements from real property. 

The people of the State of California do enact as follows: 

SECTION 1. Section 1013 of the Civil Code is hereby amended to read: 

1013. When a person affixes his property to the land of another, without an agreement permitting him to remove it, the thing affixed, except as otherwise provided in section ten hundred and nineteen this chapter, belongs to the owner of the land, unless he chooses to require the former to remove it. 

SEC. 2. Section 1013.5 is added to the Civil Code, reading as follows: 

1013.5. When any person, acting in good faith and erroneously believing because of a a mistake either of law or fact that he has a right to do so, affixes improvements to the land of another, such person may bring an action in the superior court of the county where the property is situated to permit the removal of such improvements, on such terms as the court shall prescribe. 

The court by its judgment of removal shall make such award to the owner of the land as it shall deem equitable to compensate him for his damages and expenses, including attorneys' fees, resulting from such affixation and removal and for defending the action.

Memorandum on Amendment to Civil Code Section 1013 and Proposed New Section 1013.5 

Purpose. This measure is designed to improve the position of one who, because of a good faith mistake, affixes permanent improvements to the land of another. The proposed legislation would extend to such person the right to remove the improvements, pursuant to a court order authorizing such removal. Provision is made for full compensation to the owner of the realty, including the amount of attorneys' fees he might incur in defending the action in which removal is sought. 

Background. The general rule of the common law is that whatever a trespasser attaches to the land at once passes to the owner of the realty. 

There can, of course, be no quarrel with the rule as it applies to one who in bad faith appropriates the land of another as a building site. It is, however, equally clear that the rule is harsh and unjust when applied against an improver who is the innocent victim of a good faith mistake. 

There is no reason to bestow an undeserved gift upon the owner of the land.

For this reason the rigid common law rule has been modified in most jurisdictions, in varying degrees, to protect one who makes improvements under the good faith belief that he has a right to the land. Most states have enacted statutes, known as "occupying claimants acts" or "betterment acts' permitting a good faith improver to recover the value of the improvements. (Tiffany, Real Property, 3d Ed., 1939, Section 625.) The statutes so enacted are not uniform in their provisions. (See discussion in 

137 A. L. R. 1078.) In general, however, they provide that the landowner must, as a condition of his recovery of the land pay for the value of the improvements over and above the value of rents and profits during the period of the occupancy. (42 C. J. S., page 430.)

In California the law is well settled that, barring circumstances upon which to raise an estoppel against the landowner, a good faith improver has no rights beyond those accorded him by Section 741 of the Code of Civil Procedure. This section permits an innocent improver to offset the value of the permanent improvements against a claim of the owner of the realty for the recovery of rents, issues and profits. (Huse v. Den, 85 

Cal. 390, 401; Wood v. Henley, 88 Cal. App. 441, 462.) And if the owner of the realty does not seek to recover such damages, the innocent improver cannot assert the value of the permanent improvements at all, since “the value of the permanent improvements *** may be allowed only as a set-off to such damages as may be claimed for the withholding of the property sued for." (Kinard v. Kaelin, 22 Cal. App. 383, 389, emphasis added.) (Other cases collected in the California Annotations to the Restatement of Restitution, Section 52.)

It appears, therefore, that the California rule is more harsh than that of most other states. These other states have attempted varying solutions to the problem, all based on the idea that the owner of the land has no just claim to anything except the land itself and fair compensation for damage and loss of rent. Most of the "betterment acts" provide that the landowner must pay for the permanent improvements. (See, e.g. Ill. 

Anno. Stats., Vol. 45, Sections 53 to 58.) Provisions of this nature raise a problem as to whether or not it is fair to insist that the owner of land pay for improvements that he did not request and may not want. For this reason it is felt that something short of the conventional "betterment act" would be more desirable. The proposed amendments are designed, therefore, to accomplish the narrow purpose of permitting removal of the improvements with full compensation to the landowner. Such an enactment would protect the good faith improver in most cases, and would neither compel the landowner to purchase unwanted improvements nor cause him any other expense.

AMENDED DRAFT 

An act to amend Section 1013 of the Civil Code and to add a new section to said code to be numbered 1013.5, relating to removal of improvements from real property.

The people of the State of California do enact as follows: 

SECTION 1. Section 1013 of the Civil Code is hereby amended to read: 

1013. When a person affixes his property to the land of another, without an agreement permitting him to remove it,

the thing affixed, except as otherwise provided in section ten hundred and nineteen this chapter, belongs to the owner of the land, unless he chooses to require the former to remove it, or the former elects to exercise the right of removal provided for in Section 1013.5 of this chapter. 

SEC. 2. A new section is hereby added to said code, reading 

1013.5. When any person, acting in good faith and erroneously believing because of a mistake either of law or fact that he has a right to do so, affixes improvements to the land of another, such person shall have the right to remove such improvements upon his obtaining, in an action brought in the superior court of the county where the property is situated, a judgment permitting the removal, on such terms as the court shall prescribe. The court by its judgment of removal shall make such award to the owner of the land as it shall deem equitable to compensate him for his damages and expenses, including attorneys' fees, resulting from such affixation and removal and for defending the action.

Committee Memorandum on Amended Draft 

Some members of the committee felt that it might be said of the first draft of this measure that it did not clearly create a substantive right of removal. For this reason the proposed legislation was amended as above set forth.

F. SECTION 386.5 OF THE CODE OF CIVIL PROCEDURE 

An act to add a new section to the Code of Civil Procedure to be numbered 386.5, relating to dismissal of mere stakeholder on affidavit and deposit.

The people of the State of California do enact as follows: 

SECTION 1. A new section is hereby added to the Code of 

Civil Procedure, to be numbered 386.5, reading as follows: 

386.5. Where the only relief sought against one of the defendants is the payment of a stated amount of money alleged to be wrongfully withheld, such defendant may, upon affidavit that he is a mere stakeholder with no interest in the amount or any portion thereof and that conflicting demands have been made made upon him for the amount by parties to the action, apply to the court for an order discharging him from liability and dismissing him from the action on his depositing with the clerk of the court the amount in dispute and the court may, in its discretion, make such order. A copy of such order shall within 10 days after entry thereof be mailed to the attorneys for all parties who have appeared in the action. 

Memorandum on Proposed New Section 386.5, Code of Civil Procedure 

Purpose. This section would permit one who is party to an action wherein two or more other parties to the action seek from him nothing but a sum certain, to obtain an order dismissing him from the action upon his affidavit that such conflicting demands are made upon him

and on his depositing with the clerk of the court the full amount in dispute.

Background. Section 386 of the Code of Civil Procedure now pro vides that a defendant who is a mere stakeholder may apply to the court, upon affidavit that a person not a party to the action is also demanding the sum in dispute, and upon notice to such person and the adverse party, for an order discharging him from liability on his depositing the amount claimed, and substituting such person in his place. This procedure is unavailable where both persons claiming the amount are already parties to the action, since the affidavit must state that "a person not a party to the action makes * * * a demand."

Where both claimants are already parties to the action, the following procedure is sometimes followed: (a) The defendant stakeholder files an answer and cross complaint in interpleader. (b) The stakeholder then moves for an order to the effect that the disputed amount be deposited with the court and the stakeholder dismissed from the action. (Must also prepare affidavit, points and authorities, and notice of motion.) (c) The matter is then set on the calendar for hearing. Usually only the attorney for the stakeholder appears. The judge signs the order.

The proposed new Section 386.5 would provide for a simpler procedure, permitting the defendant stakeholder to be dismissed from the action (where the only relief sought against him is the payment of a stated amount) upon affidavit that he claims no interest in such sum and that parties to the action have made conflicting demands upon him for the same, upon his depositing said amount with the clerk of the court. The fact that the stakeholder may be dismissed, at the discretion of the court, before the other parties are notified, is not significant. The proposed statute is drawn so narrowly that the other parties cannot be prejudiced by such dimissal; if A and B seek $500 from C, who claims no interest therein, and C is willing to deposit the precise sum demanded with the court, A and B cannot be injured by the dismissal of C from the action. 

By making it possible for the court to make such an order ex parte, court calendars will be relieved of unnecessary hearings, considerable useless paper work will be eliminated, and it will be easier and quicker for one who has no interest in the outcome of the litigation to get out of it. 

AMENDED DRAFT 

An act to add a new section to the Code of Civil Procedure to be numbered 386.5, relating to dismissal of mere stakeholder on affidavit and deposit.

The people of the State of California do enact as follows: 

SECTION 1. A new section is hereby added to the Code of 

Civil Procedure, to be numbered 386.5, reading as follows: 

386.5. Where the only relief sought against one of the defendants is the payment of a stated amount of money alleged to be wrongfully withheld, such defendant may, upon affidavit that he is a mere stakeholder with no interest in the amount or

any portion thereof and that conflicting demands have been made upon him for the amount by parties to the action, upon notice to such parties, apply to the court for an order discharging him from liability and dismissing him from the action on his depositing with the clerk of the court the amount in dispute and the court may, in its discretion, make such order. 

Committee Memorandum on Amended Draft 

It will be observed that the proposal as originally drafted would have permitted a dismissal without any prior notice to the other parties to the action. The committee objected to the ex parte aspect of the proposal. 

The draft was, therefore, amended and the same language with reference to notice was used as is now found in Code of Civil Procedure Section 386. Section 386 provides for the substitution of a person not previously a party to the action upon affidavit by the stakeholder and states that the stakeholder may apply to the court for such an order "upon notice to such person and the adverse party." Relying upon this language, there has been inserted in the proposed new Section 386.5 the language "upon notice to such parties."

The following bills were also proposed by the California Land Title Association, and there is set forth after the bill an explanation submitted as to the reasons for the measure. However, as to these bills, the committee reports them as a matter of information only and as of the time of writing this report has not determined to sponsor either of the measures. On the contrary, at the time of writing this report, the committee had determined to investigate each of the measures further before deciding the desirability thereof.

G. SECTION 787 AND SECTION 1532 OF THE PROBATE CODE 

An act to amend Section 787 of the Probate Code, relating to sales on credit by an executor or administrator. 

The people of the State of California do enact as follows:

SECTION 1. Section 787 of the Probate Code is amended to read: 

787. If a sale is made upon a credit, the executor or administrator must take the note or notes of the purchaser for the unpaid portion of the purchase money, with a mortgage or deed of trust on the property to secure their payment -, which mortgage or deed of trust shall be subject only to encumbrances existing at date of sale and such other encumbrances as the court shall approve.

An act to amend Section 1532 of the Probate Code, relating to sales on credit by a guardian.

The people of the State of California do enact as follows: 

SECTION 1. Section 1532 of the Probate Code is amended to read: 

1532. All sales must be for cash or for part cash and part deferred payments, the credit in no case to exceed 10 years from

the date of sale, and the terms being subject to the approval of the court. When real property is sold upon deferred payments, the guardian must demand and receive from the purchasers, notes, and a mortgage or deed of trust on the property sold, with such additional security as the court deems necessary and sufficient to secure the prompt payment of the amounts so deferred, and the interest thereon. The mortgage or deed of trust herein provided for shall be subject only to encumbrances existing at date of sale and such other encumbrances as the court shall approve.

Memorandum on Proposed Amendments to Probate Code Sections 787 and 

1532 Sometimes a probate court will authorize a sale on credit where the executor or administrator takes, as security for the unpaid balance, a deed of trust junior to a deed of trust placed on the property by the purchaser. Section 787 of the Probate Code, authorizing sales on credit, does not require that the note for the unpaid balance must be secured by a first mortgage or trust deed. However, neither does Section 787 expressly authorize a sale on credit with the unpaid balance secured by a mortgage or deed of trust inferior to one placed on the property by the purchaser. The same problem arises under Section 1532, relating to sales by a guardian.

The proposed amendments would make it clear that the court has authority to approve such sales, whether by an executor or administrator (Section 787) or by a guardian (Section 1532). This would be done by providing, in effect, that where the security for the unpaid balance of the purchase price is a mortgage or deed of trust inferior to other encumbrances (except those existing at date of sale) the court may approve the sale subject to such encumbrances. As to encumbrances existing before the date of sale (e.g. those placed on the property by the decedent before his death) no such express approval is required. 

H. SECTION 1198.1 OF THE CODE OF CIVIL PROCEDURE An act to amend Section 1198.1 of the Code of Civil Procedure relating to mechanics' liens.

The people of the State of California do enact as follows: 

SECTION 1. Section 1198.1 of the Code of Civil Procedure is amended to read: 

1198.1. (a) No lien provided for in this chapter binds any property for a longer period than 90 days after the same has been filed except as hereinafter provided, unless within that time, proceedings to enforce the same be commenced in a proper court within that time to enforce the same and a notice of pendency of such proceedings be filed as provided in Section 409 of this code. If a credit be given and notice of the fact and terms of such credit be filed in the office of the county recorder subsequent to the filing of such lien and prior to the expiration of said 90-day period, then such lien continues in force until 90 days after the expiration of such credit, but no lien continues in force by reason of any agreement to give credit for a longer

time than one year from the time the work is completed. If the proceedings to enforce the lien be not prosecuted to trial within two years after the commencement thereof, the court may in its discretion dismiss the same for want of prosecution, and in all cases the dismissal of such action (unless it be expressly stated that the same is without prejudice) or a judgment rendered therein that no lien exists shall be equivalent to the cancellation and removal from the record of such lien. (b) As against any purchaser or encumbrancer for value and in good faith whose rights are acquired subsequent to the expiration of the 90-day period following the filing of such lien, no giving of credit or extension of the lien or time to enforce the same shall be effective unless evidenced by a notice or agreement filed for record in the office of the county recorder prior to the acquisition of the rights of such purchaser or encumbrancer, and no proceedings to enforce the lien which include a personal action and issuance of attachment as provided in Section 1200 of this code shall be effective unless a notice of pendency of such proceedings is filed for record in the office of the county recorder, as provided in Section 409 of this code. 

Memorandum on Proposed Amendment to Section 1198.1 of the Code of Civil 

Procedure 

Purpose. Section 1198.1 of the Code of Civil Procedure now provides that no mechanic's lien shall bind any property for more than 90 days from the date it was filed unless within the 90 days (1) Credit be given, and notice of the fact and terms of such credit be filed in the office of the county recorder, or (2) Proceedings be commenced in a proper court to enforce the lien. 

The proposed amendment would provide, in addition, that a notice of pendency of the enforcement proceedings must be filed, within the 90 days, in the office of the county recorder of the county in which the land is situated.

Background. Section 1193.1 of the Code of Civil Procedure provides that a person asserting a mechanic's lien must, within certain specified time limits, file in the recorder's office a claim of lien. This entitles such person to a lien for a period of 90 days, at which time the lien terminates unless a credit is given and the appropriate notice thereof is filed in the recorder's office, or-and this is the point of present concern-" ceedings be commenced in a proper court within that time to enforce the same. (C. C. P. 1198.1.) 

"pro- It will be noted that one of the two possible methods of extending the lien beyond 90 days requires, as a condition to its effectiveness, that notice be filed in the recorder's office. This is consistent with the general policy of the law of real property and of the recording statutes, directed to the end that a prospective purchaser or lender may find in one place those matters which directly affect title to realty. 

Indeed, another section (409) of the Code of Civil Procedure provides, in effect, that if an action is filed affecting the title to real property, and the plaintiff desires to put others on constructive notice of the pendency of such action, he may record a notice of pendency of the action. In 1918,

however, it was held, in Tulloh v. Boyce, 37 Cal. App. 761, that filing of a notice of lis pendens under Section 409 was not necessary to preserve the lien against a purchaser pendente lite where the action was one to foreclose a mechanic's lien, and was commenced within the 90-day period. 

The provision in Section 1198.1 that no such lien shall be binding for more than 90 days "unless proceedings be commenced in a proper court within that time to enforce the same" is the same as appeared in Section 1190 of the same code, when enacted in 1872. And certainly in 1872 there was little extra burden imposed upon those who had to determine whether the lien, because an action was commenced, continued in existence beyond 90 days. In those days the county clerk's office was probably across the hall, or downstairs, from the recorder's office, providing a quick and easy way to determine whether an action had been filed. 

It was not until 1929 that the Legislature, by an amendment to Section 29 of the Municipal Court Act, provided that such courts should have jurisdiction over mechanics' liens foreclosures. (Stats 1929, page 838; now C. C. P. Sec. 89.) This move, together with the great growth of our 

State and the increased number of municipal courts, puts the statute in question in a different light than it appeared when enacted originally, in 1872.

The purchaser of property or lender on property in Los Angeles County, to protect himself against a pending action to foreclose a mechanic's lien, must search the records in 16 different municipal courts. 

These 16 separate municipal courts are located within a radius of some 30 miles from the Hall of Records in downtown Los Angeles. He would have to cover a total distance of well over 200 miles, and it would undoubtedly take him at least two days to make the necessary search. 

Nor is the problem one unique to Los Angeles County. Other counties have municipal courts located away from the county offices. In Alameda 

County, for example, there is a municipal court in the City of Alameda, one in Berkeley, one in Oakland and one in San Leandro. 

It may be true that mechanics' liens, because of their constitutional source (Art. XX, Sec. 15), are not ordinary liens, and are treated with special respect by courts and the Legislature. 

The question, however, is really one of balance. And nothing in the 

Constitution indicates that the Legislature, in providing for "speedy and efficient enforcement of such liens" (Art. XX, Sec. 15) should fail to strike a reasonable balance. Admittedly, it is somewhat easier not to file a lis pendens than to file one. On the other hand, the heavy burden now imposed upon prospective purchasers or lenders would seem to weigh heavier in the scales. And it should be kept in mind that no special obligations are being created; the proposal would simply extend to this group the policy of the law, as expressed in Code of Civil Procedure Section 409, that filing an action "affecting the title to or the right of possession of real property' does not operate as constructive notice unless a notice of pendency thereof is recorded. 

NOTE: At the direction of the committee further investigation was undertaken with regard to the desirability of the amendments proposed in Subdivisions G and H above, and at the time of publication of this

report the following additional information on the subject had been received: 

BUILDING MATERIAL DEALERS' CREDIT ASSOCIATION MR. JOHN A. BOHN, Counsel Senate Interim Judiciary Committee 

459 Holbrook Building, 58 Sutter Street 

San Francisco 4, California 

February 10, 1953 

DEAR MR. BOHN: We appreciate your writing us on January 16th asking for the views of this association with respect to Senate Bill No. 172, an act to amend 

Section 1198.1, Code of Civil Procedure, relating to the enforcement of mechanics' 

liens.

This association has a most friendly interest in any bill introduced at the request of the California Land Title Association. We have had the friendly cooperation of the various title companies in California, and their legal departments, for many years. Our retained counsel, Attorney Glen Behymer, is chairman of the executive committee and a member of the board of directors of the Land 

Title Insurance Co. 

Our association attorney, Mr.

Behymer, cooperated and supported the approval of the Title Insurance and Trust Company's amendment in 1949 to Section 1190 

C. C. P., which consisted of the last paragraph to Section 1190 as amended in 1949.

At that time this amendment seemed to cure the evils of that phase of the subject matter so far as the title man's interest was concerned. Now it appears to us that they are requesting that we take a step backward and require in every instance the recording of a lis pendens in a mechanics' lien foreclosure action. This would add a very considerable burden of expense to mechanics' lien claimants and additional work to their counsels. It would appear to us that the filing of a lis pendens in all cases of foreclousure of mechanics' liens would be an unnecessary burden and an economic waste.

The slight advantage to the title companies along the lines indicated in your letter, we believe, would be very small compared to the heavy burden imposed upon the lien claimants. 

You refer to municipal court actions and to the proposition that it is the case that there is more than one municipal court in certain large counties. A proper title plant or proper searching would not make it too difficult for the title company to ascertain whether the mechanics' lien had been kept alive, either by foreclosure or by timely recording of an extension agreement. 

Yours very truly, (Signed) 

Law Offices 

J. M. DEAN Secretary-Manager

January 20, 1953

MR. JOHN A. BоHN, Counsel Senate Interim Judiciary Committee 

459 Holbrook Building, 58 Sutter Street

DEAR MR. BOHN: I am replying to your letter in which you request the views of the California Bankers Association with respect to three enclosed proposed bills, one amending Section 787 of the Probate Code, one amending Section 1532 

of the Probate Code and one amending Section 1198.1 of the Code of Civil Pro cedure.

While the appropriate committee of the California Bankers Association has not officially acted upon these bills I think I can safely say that they would each have the approval of the California Bankers Association, and particularly the bill amending Section 1198.1 of the Code of Civil Procedure. This bill is now quite necessary in view of the fact that many mechanic's liens suits are filed in the municipal court in the larger counties and, therefore, there is no central office, the files of which would give notice of the filing of these suits foreclosing

mechanic's liens. Furthermore the bill is consistent with long standing practice in 

California of providing for the recording of the lis pendens when actions are filed affecting title to real property. 

Thank you for referring these bills to me.
