contributory negligence of plaintiff." 26 The two essential elements necessary to permit the plaintiff to recover are a showing that the plaintiff's negligence was no more than slight, and that the defendant's negligence is gross in comparison to the slight negligence of the plaintiff. "The reference to gross negligence in the statute has no significance except in making the comparison between plaintiff's negligence, which the jury must find to be no more than slight, and the negligence of the defendant, which must be great or gross by comparison." "127 As indicated above, there are states which differ from Nebraska and South Dakota in that they attempt to define degrees of negligence, although with no uniformity from state to state,28 and the effect of such a statute as that adopted by these two states might be very different elsewhere. California is one of the states which has had occasion from time to time to attempt to differentiate between degrees of negligence.29 "Slight negligence" probably exists as a distinct degree of actionable conduct, and although the courts have had little occasion to recognize it, it consists in the failure to use great care where such care is required.30 The California courts have also had occasion to attempt to define "gross negligence," both generally 31 and in connection with special statutes, such as the original "guest" law, or the workmen's compensation statute providing extra penalties against an employer found guilty of "serious and wilful misconduct." 33 The courts of this State have determined that "gross negligence" is something short of wilful or wanton misconduct.3 Despite the fact that the appellate court has indicated that "the term gross negligence is incapable of precise definition" and that its application "may in some cases lead to unsatisfactory results," 35 it is nevertheless apparently a term with some significance in California, which might lead the courts of this State to give a "comparative negligence" statute such as that of Nebraska and South Dakota a meaning quite different from that given to it in the courts of those two states.

Both Nebraska and South Dakota have statutory provisions limiting the liability of automobile drivers to guests. Nebraska permits recovery by such guests only when the driver is "under the influence of intoxicating liquor" or is guilty of gross negligence. That state has held that its "comparative negligence" statute is applicable to actions involving such hosts and guests, and that the defense of contributory negligence on the part of the guest was not abolished by the adoption of the statute. South 

Dakota provides that liability for injury to guests either in automobiles or airplanes is limited to damage caused by the wilful and wanton misconduct of the driver or operator, and that either wilful conduct or lack of ordinary care by the guest may be a complete defense." 38 The statutes of the two states also differ with respect to the right of contribution between joint tortfeasors. Nebraska recognizes no such right, while South Dakota has adopted the Uniform Contribution Among Tortfeasors Act.39 Under that statute a joint tortfeasor is entitled to judgment for contribution when he has by payment discharged the common liability or paid more than his prorata share thereof, but there is no right to such contribution if a settlement is made which does not extinguish the liability of the remaining joint tortfeasor. When there is a disproportion of fault among joint tortfeasors so as to render inequitable an equal distribution among them

of the common liability, relative degrees of fault of such joint tortfeasors may be considered in determining their prorata shares. Wisconsin

Wisconsin, while preserving the notion that the negligence of the plaintiff must be less than that of the defendant in order for him to recover, does not use the "slightgross" terminology of the preceding two statutes. The Wisconsin law provides: "Contributory negligence shall not bar recovery in an action by any person or his legal representative to recover damages for negligence resulting in death or in injury to person or property, if such negligence was not as great as the negligence of the person against whom recovery is sought, but any damages allowed shall be diminished in the proportion to the amount of negligence attributable to the person recovering." 99 40 The courts have interpreted the statute to mean that the dividing line between the right of the plaintiff to recover and contributory negligence as a complete defense is 50 percent. Thus if the plaintiff is found to have been guilty of 49 percent of the negligence, and the defendant of 51 percent, the plaintiff is entitled to recover, but his damages are reduced by the 49 percent. On the other hand, if the plaintiff is 50 percent responsible for the accident, he recovers nothing.¹¹ 

Where more than two parties are involved in an accident, the Wisconsin courts interpret the statute to require that the negligence of the plaintiff be measured against the negligence of each of the defendants, not against the combined negligence of all such defendants.2 Thus, where the plaintiff is negligent to the extent of 20 percent, one defendant is similarly negligent to the extent of 20 percent, and the third party is negligent to the extent of 60 percent, then as to the first defendant the plaintiff may not recover at all, but as to the second he may recover 80 percent of his damages. 

There is no statutory provision in Wisconsin for contribution between joint tortfeasors, but the courts had recognized such a right in the absence of statute even prior to the enactment of the "comparative negligence" statute. The courts have, however, refused to change their former holdings with respect to contribution, in order to make that right depend upon the degrees of negligence of the respective joint tortfeasors, but preserve the former ruling that such joint tortfeasors shall share the liability equally, regardless of their respective degrees of blame.** 

Wisconsin has no "guest" statute limiting the liability of the driver of an automobile, but a gratuitous guest assumes any risk involved in any latent defect of the vehicle as well as the known habits, skill and condition of the driver, and if injury occurs through such hazards, recovery is not allowed. Assumption of risk is distinguished from contributory negligence, and where assumption of risk is found to be present, the "comparative negligence" statute has no application. Where, however, such guest is injured through the negligence of a third person, and has assumed the risk as far as his host is concerned, the third party may be responsible for the entire amount of the guest's damage.45 Mississippi 

The Mississippi statute provides: "In all actions hereafter brought for personal injuries, or where such injuries have resulted in death, or for injury to property, the fact that the person injured, or the owner of the property, or person having control over the property may have been guilty of contributory negligence shall not bar a recovery, but damages shall be diminished by the jury in proportion to the amount of negligence

attributable to the person injured, or the owner of the property, or the person having control over the property." "46 "All questions of negligence and contributory negligence shall be for the jury to determine." 47 The basic difference between this statute and the four above referred to is obvious. 

Under the Mississippi law the comparative degrees of negligence of the plaintiff and the defendant are irrelevant. If the defendant is guilty of any negligence at all, however slight, causing or contributing to the plaintiff's injury, then the plaintiff is entitled to recover, no matter how gross his own negligence may be by comparison. 

Mississippi does not allow the use of counterclaim or cross-complaint, and accordingly where both parties have been guilty of some degree of negligence, two separate actions are necessary.49 48 There is no right of contribution between joint tortfeasors in Mississippi,50 and the state has no "guest" statute affecting liability as between host and guest. Ontario 51 The Ontario statute is typical of those in operation in the various Canadian provinces, although more detailed in respect to its provisions affecting practice and procedure. The particular sections which parallel those quoted from the American jurisdictions are as follows: 

"2 (1). Where damages have been caused or contributed to by the fault or neglect of two or more persons the court shall determine the degree in which each of such persons is at fault or negligent, and, except as provided by subsections (2) and (3), where two or more persons are found at fault or negligent, they shall be jointly and severally liable to the persons suffering loss or damage for such fault or negligence, but as between themselves, in the absence of any contract express or implied, each shall be liable to make contribution and indemnify each other in the degree in which they are respectively found to be at fault or negligent." 52 "3. In any action for damages which is founded upon the fault or negligence of the defendants if fault or negligence is found on the part of the plaintiff which contributed to the damages, the court shall apportion the damages in proportion to the degree of fault or negligence found against the parties respectively." 53 

This statute embodies the same general principle with respect to "comparative negligence" as does the Mississippi law, namely that liability is not based upon a greater or lesser degree of fault on the part of the plaintiff, but the defendant who is guilty of any causative negligence whatsoever is liable to the plaintiff in damages. The result is simply that the plaintiff's recovery is diminished in proportion to the degree of fault or negligence found against him. There is a recorded instance in Ontario where the plaintiff's damages were assessed at $10,000 but his negligence was set at 98 percent, and accordingly his actual recovery was only $200.5 The elaborate nature of the procedural provisions of the Ontario statute distinguishes it from that of Mississippi in actual operation, since it is possible under the Ontario law to have counterclaims and cross-complaints, and to bring in third parties not originally sued. 

As indicated above, there is provision for contribution between joint tortfeasors, 50 even in the event of settlement of a claim, if the court is persuaded that the amount of the settlement was reasonable. Subdivisions (2) and (3) of § 2 relate to the problem posed by conflict between the original "comparative negligence" statute and the "guest" statute of the province, and a similar problem posed by suits involving spouses. Both provide 58 that no damages shall be recoverable for the portion of the

loss or damage caused by the fault or negligence of the owner or driver, when a guest is injured, or by the fault or negligence of the spouse, and the portion of loss or damage so caused is to be determined, although such owner or driver or such spouse is not a party to the action. The importance of these provisions will appear later in the discussion as to problems raised by the adoption of a "comparative negligence" statute in jurisdictions having such statutory provisions. The Ontario statute also provides for apportionment of costs, and permits the court to require the plaintiff to bear part of such costs when circumstances render such apportionment just.59 

QUESTIONS TO BE CONSIDERED IN ADOPTION OF ANY "COMPARATIVE NEGLIGENCE” STATUTE 

As indicated above, the premise upon which the proponents of some sort of "comparative negligence" doctrine base their argument, is that the common law doctrine of "contributory negligence" is unduly harsh. The argument is variously worded, but in its essence it points customarily to the problem presented where the plaintiff may have been guilty of the "slightest" degree of negligence, amounting to no more than 1 percent of the fault, but which was nevertheless a proximate cause of the accident, and is therefore barred from any recovery, allowing the defendant, who was 99 percent responsible, to go scott free. The customary illustration is that of an elderly person, or a very young child, crossing the street under some conditions such as a rainy or stormy night, struck down by a drunken motorist driving a car at 100 miles per hour. 

Of course, under the present doctrine of "contributory negligence" as a bar to recovery, it is not quite truthful to state that the defendant goes completely free. In such cases, where the negligence of both parties operates to cause the accident, neither party recovers from the other, but each bears his own damage, whatever it may be. The basis of the doctrine of "contributory negligence" has been variously explained, both as a deterrent to negligent actions on the part of both parties, and on the basis that it simply establishes the principle that no one should be permitted to profit by his own wrongdoing." 60 Judge William J. Palmer of the Los Angeles Superior Court, has stated the basis for the doctrine succinctly and in comparison with other principles of American jurisprudence. He said: **** * the law of contributory negligence is one of several rules that stem from a basic disciplinary policy, attitude and dignity of our jurisprudence. It is a policy that both reflects and contributes to the moral fibre of the people, that provides disciplinary measures, without necessity of criminal action, for certain wrongdoing, that keeps in the foreground for the attention of all concerned, standards of conduct known to be necessary for the preservation of a decent civilization. For example, this juristic policy says: 'If you, yourself, have broken a contract, one of your penalties is that you may not recover damages for a breach by another. Even if we were able to compare the seriousness and effect of your breach against that of the other party, we would feel that our courts ought not to be burdened with the claims of one contract breaker against another.' "The same policy says that he who comes into equity must come with clean hands. It says: 'If you want the courts to be available to you for equitable assistance and relief, see that your own conduct measures up to a reasonable standard in morals, law and equity.' "The same disciplinary principle announces generally that a person may be estopped by his own conduct from complaining of the conduct of another. And from this same underlying conception of social discipline came the law of contributory negligence, which says to a guilty plaintiff: 'You, too, violated the rules, and unnecessarily endangered your own safety, and possibly that of others. We leave you where we found you'." "

As against that point of view, the proponents of "comparative negligence" urge that it accomplishes greater justice between the parties than does the contributory negligence doctrine. Accordingly, it seems appropriate to consider the various problems that are involved in the adoption of any form of the doctrine of "comparative negligence," and how the various proposals which have been adopted in other jurisdictions measure up to a standard of "justice." 

50 Idem, § 7. See Appendix A for full text. 

00 See, for example, 45 C. J. 941. 

61 Superior Judge William J. Palmer, "Let Us Be Frank About Comparative Neg ligence," Los Angeles Bar Bulletin, November, 1952, reprinted in The Los Angeles 

Daily Journal, November 4, 1952.

For purposes of the following discussion, the problems are presented as they would arise if California, with its present laws, chose to adopt on the one hand a statute similar to the Wisconsin law, or on the other hand the doctrine as adopted by Mississippi or the Canadian provinces. The Wisconsin statute has been selected as illustrative of those requiring a lesser degree of fault on the part of the plaintiff than the defendant before recovery will be allowed, rather than one of those presenting a "slight-gross" negligence test, both because more information seems to be available in respect to its functioning and because, as indicated above, the "slight-gross" negligence test would be apt to involve California in the extremely difficult problem of degrees of negligence. 

1. Apportionment of Fault a. Generally. The first decision which obviously must be made is between the basic principle embodied in the Wisconsin statute, which permits the plaintiff to recover only if he is guilty of less than half of the causative negligence, and that of the Mississippi and Canadian statutes, which never bar recovery, but diminish damages in proportion to the plaintiff's own fault. In connection with this basic choice between the two principles, it is interesting to note that there seems to be no uniformity even among the advocates of "comparative negligence" as to which is preferable. Mr. Hayes, for instance, in connection with the Wisconsin statute, says: *** It is not my thought that even though a person be injured he should recover something for his injury, even in this machine age, if it is judicially declared that the injured's negligence was greater than that of the person against whom he seeks recovery, because in justice it does not seem to me that if a person is more to blame for his own injury than the person is who injured him, that the injured person should recover damages. In that respect I am in agreement with our Wisconsin statute to the point that if the plaintiff is as negligent or more so, than the person against whom recovery is sought, there shall be no recovery ***" 62 But another voice raised in that state 63 points to the operation of the statute in cases where the negligence of the two parties involved is comparatively close (discussed in more detail later in this statement), and insists that the 50 percent rule should be abolished. On the other hand, Mr. Lipscomb of the Mississippi bar, says with respect to the statute in his state: "At the same time it seems to us that the Mississippi statute goes too far. 

There is undoubtedly a happy and just medium between the rigor of the common law doctrine which denied in toto the rights of a comparatively innocent plaintiff as against a grossly negligent defendant, and a system of jurisprudence under which a grossly negligent plaintiff may recover against a comparatively innocent defendant. It seems to us that the ends of justice, as well as purely practical considerations, require that even the most liberal comparative negligence statutes provide that unless the negligence of the plaintiff is less in degree than that of the defendant he cannot recover ***. "If any comparative negligence legislation is offered in your state in the future it is recommended that you give serious consideration to the vices which are inherent in a statute which permits a plaintiff guilty of as much as 99 percent of the causative negligence to recover, and the relative virtue of a statute which denies recovery to a plaintiff unless his negligence is less than that of the defendant ***"64 In one of the leading law review articles dealing with the subject, however, Mississippi is pointed to as being more successful than any other state in the application of the doctrine of "comparative negligence." Despite the fact that the Mississippi statute permits the exact converse of the contributory negligence rule complained of as unduly harsh, namely that one who is 99 percent responsible may, nonetheless, collect from one whose negligence is only percent responsible for the accident, that 

62 Gerald P. Hayes, President Wisconsin Bar Association, "The Practical Working of a Comparative Negligence Statute," an address delivered before the New York 

State Bar Association, January 26, 1950. 

63 Richard V. Campbell, "Ten Years of Comparative Negligence," 16 Wisconsin Law 

R. 289. 

04 Hubert S. Lipscomb, "Comparative Negligence," Insurance Law Journal, September, 1951. 

65 A. Chalmers Mole and Lyman P. Wilson, "Comparative Negligence," Cornell Law 

Q., April, 1932, and June, 1932.

statute is characterized as approaching "more closely the ideals of theoretical and practical justice than the harsh and inflexible rule of contributory negligence which assumes that the total burden, financially and physically, must lie upon the injured party who has been slightly negligent ***" Another of the leading advocates of the doctrine of "comparative negligence" objects to the Mississippi statute as too limited in its practical workings, but advocates the same basic principle with the modifications as to pleading and practice to be found in the Ontario statute." b. As Applied Between Two Parties. The Wisconsin statute, when applied as between two parties only, appears to accomplish substantial justice, if the plaintiff is guilty of a very small portion of the causative negligence. For instance, where the plaintiff is found to be guilty of only 1 percent of the total negligence involved, and the defendant guilty of 99 percent of that negligence, and both suffer equal damage of $10,000, those living with the statute seem to have no quarrel with a result which gives to that plaintiff $9,900 and leaves the defendant responsible for his own $10,000 plus the $9,900 which he must pay. Where, however, the degrees of negligence are closer together, the result is very different. If the plaintiff is found to be responsible for 49 percent of the negligence, and the defendant for 51 percent, the plaintiff, under the Wisconsin doctrine, recovers $5,100, and the defendant still sustains his total damage of $10,000, plus the $5,100 he must pay, making their respective losses $4,900 and $15,100, although their negligence is almost equal. On the basis of the operation of the Wisconsin rule in these close situations, it has been recommended that the Wisconsin rule be changed and that of the Mississippi or Canadian statutes adopted in its stead.68

The principle of the Mississippi and Canadian statutes, on the other hand, accomplishes an exchange of dollars between the partes, regardless of the comparative degrees of their fault. It must be borne in mind that this form of the doctrine permits recovery not only by a plaintiff who is guilty of "slight" negligence, or a small quantum of the total negligence resulting in the accident, but also by one who is principally responsible. Although in theory each party's liability is in proportion to his share of the blame, the actual dollars and cents cost involved points up the accuracy of the term used by one of the advocates of a new system, who calls the new doctrine "loss distribution." It is actually the amount of damages sustained by the respective parties, rather than their comparative degrees of fault, which determines the dollars and cents responsibility. For instance, a plaintiff sustaining $100,000 damage and found to have been 90 percent responsible for the accident may still collect $10,000 worth of that damage from the defendant, who is only 10 percent responsible. If the defendant in turn suffered only minor damage, comparatively, in the sum of $1,000 he is entitled to recover $900 of his damage, but stands a net loss of $9,200, as the reward for his relatively minor share of the fault. 

A practitioner at the Mississippi bar points out in addition the practical results of the statute in operation, which he characterizes as "ridiculous," in the following language: "To illustrate: Jonathan Doe and Richard Roe, each precisely astride the center line of the highway and traveling at the same rate of speed, have a head-on collision. Each is damaged in the sum of $1,000. Doe sues Roe and, because each was guilty 50 percent of the negligence, recovers only $500. Under Mississippi law there is no provision for a counterclaim by a defendant in a tort action. 

So Roe has to file a separate suit against Doe. Also, because he was equally at fault, he recovers only $500. Doe has gotten some of Roe's insurer's dough, Roe has gotten some of Doe's insurer's dough, the attorneys for both, having the cases on a contingent fee basis, have cut both pots, the court officials have collected two sets of fees, and the insuring public will sooner or later have to pay the freight. If we assume that neither was insured, they have simply swapped dollars, losing half of them in the process. The illustration may seem extreme. It is not. 

With the exception that cases where each party is guilty of precisely 50 percent of the negligence are rare, it happens all the time. For instance, A suing B, is found guilty of 90 percent of the negligence and B only 10 percent. A's damages amount to $1,000 so the jury is required under the statute to award him only 

68 Idem. 

07 Charles O. Gregory, University of Chicago, "Loss Distribution by Comparative 

Negligence," Minnesota Law R., December, 1936. 

68 Richard V. Campbell, "Ten Years of Comparative Neligence," 16 Wisconsin Law 

R. 289. 

Charles O. Gregory, University of Chicago, "Loss Distribution by Comparative 

Negligence," Minnesota Law R., December, 1936.

$100. If we assume that B's damages were likewise $1,000, then B could turn around and sue A and would be entitled to recover $900.” 70 

Although the problem presented by the Mississippi statute would not be as acute in a jurisdiction recognizing counterclaims or cross-complaints, nevertheless, whenever the negligence of the two parties is comparatively close, and their damages approximately equal, the litigation results, as pointed out above, in an exchange of dollars belonging to the parties, minus only one instead of two sets of attorney's fees and court costs, or to a similar exchange between insurance carriers. 

Even as between two parties, and as will be pointed out below, the problem where more than two parties are concerned is much more complicated, there is a further question as to the exact manner in which the fault shall be apportioned. One of the advocates of the "comparative negligence" doctrine insists that the only fair method is to treat all the negligence involved as a unit, and divide it up among the parties involved, and govern their claims and recoveries accordingly." This hypothesis means that the total damages sustained by both parties should be divided between them in proportion to their respective degrees of fault. Assume A sustains damage of $10,000 and is 40 percent responsible for the accident, while B sustains damage of $5,000 and is 60 percent responsible. If the damage is treated as a unit, it amounts to $15,000, and since A was 40 percent responsible he should bear $6,000 of the loss, while B bears $9,000. As between two parties that is the result which is achieved by operation of the Mississippi and Canadian doctrines. A's damages are reduced by 40 percent, so that he recovers $6,000 from B and sustains the remaining loss of $4,000. B's damages are reduced by 60 percent, and he recovers $2,000 from A, sustaining the remaining $3,000 of his own damage. When A has paid his $2,000 to B, and his own damage of $4,000 over and above his recovery, he has equalled his $6,000 share of the total loss, as has B when he pays $6,000 to A, and his own $3,000 for which he has no right of recovery.

The Wisconsin statute does not accomplish the same result, under the provision that plaintiff's recovery is diminished by the jury in proportion to the negligence attributable to him. Under the same set of facts above set forth, A, under Wisconsin law, would recover 60 percent of his damages from B, or $6,000, sustaining an actual loss of the difference between that recovery and his total damage, or $4,000. B, on the other hand, is entitled to no recovery, and thus sustains his own $5,000 loss, plus the $6,000 he must pay A, or a total loss of $11,000, despite the fact that his share of all the damage, measured by his degree of fault, is only $9,000. This result has been criticized by a former president of the Wisconsin Bar Association in the following language: "I am not in agreement with the construction placed by our Supreme Court on the Wisconsin Comparative Negligence Statute, or, perhaps, I should say that I am not in agreement with the theory of our legislative enactment that if a plaintiff is 40 percent negligent, that he should recover 60 percent of his damages, or if he is 45 percent negligent, he should recover 55 percent of his damages, or that if he is 49 percent negligent, he should recover 51 percent of his damages. In the over-all view of our act, if a person injured is 10 percent negligent and the defendant is 90 percent negligent, I see no reason why the plaintiff should not recover the greater part of his damages, and in those situations under our act, it is my opinion that the situation is not unfair, but still I do not believe that the damages recoverable should be measured by the difference in percentages. It is my belief that if a plaintiff is 10 percent negligent and a defendant is 90 percent negligent, that he should recover 80 percent of his damages and not 90 percent, and that if a plaintiff is guilty of 49 percent negligence and a defendant of 51 percent negligence, that the plaintiff should recover 2 percent of his damages." 72

The principle advanced by Mr. Hayes as preferable to that actually in operation in Wisconsin would accomplish the same result as the Mississippi or Canadian doctrines and advocated by Professor Gregory, namely, that each party should bear a share of the total damage proportionate to his degree of fault, in any instance in

which the damage suffered by the parties is equal. In any case, however, in which the successful plaintiff sustains damage greater than that sustained by the defendant, the application of Mr. Hayes' suggestion results in the plaintiff sustaining a loss entirely disproportionate to his share of the total damage caused, and where the amounts are reversed, such a plaintiff escapes at a far less cost in dollars and cents than his share of that total damage. No recovery, leaving him to absorb his $10,000 loss plus $2,500 to A, or total of $12,500. c. As Applied Among Three or More Parties. The problem becomes more complicated when there are more than two parties involved in a collision, all of them more or less at fault. It has been posed in the following language by one of the advocates of "comparative negligence" as against the "contributory negligence" doctrine: "*** suppose T, A and B have a three-cornered collision in which each is equally negligent, only T suffering damage. If T sues A and B jointly, shall we say that half of the negligence should be attributed to each side of the law suit, thus permitting T to recover only 50 percent of his actual damage, or shall we say one-third is attributable to T and two-thirds to the defendants jointly? 

And if we do the latter, had we not best require a specific allocation of percentages to each of A and B, to govern their claims for contribution arising between them in that action? And to govern their mutual claims, should twothirds of the negligence be attributed to them jointly, or should it be one-third to each from the beginning, the size of T's recovery against them jointly to be determined by adding their respective percentages of fault? This may not seem to make much difference in the easy hypothetical case under discussion, but it is of great practical importance where the percentages of negligence as among the parties vary. "Now suppose in the above case that T chooses to sue only A. Shall B's negligence be included in determining the apportionment of fault, so that the finding would be one-third to T and one-third to A; and if so shall T recover for one-third or two-thirds of his damage, or shall B be ignored and the negligence fixed as 50 percent for T and the same for A, with T recovering just half of his damages from A? And, if so, shall T have action for the remaining onesixth from B? And if T sues only A, and A adds B as a third party in order to claim contribution, T not amending his complaint to include B, shall B still be ignored in allocating loss so that T shall recover only 50 percent of his actual loss, half of which A may shift to B; or shall T take two-thirds of his loss from 

A as if B were a codefendant, leaving A to shift half of his burden to B? "The situations could be varied almost infinitely * * * " 73

As has been indicated above, the Wisconsin statute is interpreted to mean that the plaintiff's negligence must be measured against that of each individual defendant, 73 Charles O. Gregory, "Loss Distribution by Comparative Negligence," Minnesota 

Law R., December, 1936.

and that he is entitled to recover only where his negligence is less in terms of percentages than that of the defendant against whom judgment is granted. In other words, in a situation where plaintiff is guilty of 20 percent of the causative negligence, one defendant is also guilty of 20 percent of that negligence, and the remaining defendant is responsible for 60 percent, the plaintiff is entitled to no recovery from the first defendant, but may recover 80 percent of his damages from the second. The result is obviously to require any defendant whose degree of fault is greater than that of the plaintiff to bear not only his own share of the responsibility, but that of his fellow defendants as well, if their negligence equals or is less than that of the plaintiff. 

Also, under the Wisconsin statute, the plaintiff is barred from recovery where his negligence may be less than the combined negligence of two or more defendants, but greater than that of any one single defendant, as in the instance where plaintiff may be guilty of 40 percent of the negligence, and each of two defendants guilty of 30 percent. It is difficult to defend either of these results in the name of "justice," and recommendations have been made for amendment of the Wisconsin statute in this respect.75

One possibility which has been suggested to meet the first of these problems is that in such instances the plaintiff's negligence shall be measured only against the negligence of parties responsible to him, and in the example cited plaintiff should recover only 663 percent of his damages rather than 80 percent. Another suggestion advanced to meet both problems is that the plaintiff should be limited in his recovery to instances where his negligence is less than the combined negligence of two or more defendants. Under such a provision, in the instance first given above, plaintiff would apparently be entitled to collect 80 percent of his damages from either defendant, if the principles of joint and several judgments were preserved, and the rights of the two defendants as against each other would be governed by whatever disposition is made of the problem of contribution between joint tortfeasors, discussed below. 

If such joint and several judgments are preserved, and no right of contribution is allowed, it is still difficult to defend in the name of "justice" a provision which permits a negligent plaintiff to collect from a defendant who is no more guilty than he, a substantial amount of damages caused principally by a third person. In the second instance, where the defendants are equally guilty, the results of such a provision are still difficult to defend in the name of "justice," unless some provision is made for contribution between them, since without such right of contribution, plaintiff might have 60 percent of his damages against either one, thus compelling one defendant to pay double his share of the total loss sustained, and to reimburse a plaintiff actually more negligent than he.

Same as B.

An examination of the application of the Mississippi or Canadian rule of comparative negligence, which does not bar recovery of any party, no matter what the degree of his negligence, but merely diminishes the damages in proportion to that negligence, to situations involving three parties makes even more clear the fact that such a rule has little relation to the comparative negligence of the parties, but is in fact a "distribution of loss." Assume such a situation where each of three parties is negligent, to some degree, and the negligence of each contributed not only to his own injury, but to the injuries of the other two. For illustrative purposes, assume that A is 25 percent responsible for the accident, B is 30 percent responsible, and C 45 percent to blame. A files suit against B and C, in a jurisdiction where cross-complaints and counterclaims permit B and C in turn to claim damages from A and from each other. 

Assume also a total of $30,000 damages, $5,000 suffered by one party, $10,000 by another, and $15,000 by the third. The resultant net recovery or net loss to the respective parties will depend not at all upon the extent of their respective negligence, but entirely on the amount of damage sustained. If A, who is only 25 percent responsible for the accident, manages to sustain the greatest amount of damage, he may come out on top in the shuffle, but if he sustains the least loss, he will end up paying not only his own damages, but paying out additional funds to B and C. C, on the other hand, whose negligence is greater than that of either of the other two, may end up paying the greatest share of the total loss, if his own damages are small, but if he is lucky enough to have the greatest damage, as well as the greatest share of blame, he may end up collecting from his fellow wrongdoers. 

Assume, for purposes of illustration that A, who is 25 percent responsible, sustains the $5,000 damages; B, whose fault is 30 percent, is the party sustaining $10,000 damages; and C, who is 45 percent to blame, sustains the $15,000 damages. A's recovery is diminished in proportion to his negligence, and he therefore recovers 75 percent of his $5,000, or $3,750, of which $1,500 is payable by B and $2,250 by C, in proportion to their respective degrees of negligence. B is in turn entitled to 70 percent of his damage, or $7,000, of which $2,500 is payable by A, and the remaining $4,500 by C. C, however, is entitled to 55 percent of his damages, since he was only 

45 percent responsible for the accident, and collects $8,250, of which $3,750 comes from A, and $4,500 from B. So A, whose negligence contributed the least to the accident, and whose own damage was also the least, ends up paying out $6,250 and collecting $3,750, so that he actually pays all of his own damages plus $2,500. C, on the other hand, whose negligence contributed most to the accident, but whose damages were also the greatest, ends up with a net recovery of $1,500. In each case what happens is that the individual party is responsible, as Professor Gregory's concept

indicates he should be, for his share of the total amount of damages sustained by all parties. Under the hypothetical case suggested here, A will always be responsible for 

25 percent of the total damage of $30,000, or $7,500; B will always be responsible for 30 percent of that total, or $9,000; and C will always be responsible for 45 percent of the total, or $13,500. That result sounds equitable on the surface, but the following tabulation, indicating the varying results in dollars and cents, depending on the amount of that damage sustained by each party, points out clearly the fact that the application of the doctrine in terms of the Mississippi or Canadian concept, if provision is included for counterclaims or cross-complaints, is in truth a "distribution of loss" and one which would seem to bear little relationship to the equity or justice.

Collects $2,500 toward his own damage.

The results, in terms of dollars and cents loss or recovery, are even more interesting if the assumption is made that in such a three-car collision, although each party is negligent, the negligence did not contribute to the damage of both other parties. 

2. Contribution Between Joint or Concurrent Tort-feasors 

The problem presented with respect to rights of contribution between joint or concurrent tort-feasors, in connection with the adoption of a "comparative negligence" statute, is stated by one of the advocates of the change as follows: "The device of contribution between joint tort-feasors seems to be essential to any practicable plan of thoroughgoing loss distribution in negligence actions. Its need in every case where jointly sued defendants are found to have negligently 1

contributed to the damage of a negligent plaintiff is readily apparent. The inconsistency of permitting one who is negligent to recover damages in spite of such negligence and of denying one who is negligent the right to shift part of the burden of paying some plaintiff's damages to another who jointly contributed with him to such damages, simply because such claim is for contribution instead of damages, is obvious." 77

California does not presently recognize, save in special circumstances which are not pertinent to this discussion, any right of contribution between joint or concurrent tort-feasors, but holds them jointly and severally liable for the entire damage." Therefore the Legislature of this State would have to give consideration to the propriety of some change in that respect, in connection with the adoption of any doctrine of "comparative negligence."

The Wisconsin statute makes no provision for such contribution, but, as indicated above, the courts of that state, contrary to most common law jurisdictions, permitted such right of contribution even before enactment of the comparative negligence statute. 

They have, however, refused to modify their former holdings that the responsibility should be divided equally among such joint or concurrent tort feasors and, in the absence of a special statutory provision, do not allow for such contribution in proportion to the amount of negligence charged against such defendants respectively. This again is a principle which is criticized by persons living under the Wisconsin statute," as well as other advocates of the "comparative negligence" doctrine, all of whom urge that the "comparative negligence" principle should be extended to contribution. 

The problem hardly needs illustration, but to take a simple case under the Wisconsin law, assume a situation where A is guilty of 15 percent negligence, B of 25 percent, and C of 60 percent. A may recover 85 percent of his damages from either B or C, but as between them each must bear 50 percent of the cost. Once the principle that a plaintiff should not be barred from recovery by reason of his negligence has been established, even when limited to instances where his negligence is less than that of the defendant, it is difficult to sustain, in the name of "justice," a result which compels one defendant to shoulder a part of the cost entirely disproportionate to his share of the responsibility.

Mississippi does not recognize the principle of contribution between joint or concurrent tortfeasors, as indicated above, and accordingly, although the statute of that state may be considered to do "justice" to the plaintiff, since it diminishes his damages in proportion to his share of the responsibility for the accident, to the extent that it were attempted to be applied to two or more defendants, it would seem to come farther from doing justice to those defendants whose proportionate share of the blame is far apart, than does even the rule of equal division applied in Wisconsin. 

Ontario has made special provision to cover the situation, as outlined by Justice Schroeder: “*** Under the common law there could not be contribution between joint tortfeasors. In cases in which a plaintiff sued two or three defendants, power was given to the court to determine the degree in which each of the defendants was at fault or negligent, although in a case where A recovered judgment for, say. $10,000 against B and C, he would still have his whole judgment against both B and C. An amendment was passed, however, to provide for the case of B and C raising the issue of liability inter se so that if B were found 60 percent to blame and C 40 percent, B would be entitled to a judgment against C indemnifying him to the extent that he was obligated to pay to A a sum in excess of $4,000. 

Thus by force of the statute there is now in Ontario contribution among joint tortfeasors." 81

As indicated by Professor Gregory, supra, it is difficult to defend the adoption of a principle which permits a negligent plaintiff to profit by his own wrong, to some extent 

Charles O. Gregory, University of Chicago, "Loss Distribution by Comparative Negligence," Minnesota Law R., December, 1936. 

78 Holohan v. McGrew, 111 Cal. App. 443, 295 Pac. 1059; Shea v. San Bernardino, 7 

Cal. 2d 688, 62 Pac. 2d 365; Hallinan v. Prindle, 220 Cal. 46, 29 Pac. 2d 202;
