Appendix A.

at least, and at the same time preserve the principle that a wrongdoer must be held responsible for all damage to which he may have contributed. 

3. Insolvency and Joint and Several Judgments 

Still another problem is presented by the possible insolvency of one of two or more defendants. Professor Gregory statutes the problem thus, in connection with his hypothetical case involving T, A and B in a three cornered collision in which T alone suffers damage (see supra): "An additional matter should be noted in passing, concerning the possibility that the joint judgment is unsuitable under a system of this sort unless it appears that A in no event, if he is called upon in the above case to pay a judgment for two-thirds of T's damages, will be saddled with more of the loss than T himself eventually suffers. In other words A must not be made to bear more risk of B's insolvency in contribution proceedings than does T himself. Unless some allowance is made for B's insolvency in such a case, it would be far better to junk the joint judgment and give T two judgments against A and B, both executable, each for one-third of the damages, with the understanding that if, say, A pays all his and B does not, A will, by further payment to T, share equally with T as to B's inability to pay, so that T and A, having been equally at fault, will bear equal parts of T's loss. "This matter of allocating fairly the risk of insolvency is extremely important ***"82 And in connection with the same problem, he also says: "One practical change that should certainly be considered, however, is abolition of the joint and several judgment. This has already been referred to; but it is worth stressing the fact that where all parties to a law suit are negligent, there is little reason for retaining a principle of practice like the joint judgment and joint and several liability, which was developed in a system where the plaintiff, to recover, had to be absolutely free from negligence. If this device is retained, extremely difficult problems of shifting the risk of insolvency and of placing the initiative on issuing execution are bound to arise. For why should a negligent plaintiff be permitted to place on one of two negligent defendants the burden of loss allocated to both such defendants collectively, when the judgment is for considerable more than half of the plaintiff's actual damage and the luckless judgment debtor executed against was found to have been no more negligent than, if, indeed, as negligent as the plaintiff." 83 No one of the statutes heretofore adopted seems to offer any solution to this problem. Under the Wisconsin statute, for instance, Mr. Hayes states: "At this point I might suggest that our statute, instead of being strictly comparative, really provides only for reduction of damages measured by plaintiff's percentage of fault. If plaintiff is 20 percent negligent and each of two defendants is 40 percent negligent, plaintiff recovers 80 percent of his damages from the two defendants, but, if one of the defendants cannot pay, plaintiff may recover 80 percent of his damages from the defendant who may be financially responsible. 

Here a financially sound defendant must pay for the negligence of financial shortcomings of his co-defendant.” 84 

4. Guest Statute 

The problem of adoption of a "comparative negligence" statute in California is further complicated by the existence of the so-called "guest" statute 85 which limits the right of recovery by a guest for personal injuries sustained, or for the death of such a guest, to cases where the injury or death proximately results from the intoxication or wilful misconduct of the driver, and a similar provision relating to gratuitous passengers in aircraft.86 

82 Charles O. Gregory, University of Chicago, "Loss Distribution by Comparative 

Negligence," Minnesota Law R., December, 1936. 

83 Idem. 

84 Gerald P. Hayes, President Wisconsin Bar Association, "The Practical Working of a Comparative Negligence Statute," an address delivered before the New York 

State Bar Association, January 26, 1950. 

85 Vehicle Code § 430, formerly Vehicle Code §1412. 

86 State Aeronautics Act, Stats. 1947, Ch. 1379, as amended by Stats. 1949, Chs. 653, 835, § 15. (Deering General Laws, Act 151a.)

Wisconsin has no such statute, and has thus escaped the problems involved in coordinating its provisions with those of a "comparative negligence" statute. In that state a gratuitous guest is considered to have assumed any risk involved in any latent defect of the vehicle, as well as the known habits, skill, and condition of the driver, and if the injury occurs through such a hazard, there is no recovery. Assumption of risk is differentiated from contributory negligence and where assumption of risk is present, the comparative negligence statute has no application.87 

Nebraska, on the other hand, which has both a "guest" law and a comparative negligence statute, finds that contributory negligence on the part of a guest is available to the driver of an automobile as a defense, in spite of the statute limiting liability of such driver to cases where he is "under the influence of intoxicating liquor" or guilty of "gross negligence," and considers that the comparative negligence statute of that state, with its "slight-gross" negligence tests, applies to actions involving host and guest.88

Mississippi has no "guest"statute, and, accordingly, no such problem as that facing the California Legislature. Ontario, on the other hand, has such a statute, but one which deprives the guest of any right of action against his host or his host's servant or agent or the driver of the car.89 The problem which results is similar to that which arises in Wisconsin where the plaintiff's negligence is equal to or greater than that of one defendant, and accordingly one element of the total negligence is not considered in arriving at the final result. Ontario has solved the problem by providing that in such instances no damages nor contribution nor indemnity shall be recoverable for the portion of the loss or damage caused by the fault or negligence of the owner or driver, but such portion of the loss or damage is nevertheless to be determined.⁹⁰ 

California's problem in this connection differs from that of any jurisdiction having a comparative negligence statute, and the effect of the adoption of the "comparative negligence" doctrine in any form should be carefully considered, particularly in view of the holding of the California court to the effect that ordinarily the negligence of the plaintiff guest is not available as a defense to charges of wilful misconduct, but may become so only where the act of the plaintiff is such that it is part of or an inducing cause of the defendant's misconduct. Suppose, for example, a collision between a car driven by H (the host) and one driven by A, in which G (a guest in H's car) is injured. The findings are that G is responsible for 15 percent of the negligence, H for 50 percent, and A for 35 percent. Under a statute like that of Wisconsin, should 

G be permitted to recover 85 percent of his damages from A, since H's negligence may not serve as the basis of any recovery under California's "guest" statute? Or, under the principle adopted by Mississippi and the Canadian provinces, what is G's recovery against A? Must A assume the entire responsibility for his own negligence and for that of H? And if G's negligence is not available as a defense to H, is it nevertheless available to A? 

5. Pleading and Practice 

It would seem obvious that an additional problem involved in the adoption of a "comparative negligence" statute would be the guarantee that all claims arising out of the same accident might be determined simultaneously. As stated by Professor 

Gregory, the problem is this: "It cannot be too plainly and frequently stated, however, that any jurisdiction desiring a system of loss distribution * * * must provide facilities of procedure and practice permitting all of the claims arising out of a single fact transaction or accident to be filed and litigated in the same action. Otherwise there can be absolutely no assurance that the loss distribution which would ultimately take place after a series of law suits concerning these claims, involving the judgments of different juries with respect to the same evidence and the same testimony from the same witnesses concerning the same fact transactions and parties, will be uniform and consistent." 92 

87 Hayes, supra note 84. 

88 Landrum v. Roddy, 12 N. W. 2d 82, 149 A. L. R. 1041. 

89 Walter F. Schroeder, Justice Ontario Supreme Court, "The Courts and Comparative 

Negligence," American Bar Association, Section on Insurance Law, 1950. 

90 Revised Statutes of Ontario, 1937, Ch. 115, as amended, § 2 (2). For full text see

Appendix A. 

91 Amidson v. Hebert, 93 Cal. App. 2d 225, 208 Pac. 2d 733. 

92 Charles O. Gregory, University of Chicago, "Loss Distribution by Comparative Negligence," Minnesota Law R., December, 1936.

2 ar 

As the answer Professor Gregory suggests that: "Provisions must be made for counterclaims between plaintiffs and defendants, for the adding of third party defendants in order for the sued defendants to claim damages or contribution against them and also for such third parties or already sued codefendants to cross-claim against other defendants for damages or contribution, and for outside parties to come in and make themselves parties to existing litigation in order to file claims and to be themselves subject to claims. The only limitation on such provisions should be the requirement that all the claims involved must have arisen out of the same accident or fact transaction giving rise to the original action."93

California, as a leading code pleading state, does have provision in its statutes for cross-complaints and counterclaims, but such existing provisions should be carefully integrated with any comparative negligence statute. 

6. Special Verdicts 

There seems to be general agreement that special rather than general verdicts by juries are a necessary part of any comparative negligence statute, no matter what its other provisions may be. Professor Gregory explains the problem in these words: 

66* * * when cases are tried before juries, it has been found practicable *** to require special verdicts or findings. The confusion which would result from any other course seems apparent when one considers the number of interdependent claims that have to be settled in a single action such as one of those illustrated above in this article. The nature of the findings themselves is very simple, being first to determine which parties were negligent, and in what proportions with respect to claims contributed to by the negligence of more than one party. They the jury must find whose negligence contributed to whose damages, and then it must find what the actual damage in issue amounted to with respect to each party claiming. On these findings, it is submitted, the trial court can always enter a judgment satisfactorily allocating the loss to the various parties, although it seems apparent that if the jury were to render a general verdict covering all the claims in issue, confusion would result unless the case were extremely simple, involving only one or two claims. Even then, if general verdicts were given, the court would have no way to check upon the accuracy of the deductions the jury made from the preliminary findings the jury must have arrived at for its own convenience." º¹

Another authority on the matter of "comparative negligence" has stated it in these words: "The system works best if it is handled with a special verdict, as in Wisconsin. 

That is, the jury finds the amount of damages of each party, the damages to which each has contributed, and the percentage of over-all fault attributed to each party. 

The judge then does the arithmetic. This appears to be necessary because some cases can get pretty complicated, particularly where there are counterclaims or multiple plaintiffs or defendants. In fact, I should say that the special verdict would be almost indispensable to a satisfactory system." " 99.95

Wisconsin generally uses such a special verdict, but in spite of the provision of the statute requiring that damages shall be diminished by the jury in proportion to the amount of negligence attributable to the person recovering, the courts do not permit the jury, itself, to diminish the damages. The jury is required to find separately, first, the damage of the party or parties involved, and second, the percentage of causal negligence attributable to the plaintiff and the percentage of the causal negligence attributable to the defendant, in a case involving two parties only, and the same practice is followed where there are more than two parties, such as two or three defendants.96 

In the event of an accident involving more than two parties, such special verdict may require a series of findings by the jury, as for instance that of the total causal negligence, A was responsible for 25 percent, B for 25 percent, and C for 50 percent; 

03 Idem. 

91 Charles O. Gregory, University of Chicago, "Loss Distribution by Comparative Negligence," Minnesota Law R., December, 1936. 

95 William A. Prosser, Dean of the School of Jurisprudence, University of California, in report submitted to State Bar Conference Committee on Comparative Negligence, 1950. 

90 Gerald P. Hayes, President Wisconsin Bar Association, "The Practical Working of a Comparative Negligence Statute," an address delivered before the New York 

State Bar Association, January 26, 1950.

that as to the combined negligence of A and B, 40 percent should be attributed to A and 60 percent to B; and that as to the combined negligence of B and C, 75 percent should be attributed to B, and 25 percent to C.97 

In connection with the Ontario statute, typical of those in force in the Canadian jurisdictions, special verdicts are also used. Justice Schroeder explains the importance of such verdicts in these terms: “*** If it was important to take a special verdict from a jury before the enactment of the amending negligence legislation, a fortiorari it was even more so after the coming into existence of the altered law, having particular regard to the necessity of determining whether or not there was a true case of comparative negligence established by the evidence. The taking of a special verdict removes the danger of speculative verdicts being given and affords to the courts a measure of control which they ought to possess and ought to exercise in order to insure a proper administration of justice in the field of negligence law. "While the taking of a special or general verdict is left to the discretion of the trial judge, I am not aware of any negligence case—at least within the last quarter of a century-in which a general verdict has been taken *** "Perhaps I can illustrate what I mean by a measure of control which we deem it desirable to retain in a jury trial by giving you a typical example of the questions which are submitted to a jury in a negligence case. They run somewhat 

"1. Was there any negligence on the part of the plaintiff which caused or contributed to the cause of the accident? Answer 'Yes' or 'No.' "2. If your answer to question No. 1 is 'Yes,' then state fully and clearly in what such negligence consisted. "3. Was there any negligence on the part of the defendant which caused or contributed to the cause of the accident? Answer 'Yes' or 'No.' "4. If your answer to question No. 3 is 'Yes,' then state fully and clearly in what such negligence consisted. "5. If your answer to question No. 1 is 'Yes' and your answer to question No. 3 is 'Yes,' do you find it practicable to determine the respective degrees of fault or negligence on the part of the plaintiff and the defendant? Answer 'Yes' or 'No.' "6. If your answer to question No. 5 is 'Yes,' then state in percentages the degrees of fault or negligence on the part of the plaintiff and the defendant- (a) the plaintiff (b) the defendant _% -% "7. In any event and irrespective of your answers to the foregoing questions, at what amount do you assess the total damages— (a) of the plaintiff__. (b) of the defendant. $. "In charging the jury with respect to questions 2 and 4, the presiding judge tells them that they must give specific answers from which it will appear clearly just why they say the plaintiff or the defendant was negligent. They are warned against bringing such vague and indefinite answers as 'He should have been more cautious' or 'He should have used greater care.' They are told that any such answer is meaningless and that their answer must be such that the court examining it will have no doubt as to what was the precise ground of negligence which they found against either or both parties. They are also warned to state all the grounds of negligence which they may find, because their silence as to other grounds of negligence alleged will be interpreted as a negativing of such allegations. If a jury brings in a vague and meaningless answer such as I have alluded to, we do not hesitate to send them back and ask them to clarify their answer by making it more specific so as to enable the court to determine just what they meant to say in answer to questions 2 and 4. This has many advantages, chief of which is that the court is enabled to say whether or not the particular ground of negligence found against a party is negligence in law. If a general verdict is taken such an inquiry may be seriously circumvented. Then, too, if the case is carried to the Court of Appeals, the judges of that forum with the record before them, can determine whether or not there is any evidence to support the particular finding made by the jury. Of course, a judge must tell the jury that questions 2 

97 Kirchen v. Tisler et al., 255 Wis. 208, 38 N. W. 2d 514.

N 7. and 4 are not concerned with negligence in the abstract, but with acts of negligence which were a real effective cause of the accident and this is explained to the jury by giving them illustrations which they can easily understand." 98 

Obviously, question 5 is one which would not be usable under a statute such as that of Wisconsin, where the finding must be that the plaintiff's negligence is less than that of the defendant before recovery will be allowed. It flows from the provision in the 

Ontario statute to the effect that if it is not practicable to determine the degrees of fault as between the parties, they shall be deemed equally responsible." 

Mississippi does not permit the use of special verdicts, with results described in the following language by one of the practitioners of that state: "Another difficulty in the practical administration of the statute in Mississippi is that we do not have special verdicts, but general lump-sum verdicts only. There is no way in which it can be known, for instance, that the jury found that the plaintiff had been damaged $1,000, that the plaintiff was guilty of 25 percent of the negligence, and that the jury awarded $750 for this reason. Thus, the appellate court has a most difficult time in dealing with pleas of excessiveness or inadequacy. "An automobile damage suit is usually a swearing contest. The appellate court has no way of knowing whether the jury believed or disbelieved the testimony offered to establish that the plaintiff was guilty of negligence. If the verdict seems to be unusually large, the court may assume that the jury did not believe that the plaintiff was guilty of any contributory negligence where that fact is in dispute when, as a matter of fact, the jury might not have considered the comparative negligence statute at all in arriving at the verdict. We have not had much experience with special verdicts, because there has never been any provision for them in this state, but in our humble opinion a provision for special verdicts should go along with a comparative negligence statute as one of the Siamese twins goes along with the other. "General lump-sum verdicts are not conducive to a just, fair application of a comparative negligence statute. Jurors are too prone to generalize and to arrive at their verdict on the basis of general impressions as to whether the plaintiff should recover, and, if so, how much. Specific inquiries propounded to them have the tendency, we are sure, to direct their attention to the importance of comparing the negligence of the plaintiff with that of the defendant and the mathematical mechanics of applying the statute to the facts." 100 

7. Doctrine of "Last Clear Chance" or Ultimate Negligence 

A further problem is presented in California by the existence in the law of the State of the doctrine here called "last clear chance," and known in other jurisdictions as the "humanitarian doctrine" or ultimate negligence. Those advocates of the "comparative negligence" doctrine who consider the doctrine of "last clear chance" to be one which is merely a device of the courts to avoid the so-called harshness of the contributory negligence rule, insist that it should be abandoned with the adoption of a comparative negligence statute. For instance, Professor Gregory says: "The doctrine of ultimate negligence *** has become entrenched in the Canadian jurisdictions having comparative negligence statutes, and persists in spite of the fact that after the adoption of such statutes its function had entirely ceased. For it seems very clear that the common law doctrine was initiated merely to overcome the harshness of the strict common law rule of contributory negligence." 101

Justice Schroeder, on the other hand, indicates that the doctrine is primarily one of causation, and not only has survived in Ontario law, but in his opinion has properly survived the adoption of a "comparative negligence" statute there. He says: "One may be moved to ask what has become of the old doctrine of 'last chance' or 'last opportunity' in the law of negligence in those jurisdictions which have adopted the comparative negligence theory. In a recent case the Ontario Court of Appeal, namely Broderick et al. v. Toronto Transportation Commission et al., 98 Walter F. Schroeder, Justice Ontario Supreme Court, "The Courts and Comparative 

Negligence," American Bar Association, Section on Insurance Law, 1950. 

09 Revised Statutes of Ontario, 1937, Ch. 115 as amended, § 4. See Appendix A for full text. 

100 Hubert S. Lipscomb, "Comparative Negligence," Insurance Law Journal, September, 1951. 

101 Charles O. Gregory, University of Chicago, "Loss Distribution by Comparative 

Negligence," Minnesota Law R., December, 1936.

1949 C. L. R. 658, Laidlaw, J. A.

, writing the judgment of the court, stated at p. 663: 'It has been repeatedly pointed out that there is no doctrine of "last chance" or "last opportunity" in the law of negligence of this province.' "But he goes on to say: 'The real question to be decided is: What was the cause or what were the causes of the damage? In answering that question, it is essential to consider the facts of each particular case and the findings as made on the evidence.' "Ultimate negligence is still a part of our law insofar as it touches or concerns the question of causation. We may hesitate to submit specific questions to the jury directed to eliciting their view as to the existence or nonexistence of ultimate negligence on the ground that such questions tend to create confusion in their minds, but that is not a ground for the broad declaration that the doctrine of ultimate negligence has not survived the negligence acts. As long as we retain the common law concept of causation as part of our law, that doctrine must be resorted to when the proper occasion arises. It is to be noted that in his reasons for judgment in Broderick v. Toronto Transportation Commission, Laidlaw, J. A., states, after referring to the two findings of the jury, namely: '(A) Did not apply brakes at time of first sounding warning whistle. (B) In our opinion he had sufficient time to stop streetcar before impact.' 'It is abundantly clear from that finding, read as a whole, as it should be, that the jury reached the conclusion that the operator of the streetcar saw the dangerous state of affairs created by the truck standing on the crossing, and negligently failed to avoid the collision when with reasonable conduct on his part, it could have been avoided. The principle in Davis v. Mann (1842), 10 M. & W. 546, 152 E. R. 588 is plainly applicable to the situation.' "' 102

As indicated above, the British Law Reform Committee, in recommending adoption of a "comparative negligence" statute for England, reached the same conclusion, namely that the doctrine of ultimate negligence is part of the problem of causation, and should remain in force, even with the adoption of a "comparative negligence" statute.103 

8. Other Problems 

There are additional problems to which attention should perhaps be given. California, for instance, recognizes the doctrine of "assumption of risk” as a defense in some actions based on negligence.104 With the adoption of a "comparative negligence" statute, should "assumption of risk" be treated in the same manner as is mere contributory negligence on the part of a plaintiff or differently. Professor Gregory poses the problem in these words: "There is a difference in kind between consciously confronting a dangerous situation with complete appreciation of the hazard involved, and carelessly but inadvertently exposing one's self to the same situation. A legislature might be justified in concluding that one guilty of the former type of conduct should not recover any damages on the ground that the negligent defendant did not contribute at all to the plaintiff's damage, but rather that it was entirely the plaintiff's fault for deliberately stepping into the path of danger. * * *" 105

As noted above, Wisconsin, in connection with its "guest" problem, concludes that where there is voluntary assumption of risk, the "comparative negligence" provisions have no application.

An additional problem is presented where there are joint tortfeasors, if it is determined that a right of contribution between them should be allowed in connection with the adoption of a "comparative negligence" statute, namely what provision is to be 

102 Walter F. Schroeder, Justice Ontario Supreme Court, "The Courts and Compara tive Negligence," American Bar Association, Section on Insurance Law, 1950. 

103 Law Revision Committee, Eighth Report (Contributory Negligence), in Reports from Commissioners, Inspectors and Others, 1938-39 (4), Great Britain, Parliament. 

104 See for instance Potts v. Crafts, 5 Cal. App. 2d 83, 42 Pac. 2d 87; Gornstein v. 

Priver, 64 Cal. App. 249, 221 Pac. 396; Ury v. Fredkin's Market, 26 Cal. App. 2d 

501, 79 Pac. 2d 749. 

105 Charles O. Gregory, University of Chicago, "Loss Distribution by Comparative 

Negligence," Minnesota Law R., December, 1936.

made to permit settlements. Justice Schroeder outlines the problem in Ontario and the solution which that province found as follows: "Very frequently practical difficulties arose where there were joint tortfeasors and where a plaintiff was disposed to make a reasonable settlement of the action. 

It often occurred that one of the joint tortfeasors was opposed to the making of a payment of any sum whatsoever to the plaintiff. Now by an amendment made in 1948, a defendant who takes advantage of an offer of settlement may thereafter commence or continue an action against the other tortfeasor and if he can satisfy the court that the amount of the settlement was reasonable, he can recover his proper proportion by way of indemnity, or should the court find that the settlement was excessive, it has power to fix the amount at which the claim should have been settled and may adjust the rights of the respective tortfeasors accordingly. Thus a defendant can now make what appears to him to be a reasonable or advantageous settlement without losing his rights against another defendant who was, or may have been, partly responsible for the claimant's damages or losses. This was a most salutary reform and one which struck at the root of a real practical difficulty which had beset the path of harassed insurance adjusters and insurance lawyers." 106 

Professor Gregory suggests still another problem, when he points to the fact that as highly developed a "comparative negligence" statute as that of Ontario, does not contain the provision found in the British Columbia statute, which provides in Section 2 (b) that "Nothing in this section shall operate so as to render any person liable for any loss or damage to which his fault has not contributed." 107 Is there a possibility that, without such a safeguard, a comparative negligence statute, at least of the type permitting recovery regardless of degree of fault, could subject someone to liability for damage to which he in no way contributed? 

There are also such problems as the manner in which breaches of criminal statutes, such as safety laws, are to be treated. It is impossible to anticipate all the problems which might arise in adding such a doctrine as "comparative negligence" to a body of existing law, but the above outline will serve to indicate the major ones. 

REPORTS OF STATE BAR COMMITTEES 

In connection with these problems, and the various choices which have to be made in connection with the adoption of a "comparative negligence" statute, it is interesting to note that four Conference Committees of the State Bar of California have made recommendations, all different.

The 1941 committee, composed of seven members, was sharply divided, two members recommending the adoption of the Ontario doctrine, permitting recovery regardless of degree of negligence, but with diminution of damages, coupled with a right of contribution between joint tortfeasors. Two members of the committee wished no part of any “comparative negligence" statute. One expressed doubt as to the feasibility of any such statute and suggested instead an insurance scheme to take care of "loss distribution." A sixth member of the committee pointed out both the legal and social ramifications of the adoption of the doctrine, and suggested that far greater study was necessary before any recommendation should be made. 

The 1950 committee was apparently in agreement that a comparative negligence statute of some sort should be adopted in California, but wished additional time before making recommendations as to the form the statute should take, and offered solutions to none of the problems outlined above. 

The 1951 committee recommended a statute of the type in force in Wisconsin, permitting recovery only where the negligence of the plaintiff was less than that of the defendant, coupled with provisions for contribution between joint tortfeasors. 

The 1952 committee recommended adoption of a statute permitting recovery regardless of degrees of negligence, but diminishing damages in proportion to the negligence of the plaintiff, and postponement of any decision on the matter of contribution between joint tortfeasors for some future date. Both the 1951 and 1952 committees recommended the abolition of the doctrine of "last clear chance," and the adoption of special verdicts of a simple form requiring findings as to: (1) the loss in money or damages sustained by each claimant; (2) whose negligence contributed to the damages sustained by each claimant; and (3) the proportional fault of each party, expressed in percentages, leaving the calculation of damages to be allowed, if any, to the court. 

106 Walter F. Schroeder, Justice Ontario Supreme Court, "The Courts and Compara tive Negligence," American Bar Association, Section on Insurance Law, 1950. 

107 Charles O. Gregory, University of Chicago, "Loss Distribution by Comparative 

Negligence," Minnesota Law R., December, 1936.

No one of the committees apparently gave detailed consideration to the other problems involved in the adoption of a "comparative negligence" statute, although there are indications that a substantial number of members of the various committees believed that California's existing law is sufficient to meet the requirement that all parties may be brought into a single action. Matters of joint and several judgments, insolvency of one tortfeasor, reconciliation of a "comparative negligence" statute with the "guest" law, and other problems are not mentioned in any save the 1941 report.

ARGUMENT BY ANALOGY TO OTHER STATUTES 

Proponents of the doctrine of "comparative negligence" point to various specialized areas in which the theory operates satisfactorily. Instances most often cited are admiralty, the Federal Employers' Liability Act, the Jones Act, state employers' liability statutes, workmen's compensation statutes, and automobile "guest" statutes. 

While it may be true that the operation of some form of "comparative negligence" under such laws may indicate the practicability of apportionment of damages and may even stand as proof of the wisdom of such provision under certain circumstances, the compelling reasons for the adoption of the rule under such circumstances should be examined before their mere existence is accepted as a reason for extending the "comparative negligence" doctrine to the general field of negligence actions. 

The rule permitting apportionment of damages in admiralty has been in existence for many years, and there seems to be no general agreement as to the original reason for the rule.108 The doctrine is applied differently in the United States and in various other countries which have adopted the Maritime Conventions of 1911. In the United 

States the rule is that where both vessels are at fault, damages are divided equally, with no attempt to apportion in accordance with degrees of fault.109 In England, since the Maritime Conventions Act of 1911,110 damages have been apportioned in accordance with degrees of fault of the parties. The rule, in one form or another, may be valid in admiralty, but it must be remembered that there are many additional doctrines in admiralty with which it is of necessity coupled, and factors which are not present in ordinary negligence actions, such as the ability to limit liability to the value of the vessel, the difficulty of securing jurisdiction over the owner of a vessel, other jurisdictional problems where ships of different flags are involved. The fact that such a rule may be appropriate, when coupled with the specialized problems of admiralty law, is not necessarily a reason to adopt the same rule in the field of negligence actions generally.

The very special background of the Federal Employer's Liability Act and similar state statutes governing the relationships between common carriers and their employees, would seem to indicate that Congress and the state legislatures involved had a very definite social purpose in mind in adopting those statutes. Under the common law of England, suits by a servant against his master or employer for injuries incurred in the course of employment were based on the negligence of the master. Obviously, the contributory negligence of the employee was a defense, and in addition there developed in the common law the additional defenses of the "fellow servant rule" and the doctrine of "assumption of risk." Under the first of these defenses the master or employer could escape any liability for injury to an employee where the injury could be attributed to the negligence of a fellow servant, and under the second defense, the employee was not permitted to recover if it could be established that he assumed the risk by accepting employment on premises he knew to be unsafe or under other dangerous conditions. These defenses at common law developed at a period when the rapid mechanization of industry rendered the likelihood of injury to workmen and the necessity for protection far greater than before. Public reaction against the injustices of these common law defenses led to the enactment in 1880 of the English Employers' Liability Act, which was the initial step toward workmen's compensation in England and in this Country. Between 1880 and 1902 five American states had adopted some such legislation and by 1910 practically all states had some form of employer's liability laws.111 

The first Federal Employers' Liability Act was adopted by Congress in 1906,¹¹2 and the present statute was enacted in 1908.118 It establishes the liability of interstate 

108 Marsden, "Collisions at Sea." 

109 A. Chalmers Mole and Lyman P. Wilson, "Comparative Negligence," Cornell Law 

Q., April 1932, June, 1932, and cases there collected. 

110 I & II, Geo. V, Ch. 57. 

111 Hanna, "Industrial Accident Commission Practice and Procedure." 

112 34 Stats. at L. 252. 

118 35 Stats. at L. 65, now 45 U. S. C. §§ 51-60.

railroad carriers for the death of or injury to their employees and although it preserves negligence on the part of the employer as the basis of the action, it provides that contributory negligence on the part of the employee shall not bar his recovery, but that damages shall be diminished by the jury in proportion to the amount of negligence attributable to the employee.114 The statute also limits the defense of assumption of risk by the employee, providing that the employee shall not be held to have assumed the risks of his employment in any case where such injury or death resulted in whole or in part from the negligence of any of the officers, agents, or employees of the carrier.115 

The defenses of contributory negligence and assumption of risk are abolished entirely in cases where the carrier is found to have violated a statute enacted for the safety of employees and such violation contributed to the injury or death of the employee.11 

The Jones Act 17 extends the provisions of the Federal Employers' Liability Act to seamen, permitting them to maintain actions for damages at law against their employers, and making applicable to such actions all statutes of the United States conferring or regulating the right of action for death or injury in the case of railway employees.

The distinction between the policy embodied in these federal statutes, and in the remaining state statutes of similar nature, and a general “comparative negligence" statute, is pointed out by Judge William Palmer of the Los Angeles bench, in the following language: "Giving the employee the benefit of the rule of comparative negligence in cases embraced within the Federal Employers' Liability Act is an expression of that protective and paternal policy and attitude that now pervades our entire jurisprudence in behalf of employees in their relations to employers, a policy founded on the theory that, as between the two, the employee is the weaker, is more subject to the stress of need, more dependent generally, and hence more in need of the law's solicitude. "In such actions the one claim that is presented to the court for consideration is that of the employee or, if he died of his injuries, of his next of kin. I never have known of the employer filing a cross-demand against the employee in such a case. This federal law approaches insurance for the safety of the employee while engaged in his employment, but as the employee pays nothing for the 'policy' he is docked proportionately for his own negligence. The law is designed for and is limited in its application not only to a specific relationship, but to a selected group of risks, among whom a tendency is always at work to eliminate the poorer risks. Such a law obviously is not a valid precedent for a universal application of its rule of comparative negligence. "When John Plaintiff and George Defendant come before us in the typical automobile accident case, they come as equals; they bear no relationship that gives rise to any cause for special solicitude or legal advantage or paternalism in favor of either party as against the other. John Plaintiff's claim often is countered with a cross-claim by George Defendant." 118

Advocates of the doctrine of "comparative negligence" point also to the workmen's compensation statutes, almost universal throughout this Country today, as an example of the modification of common law rules which should be extended to negligence generally.110 California's workmen's compensation statute, like most of the others, places absolute responsibility on the employer to pay compensation when the injury arises out of and in the course of employment, regardless of his negligence.120 Obviously in such circumstances the defense of contributory negligence, and its common law companions, the "fellow servant rule" or "assumption of risk" have no further place. But the social and economic philosophy which led California and most other jurisdictions to adopt such a statute certainly does not seem to lead to the assumption that the same principle should be applied generally. In the first place workmen's compensation is not "comparative negligence" in any sense, but the imposition of liability without 

114 45 U. S. C. § 53. 

115 45 U. S. C. § 54. 

116 45 U. S. C. §§ 53, 54. 

117 46 U. S. C. § 688. 

118 Superior Judge William J. Palmer, "Let Us Be Frank About Comparative Negli gence," Los Angeles Bar Bulletin, November, 1952, reprinted in The Los Angeles 

Daily Journal, November 4, 1952. 

119 See for instance, A. Chalmers Mole and Lyman P. Wilson, "Comparative Negligence," Cornell Law Q., April, 1932, June, 1932. 

120 Labor Code § 3600.

fault, for social and economic reasons. Those reasons are summed up by a leading authority in the field of workmen's compensation in California, in these words: "Social Basis of Workmen's Compensation. The ancient common law idea that a man should be responsible for injuries caused by his own fault is still the basis of our general liability law, and the soundness of such an idea immediately appeals to one's sense of natural justice. In contrast thereto, the legal theory of workmen's compensation that an employer should be responsible for injuries to his employees, irrespective of fault, is a somewhat startling concept. While various ideas have been advanced in justification of such a theory, it would seem in the last analysis to stem primarily from considerations of public policy. The amelioration of social conditions is undoubtedly a legitimate function of the state; and when it finds that distress and economic insecurity are being thrust upon numerous victims of industrial casualties by reason of the existence of an inadequate system of legal remedies, the state may conceive it to be its proper function to take such steps as may be necessary to correct this situation. Such an objective may well constitute sufficient justification for abandonment of the long established principle of liability based upon fault in favor of the principle of liability irrespective of fault, where it appears that the latter policy will produce the greatest good for the greatest number of people. 

"Economic Philosophy of Workmen's Compensation. From an economic stand point, the basic philosophy of workmen's compensation is that the risk of injury and the financial burden resulting therefrom should be borne by industry as a whole rather than fall solely upon the employee involved; that the burden of the wearing out and destruction of human, as well as inanimate machinery, should be borne by industry, just as other costs of production are assumed by the employer and ultimately passed along to the public. It is in effect a special tax and part of the operating expense, just as truly as any other cost of repair or operation. The industry as a whole is subject to the same hazard, and no unequal burden is placed upon a particular employer's establishment. Furthermore, placing such a responsibility upon industry, through a system which provides financial reward to the individual employer for a lowered accident rate, tends by the spur of competition to reduce accidents, thus furthering the general social policy." 121

Argument is also made for adoption of a "comparative negligence" statute by analogy to the adoption of statutes by various states limiting the liability of a host to a guest and obviating the general rules of negligence actions in that particular situation.122 There, however, the advocates of "comparative negligence" point to the fact that such statutes were adopted to meet a specific social evil, namely" *** the serious increase in the evils of vexatious litigation between host and guest, the great opportunities for collusion among the parties in such cases when an insurance company is in fact the real party in interest, the consequent fraud upon the court and the risk of manufactured claims, to say nothing of the basic injustice of permitting a bare invitee to sue his inviter for merely minor derelictions." 123 

CONCLUSION 

In conclusion it should be said that this report is presented not for the purpose or with the intention of arguing the merits or demerits of "comparative negligence" as a doctrine. It is submitted merely with the intention of pulling together in one place most of the published material which seems to throw some light on the doctrine and to give the committee fairly comprehensive information on existing statutes in other jurisdictions and the ways in which those statutes are considered by persons dealing with them to meet the standard of "justice" in the name of which the new doctrine is urged, and further to suggest some of the problems which would have to be considered in coordinating such a "comparative negligence" statute with existing California law. 

Respectfully submitted,

CALIFORNIA STATE CHAMBER OF COMMERCE AGRICULTURE AND INDUSTRY LEWELLYN T. MCMAHAN Legal Research Department

APPENDIX A THE NEGLIGENCE ACT OF ONTARIO 

(R. S. O. 1937, Ch. 115, as amended) 

1. Interpretation-In this Act- 

(a) "Action"-"Action" shall include counterclaim; (b) "Defendant"-"Defendant" shall include a plaintiff against whom a counterclaim is brought; (c) "Plaintiff"-"Plaintiff" shall include a defendant who counterclaims. (1930, c. 27, s. 2.) 

2. (1) Extent of Liability-Remedy Over. Where damages have been caused or con tributed to by the fault or neglect of two or more persons the court shall determine the degree in which each of such persons is at fault or negligent, and, except as provided by subsections 2 and 3, where two or more persons are found at fault or negligent, they shall be jointly and severally liable to the persons suffering loss or damage for such fault or negligence, but as between themselves, in the absence of any contract express or implied, each shall be liable to make contribution and indemnify each other in the degree in which they are respectively found to be at fault or negligent. (1930, c. 27, s. 3; 1931, c. 36, s. 2; 1935, c. 46, s. 2 (1).) (2) Where Plaintiff is Passenger. In any action brought for any loss or damage resulting from bodily injury to, or the death of any person being carried in, or upon, or entering, or getting on to or alighting from a motor vehicle other than a vehicle operated in the business of carrying passengers for compensation and the owner or driver of the motor vehicle which the injured or deceased person was being carried in, or upon or entering, or getting on to, or alighting from is one of the persons found to be at fault or negligent, no damages, contribution or indemnity shall be recoverable for the portion of the loss or damage caused by the fault or negligence of such owner or driver, and the portion of the loss or damage so caused by the fault or negligence of such owner or driver shall be determined although such owner or driver is not a party to the action. (3) Where Plaintiff is Spouse of Negligent Party. In any action founded upon fault or negligence and brought for loss or damage resulting from bodily injury to, or the death of any married person where one of the persons found to be at fault or negligent is the spouse of such married person, no damages, contribution or indemnity shall be recoverable for the portion of loss or damage caused by the fault or negligence of such spouse, and the portion of the loss or damage so caused by the fault or negligence of such spouse shall be determined although such spouse is not a party to the action. (1935, c. 46, s. 2 (2).) 

2a. Recovery as Between Tortfeasons. A tortfeasor may recover contribution or indemnity from any other tortfeasor who is, or would if sued have been, liable in respect of the damage to any person suffering damage as a result of a tort by settling with the person suffering such damage, and thereafter commencing or continuing action against such other tortfeasor, in which event the tortfeasor settling the damage shall satisfy the court that the amount of the settlement was reasonable, and in the event that the court finds the amount of the settlement was excessive it may fix the amount at which the claim should have been settled. (New, 1948, c. 61, s. 1.) 

3. Plaintiff Guilty of Contributory Negligence. In any action for damages which is founded upon the fault or negligence of the defendants if fault or negligence is found on the part of the plaintiff which contributed to the damages, the court shall apportion the damages in proportion to the degree of fault or negligence found against the parties respectively. (1930, c. 27, s. 4.) 

4. Where Parties to be Deemed Equally at Fault. If it is not practicable to determine the respective degree of fault or negligence as between any parties to an action, such parties shall be deemed to be equally at fault or negligent. (1930, c. 27, s. 5.) 

5. Adding Party Defendant. Whenever it appears that any person not already a party to an action is or may be wholly or partly responsible for the damages claimed, such person may be added as a party defendant, or may be made a third party to the action upon such terms as may be deemed just. (1930, c. 27, s. 6; 1939, c. 27, s. 23.) 

6. Jury to Determine Degrees of Negligence of Parties. In any action tried with a jury, the degrees of fault or negligence of the respective parties shall be a question of fact for the jury. (1930, c. 27, s. 7.) 

7. When Plaintiff May Be Liable for Costs. When the damages are occasioned by the fault or negligence of more than one party, the court shall have the power to direct that the plaintiff shall bear some portion of the costs if the circumstances render this just. (1930, c. 27, s. 8.) 

8. Limitation of Actions. Where an action is commenced against a tortfeasor or where a tortfeasor settled with a person who has suffered damages as a result of a tort, within the period of limitation prescribed for the commencement of actions by any relevant statute, no proceedings for contribution or indemnity against another tortfeasor shall be defeated by the operation of any statute limiting the time for the commencement of action against such other tortfeasors provided, (a) Such proceedings are commenced within one year of the date of the judgment in the action or the settlement, as the case may be ; and (b) There has been compliance with any statute requiring notice of claim against such tortfeasor. (New, 1948, c. 61, s. 3.)
